SOUTHWEST JEWRY 

Editor's Preface 

The biblical student will recall the saying: זכור ימות עולם, בינו שנות דור ודור". 

The 

To paraphrase it in English, it means: "Remember the days of old, understand the ages of generations past. .

When we stop to consider what Los Angeles Jewry was a hundred years ago, and what we are here today, we are reminded of another thought it was brought to us by our ancestors after the exdous from Egypt: בשבעים נפש ירדו אבותיך מצרימה, ועתה שמך השם אלוקיך ככוכבי השמים לרוב". "Thy anscestors descended into Egypt seventy souls, and behold, now, the 

Lord has made thee as the stars in heaven manifold."

This is just what one must think when he knows the remarkable history of southwest Jewry, In 1854 six Jews landed here.

One hundred years later we are being considered the second largest Jewish community in the United States. We are numbering four hundred thousand strong. כן ירבו !

From the Hebrew Benevolent to The Federation of Jewish Welfare, and from a single house of worshin. B'nai B'rith Temple, to hundreds of houses of worshin. from a Federation as a mother institution to the L. A. Tewish Community Council. from the "Benevolent" to the Jewish Welfare Fund. from a "Bikur 

Cholim" for the sick to a Cedars of Lebanon. from the Duarte Sanitarium, to the City of Hope, and the Mount Sinai Hospital. one is bound to see how true it was the saving: From seventy souls into exile in Egvnt. we rose to a multitude of six hundred thousand free people in the exodus.” as we see in our times. 

From six Jewish souls we are numbering now four hundred thousand. And since we were told that we are "to remember the days of old" and "to understand the ages of generations nast." what more can we do but to record our deeds of the past. to leave chronicles for the generations that will succeed us and take over our heritage to continue in our footsteps, perpetuate our institutions, our traditions. our historv. our lore. and strive even to make this world a better place to live in?

They speak here of a “Miracle Mile," nay, this is indeed a mirculous city. 

One who is well versed in the history of Los Angeles Jewry, has to admit that the good Lord has done wonders with us, and when a newcomer arrives here and settles, he should study our history, and when he learns of our achievement and progress, he is indeed bound to say: שהחינו !"

With deep humility and blessing, I feel privileged for the opportunity to record our deeds of the past and to leave records for the historian that will follow in years to come when I am gone, and he will continue where I am now closing these records as the heritage of my era. 

JOSEPH L. MALAMUT

Jewish Center, City of Hope, University of Judaism, Cedars of 

Lebanon, and the State of Israel.

5 

Jewish Pioneers of the Southland KASPARE COHN 

(Deceased) 

Born in Loebau, Prussia, June 14, 1839. Son of Abraham and Rachel Cohn. Married Hulda Newmark July 

17, 1872. Educated in the public schools of his native state until the age of fourteen.

RABBI ABRAHAM W. EDELMAN 

(Deceased)

Rabbi Abraham Wolff Edelman was born in the vicinity of Warsaw, Poland, in 1832. His father, David Edelman, was a prosperous merchant. His education was received at the schools of Warsaw and at the rabbinical school of that city. 

Moved to Los Angeles in 1859, where he clerked for Harris Newmark for a short period. Engaged in the crockery business in Red Bluff until the outbreak of the Civil War, when he returned to Los Angeles and entered the firm of H. Newmark & Co., until 1885, at which time he organized the firm of K. Cohn & Co., of which concern he was president. On July 1, 1914, he established the Kaspare Cohn 

Commercial & Savings Bank, of which institution he was president. He had always been a very charitable man and the Kaspare Cohn Hospital was named after him. 

He was president of the B'nai B'rith 

Congregation for many years. He was active in civic matters in his earlier career. He was extensively interested in hydro-electric plants and natural gas industry. 

In 1852 he married Miss Hannah 

Pesia Cohn, daughter of Rabbi Benjamin Cohn, and immediately after his marriage he came to the United 

States, his trip, of sailing vessel, consuming eleven weeks. Remaining but a short time in New York, he went to 

Paterson, N. J., but seeing no opening to his liking there, he moved to Buffalo, N. Y., subsequently moving to 

San Francihco, where he was a private teacher of Hebrew. Rabbi Henry of 

Emanuel Congregation took a great interest in this young man, who was an industrious student under his tutelage. In 1862 he came to Los Angeles, where there was a large settlement of Jews, and organized the B'nai B'rith Congregation, which was orthodox in all its forms and rituals. The 

Jewish community needed the inflexible purpose of ambitious youth and the teachings of this scholarly man to develop its resources. The wave of reform which was sweeping the Jewry

SOUTHWEST JEWRY 

of the American continent touched this man not at all. His Judaism was that of his fathers, and though in several very minor degrees reform affected his congregation, it speaks volumes for this good man's character that after twenty-five years of faithful service at the helm of the congregation he resgined rather than surrender his ideals and beliefs. Many sermons could be preached and epics written upon this momentous sacrifice, thus giving up of one's life work at an advanced age. Nothing can give one a clearer insight into Rabbi Edelman's soul. He retained his membership in the congregation, though the one attended by him was Beth Israel, whose services were conducted according to his beliefs.

After his retirement from the B'nai 

B'rith Congregation he devoted his whole time to charitable work. He was universally respected, and was a handsome, kind, capable and lovable man. His home life was ideal, the wife of his bosom the ideal Jewish wife, a great inspiration and a loving helpmate. Their evenings would find them together over a book, and her loss to him, in 1896, was a heavy blow.

His six children were carefully reared and splendidly educated. They are: 

Mrs. Matilda Jacoby, Mrs. W. T. Barnett, B. W. Edelman, A. M. Edelman, H. W. Edelman (residing in Brooklyn), and Dr. D. W. Edelman. 

On Yom Kippur and other holidays, after his retirement from the Congregation of B'nai B'rith, he would hold services in some public hall, accepting nothing for himself, and devoting whatever proceeds followed to some worthy charity. He was known as one of the best Talmudic scholars in the West. He was a fine public orator, his English pure, almost without an accent. Though he never took an active interest in politics, he always represented the Jews at all public gatherings. The subjects for discussion which he selected were always on religious topics. He was a great student, and his fine library, well selected and voluminous, was donated by him some time before his death to the Los Angeles Public Library, and it is now known as the A. W. Edelman

Library. 

Rabbi Edelman took an active interest in fraternal matters. He was past president of Los Angeles Lodge, I. O. 

B. B.; he was a past master of Lodge 

No. 42, F. & A. M., and upon retiring from the office of master of that lodge was presented with a silver service. 

He was also a member of the I. O. O. 

F. and A. O. U. W. Amongst the old 

Spanish and Mexican residents he was known as "Padre," and many the kindnesses and much the goodness they received from thih "gentle Jewish Padre." He spoke Spanish well, having mastered that language on his arrival in Los Angeles. He was extremely charitable, and though not by any means a rich man, he proved his great philanthropy by his will (and up to the time of his death there had never been a will made by a Jew in the Southwest which gave so much to charity). His wili, carried out by his children, remembered liberally not only all the Jewish charitable institutions, but held bequests for the 

Catholic and Protestant orphans' homes and other non-Jewish institutions.

ASHER HAMBURGER 

(Deceased) 

Asher Hamburger was born in the small village of Altschoenback, near 

Wurzburg, Bavaria, in 1821. After having received the rudiments of his education in the village school, he became a ropemaker's apprentice. As he grew up his sense of justice and his love of freedom became so strongg that he resolved to break the fetters that held him to the land of his birth and accordingly he and his brother, who was a weaver, started for the nearest seaport with only a few coppers in their pockets. They were bound for the United States.

In 1839, he, with his brother, arrived in the United States and set out

SOUTHWEST JEWRY 

7 to find employment. Asher immediateliy obtained work in a tassel factory in New York. After he had saved sufficient money he started a small general store in Pennsylvania. It was through his fair way of dealing that he gained the sobriquet of the "Honest Retailer."

It was in 1843 that he was joined by his other brother and the three of them went to Alabama and launched out in a bigger way. They built up a splendid business and soon owned three stores on the Tombigbee River.

When the news of the great gold discovery in California reached the 

South, Asher Hamburger was fired with enthusiasm and wished to start at once. His brothers, however, demurred, but Asher's indomitable will won the way, and in 1850 they started for the golden west via the Isthmus to seek their fortunes. 

Sacramento was their destination, but it did not prove the ideal spot for them, and in 1851 they moved to San 

Francisco and there started a wholesela house under the name of Hamburger Bros. Asher Hamburger was left in charge of the business in 

Sacramento, but fire and flood played havoc several times and destroyed the labor of years. His undaunted spirit stood him in good stead in these discouraging times, and in due course he was again on the road to the top. 

In 1881 his two sons, A. and M. A. 

Hamburger, who were then in business in Sacramento, concluded they wanted larger fields for growth, so they induced their father to go to 

Los Angeles. In November, 1881, the splendid business that became known as A. Hamburger & Sons, Inc., and the largest in Southern California, was inaugurated. D. A. Hamburger, the other son, joined them January 1, 1883. 

Asher Hamburger took an active interest in business affairs up to within a year of his death,which occurred on the 2nd day of December, 1897, he being in his sixty-seventh year. 

Through his liberality, enterprise and engrey he has contributed toward many of the improvements of Southern 

California. He had a most pleasing disposition and through his kindness and good heartedness assisted many men to positions of affluence in this country. 

In 1855 Asher Hamburger married 

Miss Hannah Bien, and they enjoyed fifty-two years of wedded life. Mrs.

Hamburger, who was an ardent active worker in Jewish charitable affairs, passed away on the 16th day of May, 1907. Seven children were born of them, six of whom survive

David A. Hamburger, Moses A. Ham burger, Belle Hamburger, Mrs. Otto 

Sweet, Mrs. Jennie H. Marx, and Miss 

Evelyn Hamburger. 1 

Hard, honest labor, upright methods, and taking for his precept the teachings of his early childhood, "Do ye unto others as ye would have them do unto you," these laid the foundation upon which his children built. 

LEOPOLD HARRIS 

(Deceased) 

Leopold Harris was born in West 

Prussia in 1836 and his death occurred in September, 1910. He had a very fair education with which to begin life in the business world, and this he received prior to his leaving his native land. Being a great believer in the power of education, he sought to avail himself of his spare time, and

SOUTHWEST JEWRY 

with much reading and study_developed into an exceedingly well-informed man. 

He left his European home a boy of sixteen and came to the United 

States. He settled in Louisville, Ky., where his brother resided. His destination, however, was the West, and from San Francisco, his first stopping place, he moved to San Bernardino, where he conducted a store. witz, and brought her back with him. to Los Angeles. To them the following children were born: Mrs. H. W. 

Frank, Mrs. M. C. Adler, Mrs. Alfred 

Stern and Alfred Harris. 

Mr. Harris was an officer of the

B'nai B'rith Congregation and an active member of various Jewish charity organizations. He was a man true to his word and always met his obligations, both as a family man and in the world of business. He left many staunch friends, and the community in which he spent the greater part of his useful life will ever bear the impress of his work and industry. In the early sixties he decided that 

Los Angeles offered better business opportunities, and consequently it was not long before the latter city numbered Leopold Harris among her citizens. He engaged in various lines of business before he settled into the one bearing the name of L. Harris & Co., wholesale and retail men's wear. This was in 1880. The business prospered and later the firm of Harris & Frank was formed, which continues at the present time.

Los Angeles owes much to Mr. Harrisb ecause of the fact that he helped build the city, in actuality, several blocks were built. He was one of the first men in Los Angeles to erect buildings on leased ground-that was as far back as 1880. At one time Mr.

Harris invested in land and sheep to a great extent, and it proved to be a good stroke of business. 

In 1869 Leopold Harris returned to

Germany and became wedded to the woman of his choice, Minna Jastro 

RABBI SIGMUND HECHT 

(Deceased) 

Born in Hungary, 1849. Son of 

Moritz Hecht, teacher and Talmudist. 

Married in 1875 in New York to

Liska Tuska, who was a niece of 

Rabbi Tuska of Memphis, Tenn., also a niece of Maurice Tuska of the Jew ish Orphane Asylum of New York. 

Children, Mrs. S. M. Newmark, Grace 

Hecht, Mrs. J. Y. Baruh and Victor Hecht.

Educated in Vienna at the Teachers' 

Seminary and at the University of

Vienna. Studied Hebrew and theology with the Rev. Dr. Jellinek of Vienna. 

Received degree of D.D. 1886, from the University of Alabama. In 1867

SOUTHWEST JEWRY 

9 moved to New York with his parents, continuing his studies in that city. 

Dr. Felix Adler and Dr. Gottheil of 

New York influenced him to pursue a rabbinical career.

In 1876 he moved to Montgomery, Ala., where he occupied the pulpit of the Jewish congregation until 1888, when he was called to Milwaukee,

Wis., where he held a similar position for twelve years, and was then called to the B'nai B'rith Congregation of Los Angeles, where he continued for some time. After his advent in Los 

Angeles his congregation increased many times its original size in membership, and in the outer world Dr. 

Hecht creditably represented the 

Jewish community. Dr. Hecht received much complimentary recognition from civic and religious bodies. 

He was at one time a member of the Los Angeles public library board and gave the first impetus to the organization of the Federation of Jewish Charities. He was the administrator of the funds of the Free Loan Society and treasurer of the Relief 

Fund for Jewish War Sufferers. Dr. 

Hecht was the author of a postbiblical history and wrote many essays on scholastic subjects. He was also one of the contributing editors of "Emanu-El."

HERMAN W. HELLMAN 

(Deceased)

The enterprises sustained by the financial aid and unerring business ability of Herman W. Hellman have given Los Angeles in the past few years a decided impetus toward a phenomenal growth and development. 

Fortunately wise and conservative, he held in check any movement which might have tended to inflate values, attract speculators and thus produce a condition disastrous to permanent development. Mr. Hellman's long association with the banking institutions of the Southern Metropolis he helped to build proved his peculiar fitness as a leader in financial circles.

Nearly fifty years have passed since this man went to his reward, and the wonderful results of his unending toil, his calm foresight and clear-headedness are seen on every hand as one makes a tour of the South, and it is hard, indeed, to believe this man who wrought nothing but great good to the city of his adoption is not among us today, so vividly does he live in our memories. 

Born September 25, 1843, in Bavaria, Germany, his early education was a practical training in the common branches of study. At the age of fifteen he decided to leave the shelter of the paternal roof and seek his fortune in a new country. Accordingly he took passage on a vessel bound for 

California. Los Angeles and its vicinity attracted him from the first, and his residence here was practically continuous. Commercial affairs interested him, and in the month of June, 1859, he took a position as freight clerk in the forwarding and commission business at Wilmington, conducted by General Phineas Banning. He held the position until he acquired sufficient means to enable him to return to Los Angeles and establish himself in the stationery business in partnership with a cousin.

After conducting a successful enterprise for several years he withdrew from the company to take up the work on his own responsibility. În 1870 he disposed of his business and spent the

SOUTHWEST JEWRY 

following year in Europe, visiting his boyhood home. In November of 1871 he entered into partnership with Jacob 

Haas, an old schoolmate of his, and established a wholesale grocery business, known as Hellman, Haas & Co. 

For nineteen years this firm catered to an extensive trade in Southern 

California, Arizona, New Mexico and 

Texas, the strong, forceful management of the men adding materially to the commercial supremacy of that section of the state.

In the meantime Mr. Hellman had become associated with the Farmers' 

National Bank, and when, in 1896, he became vice-president and local manager, he retired from the firm of Hellman, Haas & Co., and from that time he became one of the most widely known bankers of California.

Shortly after assuming his duties in the bank, the financial panic of 1893 brought disaster to many of the monetary institutions throughout the

United States. The security with which this bank stood out among others whose doors were closed either temporarily or permanently, and the long era of prosperity which followed that crisis, were largely due to the conservative and sagacious judgment of Mr. Hellman. 

Outside of his associations with the 

Farmers and Merchants' Bank, Mr. 

Hellman was extensively identified with other financial concerns of Los 

Angeles. In July, 1893, he accepted the presidency of the Merchants' National Bank, after resigning, in May of that year, as vice-president of the former institution. He was director of twelve other banks in Los Angeles and other cities in Southern California. In the business of all he brought to bear that energy and ambition which invariably spelled success. 

On the 26th day of July, 1874, Herman W. Hellman was married to Miss 

Ida Heimann, in Italy, and to them. were born the following children: 

Mrs. Freda Cole, Mrs. Sollie Aronson, Marco H. Hellman and Irving H. Hellman. 

Mr. Hellman belonged to many clubs and fraternal orders. Under his administration as president of the 

B'nai B'rith Congregation the new temple was erected. Many charitable institutions were given support by him. He was a member of the California, Jonathan, and Concordia clubs;

Masonic Order, Master of Pentalpha 

Lodge No. 202, Thirty-second Degree, Scottish Rite, Shriner, Al Malaikah

Temple. 

In reviewing the life of Mr. Hellman an impression is gained not of the opportunities which presented themselves throughout his career, but of the manner in which he understood and grasped each situation. Practically empty-handed he came to the Pacific Coast in his boyhood, when the country was lawless, when the survival of the fittest was the unwritten law, and where it was far easier to sink into insignificance with the multitude than to rise to heights with the few. 

On the 19th of October, 1906, his death occurred, and with his passing scores of men mourned. "Herman W. Hellman was a very rich man, but not an aristocrat, said one who knew him well. "He helped those in need. He was always approachable. He was loved by those who knew him. He was regarded by the younger members of his family as their counsellor and adviser. Mr. Hellman was respected by the community. 

Those who knew him always had a kind word for him."

Mr. Hellmna's interest in the growth of Los Angeles, the Herman W. Hell man Building, at Fourth and Spring streets, stands as a monument to his public spirit and enterprise. His everready assistance to charitable cases and his belief and interest in young men, all go to show that this man is missed not only by the members of his household and his near friends, but by the entire community as well.

SOUTHWEST JEWRY 

11 

HARRIS NEWMARK 

(Deceased) 

Not many names of Los Angeles merchants or financiers were better known nor more honorably associated with the history of that city's commercial development than that of 

Harris Newmark. Born in Loebau, West Prussia, on July 5, 1834, the son of Philip and Esther (Meyer) 

Newmark, Harris Newmark profited from early youth through an intimate association with a father whose natural enterprise and business operations enabled him to see a good deal of the world. 

Preceded by his brother and an uncle, Joseph, who had settled in 

Somerset, Connecticut, as early as 

1830, Harris Newmark came to America in 1853 by way of Gothenberg, Hull and Liverpool, arriving in New 

York in due time, and sailing again for San Francisco via Nicaragua, he arrived at the Golden Gate October 

16, 1853. On October 21, a mere youth of nineteen, Newmark reached 

San Pedro on the steamer "Goliah," where he was met by Phineas Banning, to whom he bore a letter of introduction, and who conducted him to 

Los Angeles. 

At first Harris clerked for his brother in a little store at the southcorner of Main and Requena streets, and when in June, 1854, J. 

P. Newmark sold out, Harris Newmark commenced business for himself. In a few months he organized the firm of Rich, Newmark & Co.

During 1856 this firm was dissolved, after which Mr. Newmark joined his uncle Joseph, who had come to California two years before, his brother and Maurice Kremer, and together they formed Newmark, Kremer & Co., a retail and wholesale business. In the fall of 1858 this business was dissolved. Harris Newmark continued to sell clothing. About the same time 

Mr. Newmark, who had already dealt somewhat in hides, began to invest in sheep. In 1861 he abandoned the clothing business, which was always distasteful to him, and became a commission broker. In 1865, hearing of a threat "to drive every Jew in Los 

Angeles out of business," Mr. Newmark speedily made a private agreement with Phineas Banning by which the cost of hauling merchandise from 

San Pedro was saved and a clear advantage over all competitors was thus assured, and straightway he established, in the Arcadia block, the wholesale grocery of H. Newmark &

Co., resulting eventually in a number of leading rivals, including the one who had made the boastful threat, retiring altogether from business. In 

1863 M. A. Newmark, a nephew, was encouraged to come to California.

Two years later Harris Newmark removed to New York, where he opened an office soon placed in charge of 

M. J. Newmark, later president of the Chamber of Commerce - returning to Los Angeles in 1868, when one of his partners there became ill. 

During the period of the great boom Harris Newmark joined another nephew, Kaspare Cohn, in creating the firm of K. Cohn & Co., hide and wool merchants, doing business on Main street, but this firm was dissolved in 1896, when Mr. Newmark continued to handle hides. In 1906 

Harris Newmark retired from business and devoted himself to the management of his estate. 

He assisted in the organization of both the Board of Trade and the

SOUTHWEST JEWRY 

Chamber of Commerce, and as a member of a committee from that body helped to exploit Los Angeles at the Philadelphia Exposition. With

Kaspare Cohn and other associates he bought the Repetto rancho, and there later laid out the towns of 

Newmark and Montebello. Newmark & Co. also owned the Santa Anita rancho, selling the same, after negotiations full of interesting details, to 

Lucky Baldwin. 

He was a member of Masonic Lodge 

No. 42, and one of the original organizers of the Los Angeles Public 

Library. He was for years president of the B'nai B'rith Congregation, and a member of the Archaeological Society of America. 

On March 24, 1858, Harrih Newmark was married to Miss Sarah, the second daughter of Joseph Newmark. 

Eleven children were born of this union. Surviving were three daughters, Mrs. L. Loeb, Mrs. J. Loeb, Mrs. 

Carl Seligman, and two sons, Maurice 

H. and Marco R. Newmark. On 

April 25, 1910, Mrs. Newmark died, beloved, as well as esteemed, by all who knew her. The site of the 

Southern California Hebrew Orphan

Asylum and its administration building commemorates her life and work. 

Mr. Newmark died April 4, 1916.

He was a noble man, who has put the imprint of his fine character upon his work, especially when the doing of charity and good were involved. He represented a splendid generation of Jewish thought and activity. Liberal 

Jewish thought and activity. Mr. 

Newmark's book, "Sixty Years in 

Southern California," edited by his sons, is a monumental work, being still used for references.

ISAAC NORTON 

(Deceased) 

Born in Warsaw, Poland, in 1844, a son of Moses Norton. Moved to New 

York at the age of five and was educated in the public schools of that city. 

He learned the jewelry trade after leaving school; subsequently clerked in a stock and bond office, and later engaged in the jobbing business. 

In 1869 he came to Los Angeles, where his brothers were already established in the mercantile business. 

In 1881 Mr. Isaac Norton established a retail merchandise store in a mining camp in California, which he continued successfully until 1886, when he returned to Los Angeles and entered the real estate and insurance business and was one of the organizers of the Metropolitan Building and Loan Association, of which concern he was secretary for a number of years. He also organized the Central Broadway Building Co., of which he was president. In 1875 he married Miss Berta Greenbaum (the first 

Jewish girl born in Los Angeles).

Their children are Samuel Tilden Norton, the architect, and Albert M. 

Norton, of the Norton Securities, and 

Mrs. M. B. Desenberg of Monrovia. 

Mr. Isaac Norton was the organizer of the Free Loan Society, for a number of years a trustee and director of the B'nai B'rith Congregation, president of the Hebrew Consumptive Relief Association, executive member of the Federation of Jewish Charities, president I. O. B. B. No. 487. He was also the organizer of the first lodge in Los Angeles, known as Orange 

Lodge. He was also a member of the 

Masonic Order.

SOUTHWEST JEWRY 

13 

Federation of Jewish Welfare 

Organizations by Steve Broidy, President

Organized under the name of Federation of Jewish Charities, incorporated in March, 1912. Originally embraced six constituent societies-The Hebrew Benevolent, Hebrew Consumptive, Ladies Hebrew Benevolent Jewish Orphans Home, Kaspare Cohn Hospital and the Temple Sewing Circle. 

The Hebrew Benevolent was the first Jewish relief organization, it was followed by the creation of the Ladies Hebrew Benevolent. Eventually they were merged in the Jewish Aid Organization and incorporated in 1918. 

In 1929 the name was changed to FEDERATION OF JEWISH WELFARE ORGANIZATIONS OF LOS ANGELES. 

The constituent societies then were: Hebrew Consumptive Relief Association, Jewish Aid Society, Jewish Alliance of Los Angeles, Jewish Orphans Home, Jewish 

Big Brothers, Jewish Loan and Housing Association, Jewish Committee for Person al Service in State Institutions, Jewish Mother's Alliance Day Nursery, Kaspare 

Cohn Hospital, Los Angeles Jewish Dispensary, Modern Social Center and Temple 

Sewing Circle.

We may begin to grasp the significance of Federation when we recall the herculean task which confronted this community beginning in 1933 with the coming into power of Hitler and again after the war, resulting in the influx of large numbers of refugees and displaced persons. Picture a situation in which every agency involved in their re-settlement, had operated on its own, without regard to the other agencies. The result would have been confusion, duplication, exorbitant costs and inefficiency. It was your Federation that stepped into the picture and, as the coordinating and planning arm of the Jewish community, brought together all the welfare organizations concerned with the problem, and worked out a plan of teamwork which resulted in the saving of community dollars and in a more effective and humane program. 

Federation is a shirt-sleeve organization. It works quietly. It is the social engineer, the community planner, comptroller-responsible for meeting the health, social welfare and recreation needs of the city. 

At this very moment Federation is working on how best to meet the urgent needs of the aged population. It has organized a city-wide council on the aged, which includes representatives from every organization, in or out of Federation, concerned with this problem. Meetings are being held; plans considered to meet the problem. It is the hope that out of these meetings will come a coordinated program which will prevent the mushrooming of aged homes and provide effective and humane services to this segment of our population. 

Another illustration of Federation's role is its concern with the long neglected area of the handicapped. Its social planning committee is hard at work on how best to meet this urgent need.

It also serves the community by providing member agencies with such services as: central auditing and bookkeeping, an over-all personnel policy, public relations and information, budgeting, research and surveys. 

Federation is a union of 22 leading health and social welfare agencies and committees. They serve in the field of family service, health, child and youth care, recreation, housing, economic adjustment and in public institutions. This past year they helped 109,000 men, women and children to find a better life. From the cradle to the grave-regardless of age or income, they stand ready to help every man in the hour of need. Federation is truly the heart of the Jewish community.

SOUTHWEST JEWRY STEVE BROIDY 

President, Federation of Jewish

Welfare Orgs. 

A long course of training in the motion picture industry has ideally fitted 

Steve Broidy for his position as presi dent of Allied Artists Pictures Cor poration.

It was in 1924 that Broidy abandoned the study of law at Boston University and joined the staff of the Franklin Company in Boston, covering western Massachusetts as a salesman.

Later, for a period of five years, he handled the same territory for Universal, and established an intimate acquaintanceship with the exhibitors of the area. Following this extended tour of duty, Warner Bros., taking advantage of Broidy's complete familiarity with the territory, engaged him for a similar assignment out of their 

Boston office, and he remained with them for more than three years. He had come to know western Massachusetts as well as he did his own back yard. 

Broidy's first connection with Monogram (which later became Allied Artists) came in 1933, when he joined the staff of the Boston office, enlarging his territorv to become a salesman onerating in Massachusetts and Rhode Island. Two vears later, when 

Monogram merged temporarily with

Republic, he continued with the new company in the same area. 

In 1937, when the new Monogram company was formed, Broidy, as head of the Boston exchange, created an enviable record. He increased vastly the company's business and standing in the territory, and won two nationwide "drives" for theatre bookings of the Monogram product. During this period he was elected president of the Friars Club in Boston, and also served as Grand Barker of the New England 

Variety Club.

In 1940 Broidy was elected to the Monogram board of directors, and by the end of that year had become vicepresident and general sales manager. with headquarters at the home office in Hollywood. At the Chicago convention of the company in the spring of 1945, he was elected vice president in charge of operations, and in November of the same year was elevated to the office of president, thus being one of Hollywood's youngest top executives. 

Broidy's regime as president has been eminently successful, and during this period the organization has made the greatest progress in its history. 

Distribution outlets have been established in cities around the globe, giving the company important playing time in practically every country in which American films may be shown. 

The foreign market is handled by Allied Artists International Corporation, formed in 1945. 

Broidy was the president of Temple

Israel of Hollywood, and is a member of the Brandeis Institute board of directors. He is a past presideht of the Independent Motion Picture Pro ducers Association, the Motion Picture Industry Council and the Permanent Charities Committee. He also has held important posts with the United Jewis hWelfare Fund. Active in the Variety Club. he holds memberships in that organization in Los Angeles, New 

York and Boston. President of Federation of Jewish Welfare Organizations and Vice Chairman of 1956-57 Chest 

Campaign. 

Born on June 14, 1905, he is the father of two sons, Arthur and Steven, and a daughter, Eleanor.

SOUTHWEST JEWRY 

15 

BEN SOLNIT 

Ben Solnit, president of the Federation of Jewish Welfare Organizations, died October 29, 1955.

When people learned that Ben had died there wasn't too much talk, not at first. There was a kind of shocked silence. People mostly sat alone, and waited for someone else to speak.

There was this sense of personal and communal loss. A difficult thing to share. After a while, the talk started all over the city. There were stories. 

Remembering when Ben went to Europe and Israel in 1947 and upon his return set to work packing hundreds of nairs of shoes for the hundreds of children in Displaced camps. There were others who remembered the new spirit which Ren brought into fundraising when he was chairman of the 

United Jewish Welfare Fund in 1951. 

There were those who remembered 

Ben's natural love of people, his deep and humble understanding of human mankind. They were the measure in the man. 

Ben Solnit was born in Russia. He came to this country at the age of 17 and arrived in Los Angeles in 1919. where he lived ever since. In 1938, he became the president of the Merchants and Manufacturers Club of Los 

Angeles. In 1944. he was president of the City of Hope. In 1951 he was chairman of the United Jewish Welfare Fund and in 1954. he was elected president of the Federation of Jewish 

Welfare Organizations. Ben Solnit was a man who introduced kindness and understanding in his business affairs and in the life of the community. He was shy, modest, tolerant but verv able. 

Above all, he will be remembered for his innate svmpathy for the troubled. the underdog. the lost. Although he was deenly devoted to Israel. having visited the country on three differrent occasions since the war. and to the needs of Tewrv the world over. he never lost track of the fact that his own community also needed his heln. He was a tireless worker on behalf of every good cause. No task was too menial or too humble for him. 

Ben was a fighter too. When he believed in a cause or people there was no effort that he was unwilling to spend on their behalf. Everybody is going to miss Ben - the lowly, as well as the rich. He was beloved by everyone who knew him and admired by his co-workers in the business world, as well as in communal life. 

As Federation president Ben Solnit was active in everv phase of the organization's activities. While vicepresident he was responsible for obtaining the land on which the new Hamburger Home has been built and was the leader in its campaign to raise funds for the construction. In the recently worked out agreement with the 

Community Chest. Ben served as a member of the committee and plaved a leading role in realizing it. The current Community Chest campaign owes a great debt to him for the ceaseless efforts in working and giving to make the 1955 campaign a success.

It will not be easy to fill the void left by Ben Solnit. He has left behind him an indelible impression for good. 

His generosity and labor for the community's good are unmatched. His passing should refresh our recollection of the qualitv and work done with honesty. of action lighted with inner humility and gentleness. Many a man and woman is grieving deeply, personally the loss of Ben Solnit.

SOUTHWEST JEWRY MARTIN RUDERMAN Executive Director 

Federation of Jewish Welfare Orgs. 

Martin Ruderman, executive director of the Federation of Jewish Welfare Organizations of Los Angeles, was born in New York City, December 18, 1907, and came to Los Angeles in 

1921. After attending Polytechnic 

High School and the University of 

Southern California, he graduated from USC in 1930 with a degree of 

Bachelor of Arts and general secondary credential. Returning to USC for an additional year's training in social work, he received a certificate in Social Work in 1931. then to chief of the division, which had the responsibility for complete supervision and direction of county operations of all social workers throughout the state engaged in the unemployment relief program. 

With the closing of the State Relief 

Administration in July, 1941, he became affiliated with the National Jewish Welfare Board, being assigned first as northern area worker with headquarters in San Francisco; then transferred to San Diego, and finally transferred to Los Angeles, where he became area director of the Jewish Welfare Board. 

In December, 1942, he joined the administrative staff of the Federation of Jewish Welfare Organizations as assistant director, and in December, 1945, became its executive director. 

He is married and has two children.

DR. BORIS D. BOGEN 

Dr. Boris D. Bogen, born in Moscow, Russia, July 25, 1869. Son of 

David M. Katzenellenbogen, of an old rabbinical family. Married Elizabeth 

Scholtz of Moscow in 1888, and had seven children, Lily (deceased), Jessie (Mrs. Abr. Shohan), Emil, Sophie (Mrs. Henry Mayers), Esther (Mrs. 

Julius Tietz) Robert and David. Died in Arcadia, California in June 28, 1929. Trained as an educator in Russia, where he taught in the household of Count Leo Tolstoy, he continued his studies, after coming to the United States in July, 1891, at 

New York University, where he received the degree of Doctor of Pedagogy in June, 1896. As instructor in the Baron de Hirsch Trade School in 

New York City 1898-1900 and as principal of the Baron de Hirsch Agricultural School for Boys in Woodbine, N. J. 1900-1904 he helped train many Jewish youths to economic independence. As Superintendent of the 

United Jewish Charities of Cincinnati from 1904 to 1913 and as Field Secretary of the National Conference of 

Jewish Charities from 1913 to 1916 he developed and applied the principles of Jewish social service which he formulated in many talks and publications, and in the text book "Jewish Philanthropy", published in 1917. 

As Director General of the Joint Dis tribution Committee during the next six years he organized and supervised the distributions of the millions of dollars raised by American Jews for the relief of the war-ravaged Jewish communities of Europe, especially in 

Poland and Russia. In 1923-24 he was superintendent of the Federation of 

Jewish Charities of Los Angeles.

SOUTHWEST JEWRY The Los Angeles 

Jewish Community Council The Los Angeles Jewish Community Council came into being in 1934 at the very moment in history Hitler came into power vowing to exterminate the Jews. 

In fact, Hitler's and the Nazis' emergence on the world scene speeded the formation of the LAJCC. 

Its beginnings are quite dramatic.

Calls from the daily newspapers to community leaders asking for someone to speak with authority on the official sentiments of the Los Angeles Jewish community about the Nazis and their anti-Semitic outrages, had pointed up the need for an official body of some kind to act for the Jewish community. A great overflow mass meeting held at the Philharmonic Auditorium to register the general community's protest against the outrages against the Jews in Germany which was summoned by representatives of the major Jewish organizations of the city, emphasized the importance of creating a central Jewish organization that could speak and act not only in times of emergency, but in all matters affecting the well-being of the Jewish community. 

The original incorporators included such wellknown Jewish community figures as the late Judge Harry A. Hollzer (first LAJCC president), Rabbi 

Edgar F. Magnin, Louis B. Mayer, Ben R. Mever, Marco R. Newmark, Charles 

Brown (LAJCC past president, M. B. Silberberg, Aaron Riche, Walter S. Hilborn, Judge Isaac Pacht (LAJCC past president). Also I. B. Benjamin, Chaim Shapiro, Judge Lester W. Roth, Mrs. S. H. Berch, Rabbi Jacob Kohn, David O. Selznick, Joseph L. Loeb, Jacob J. Lieberman, Mrs. Harold M. Stern, Joseph H. Rosenberg, and the late David Blumberg, David Familian and Dr. Nathan S. Saltzman. 

Today the LAICC is the united voice and representative instrument for carrying out the cooperative efforts of nearly 470 Jewish organizations. 

Every organization, with a Jewish purpose, in existence one year or more (and a membership of 25) is eligible for membership.

The LAJCC is the corrdinating body for this area's Jewish population of 400,000.

It works, through its various agencies and committees, to solve the problems of the afflicted, eradicate social and racial tensions, create a secure Jewish future and build a better and stronger Jewish community, and it conducts the annual 

UJWF Campaign, United Jewish Community Building, 590 N. Vermont Avenue where the LAJCC offices are maintained: "Behold how good and how pleasant it is for Brethren to dwell together in unity."

SOUTHWEST JEWRY 

25 

United Jewish Welfare Fund 

The Jews of Los Angeles have a long, almost 100-year record, of pooling their financial means to aid fellow Jews in distress the world over. 

As far back as 1905, Los Angeles Jewry rallied to aid their co-religionists in Russia through a united campaign. 

There was a United Jewish Welfare Fund campaign-incorporated as suchthroughout the 1920's and up until 1937 when it was taken over by the Los 

Angeles Jewish Community Council.

Since its "big" campaigns began in 1946, the UJWF has done an outstanding job not only for the community but for Jews in Europe and Israel and for national organizations.

This is the proud record of the UJWF for the past decade. 

Since 1946, the UJWF has raised nearly $70 million dollars. It has sent almost $40 million dollars overseas for Europe and Israel. During these same years, it has allocated to our local Federation of Jewish Welfare Organizations some $8,000,000 and has granted $2,500,000 to our Bureau of Jewish Education. 

It has spent more than $2,000,000 through local and national defense agencies to combat anti-Semitism and bigotry. 

It has demonstrated its ability, time and again, to meet emergencies and crises. It is efficient and flexible. It makes our community strong and keeps it united.

The Welfare Fund is more than a convenient way of raising vast sums of money. It is the expression of the basic unity of our Jewish community life; it provides an opportunity for all Jews to participate in a common undertaking to meet basic responsibilities. 

More than any other historic development, it is the common denominator of American Jewish life. The Welfare Fund drive, American and Los Angeles 

Jewry has produced a unique blend of its ancient heritage of community responsibility with the newer concept of American democracy. 

The inspired and inspiring men who have served as general chairmen, beginning with 1946, are as follows: Max Firestein, the late Leonard A. Chudacoff, Julius Fligelman, M. F. Berg, Oscar Pattiz, the late Ben Solnit, Leslie Cramer, Mark Taper, Joseph D. Shane, Ike Greenberg, William W. Bruck and the 1957 chairman Samuel Pensick.

SOUTHWEST JEWRY Bureau of Jewish Education By DR. SAMUEL DININ 

The history of the Bureau of Jewish Education is that of greater community responsibility for Jewish education and greater concern with the total Jewish educational needs of the Los Angeles Jewish comunity. In the continued efforts of the Los Angeles Jewish Community Council to draft a blueprint for an emerging Jewish community, the Bureau has served its parent body as an instrument for planning and co-ordinating a system of Jewish schooling for the ever-increasing Jewish child population in this city. 

The formation of the Bureau of Jewish Education is closely connected with the early history of the Los Angeles Jewish Community Council. The need for a Bureau as a co-ordinating agency was felt as early as 1933, when the Jewish Community Council was in its infancy and was still known as the United Jewish Community of Los Angeles. The idea took shape in 1935, when the United Jewish 

Community became a permanent organization. 

At its second annual meeting, held in October, 1935, Judge Harry A. Hollzer, president of the United Jewish Community, appointed a Committee on Jewish Education, with Jacob J. Lieberman as its chairman. Out of this committee grew our present Bureau of Jewish Education.

The Bureau started slowly. Its initial activities consisted of a series of meetings seeking to find ways and means for co-ordinating the work of local schools. 

Finally, in 1937, the United Jewish Community voted $15,000 for Jewish education (part of which went to the University Religious Conference), and subsidies were then given to four schools which met certain curricular and enrollment requirements.

The growth of the community and the subsequent development of the Los Angeles Jewish Community Council gave added impetus to the work of the infant Bureau. In the ensuing years, new schools became affiliated with the 

Bureau. Under the chairmanship of Herman A. Bachrack, the Bureau made a great deal of progress in co-ordinating the work of its affiliated schools, and in creating an awareness that Jewish education is a matter of community concern and responsibility.

By 1944 the growth of the Los Angeles Jewish community began to assume hitherto unprecedented proportions. The number of newly organized schools rose steadily. The great influx of population led the Community Council leadership to think not only of elementary Jewish schooling, but also in terms of the Jewish educational needs of all groups and ages in the community. As a result, Dr. Alexander M. Dushkin, renowned Jewish educator, was asked by the Los 

Angeles Jewish Community Council to survey Jewish education in this city. 

Dr. Duchkin's study and recommendations led to the reorganization of the Bureau in 1945, under the chairmanship of Peter M. Kahn. In the same year, Dr. Samuel Dinin and Dr. Morris Leibman were invited to the Bureau to serve as executive director and associate director, respectively. 

Since its reorganization in 1945, the Bureau has developed a new concept of community responsibility to Jewish education. Financial aid has been extended not merely to so-called community schools (which in the past meant any school not under congregational auspices) but to all schools, congregational and otherwise, which need it. Whereas in the past the Bureau concerned itself mainly with week-day Hebrew and Yiddish schools, after its reorganization it extended its educational services to all types of Jewish schools in the community, including one-day-a-week schools. And last but not least, it began to concern itself with the whole range of Jewish education in the community, elementary, youth and adult.

SOUTHWEST JEWRY 

29 29 The Bureau has come a long way in its comparatively short history of twenty years. The number of its affiliated schools grew from 13 in 1945 to 67 in 1955, consisting of 130 units with a total of 14.108 pupils in attendance. The Bureau is not only an administrative agencv whose job it is to see to it that subsidies are given for good cause and in accordance with definite rules and regulations. It is today a service agency which extends supervision. aid in registration and enrollment. guidence to school boards, in-service courses for teachers, and other services to schools in the Greater Los Angeles area. The Bureau tries to help establish schools in new neighborhoods, to merge small schools of similiar ideology in the same neighborhood, to give services to outlying suburban communities, and to disseminate information of interest to Tewish teachers and parents. 

As a committee of the Los Angeles Jewish Community Council, the Bureau of Jewish Education has always believed in the need and value of Ahdut-unity. 

Affiliated with the Bureau are all types of Jewish schools. Hebrew and Yiddish schools, Center schools. Orthodox. Conservative and Reform congregational schools-a unique phenomenon in American Jewish community life. Likewise, the members of the Bureau of Jewish Education represent every ideology, religious orientation and opinion prevalent in our Jewish community. Thus, the structure of the Bureau of Jewish Education. its affiliates, its activities give concrete form to our Community Council's cherished slogan: AHDUT-UNITY! 

This is the story of the Bureau of Jewish Education of the Los Angeles Tewish Community Council. one of the largest agencies of its kind in the United States, which grew out of a small committee formed by a group of courageous, far-seeing community leaders in the year 1935.

JACOB M. ALKOW 

Tacob M. Alkow, was born in Wilno, Lithuania and came to the United

States as a small child with his parents. 

In the United States. he attended the 

Rabbi Jacob Ioseph School, high school. City College of New York, and the University of Southern California.

He is a graduate of the Hebrew Teachers Training School and has a LLB degree. 

After moving from New York, he moved to Los Angeles, California, where he became prominently identified with the Zionist and Hebrew Educational work in the city. In addition to his having served as a princi-

SOUTHWEST JEWRY 

pal of the first modern Hebrew school of Los Angeles that had six teachers and three hundred pupils in 1929-1937, he was also the first executive director of the Jewish Centers Association of 

Los Angeles. Mr. Alkow, was a pioneer in the building of the Jewish Center Movement in Los Angeles. After having served as the first director of the modern social center in Boyle Heights, he was instrumental in the establishment of the Beverly Fairfax Community Center. This later became the new 

Jewish Center of Los Angeles.

After leaving his professional work, Mr. Alkow became engaged in various business enterprises. He was successful in the export import art business with offices in various cities in the Orient. When these were closed, as a result of the World War. Mr. Alkow founded the Filmcraft Studios that was the pioneer studio for television on film. Under Mr.. Alkow's general management, the studio obtained and produced the first major television shows on film. 

Having served as the president of the Pacific Coast Zionist Organization, and having devoted a life-time of service to the Zionist Movement, Mr. 

Alkow volunteered to serve (for a period of two years) in Israel where he built the Z.O.A. House Cultural Pro gram and where he erected the first important open air Hollywood Bowl type musical and art center in Tel Aviv. While in Israel, he was also active as the director of the Palestine 

Economic Corporation and as a director of the Foreign Investors Associations.

Mr. Alkow is affiliated with the Rotary of Tel Aviv, Israel and with the 

Masonic Lodge of Tel Aviv, Israel, the B'nai B'rith and the Histadruth 

Ivrith of America. 

On his return to the United States,

Mr. Alkow became an allied member of the New York Stock Exchange and a partner of Henry Montor in the 

Henry Montor Associates, Inc. stock brokerage firm. He has transferred his major Zionist and cultural activities to the East Coast. 

PETER M. KAHN 

1878 - 1952 

Born in Kiev, Russia, Sept. 30, 1878, a son of Mark and Fruma (Friedland) his father being a distinguished hebrew scholar. The Kahn family was known as a family of Rabbis for over eleven hundred years. lycko

Young Peter received a thorough hebrew education in his early youth, but he began noticing the suffering of the Russian peasant and very soon he began holding very strong political views and on account of his sympathy with Russian Revolutionists, he was exiled to Siberia, and had to spend several years in various Russian prisons. 

He escaped, however, from Siberia,

SOUTHWEST JEWRY 

31 and came to New York. Although he tried several times and met with success each time, he had great difficulty in remaining in one place for any length of time, being possessed with the spirit that is known as "Wanderlust." He started west, settling in

St. Louis for a while, and later in 

California, where it was necessary to start from the bottom of the ladder again.

For a while he was in the fruit retailing business, but his ability and great energy gradually brought him to the front of the ranks, and he organized the Kahn and Simmonds 

Wholesale House, of which he became president.

He has given very much of his time and support to almost every Jewish national and local institution. 

He was one of the first supporters and organizers of the Jewish Consumptive Relief Sanitarium, of the Yiddish

National Folk Shule, the Hebrew Sheltering Home, Jewish Ex-Patients' 

Home, etc. It is quite evident that 

Mr. Kahn's great interest in social work sprang from his human very sympathy, as he was known to have rendered his friends and acquaintances many an assistance, that they might gain a foothold.

He was one of the organizers of the People's National Bank, of which he was vice-president, a director of the

People's Mortgage Company. presi dent of the Peter M. Kahn Produce 

Company, member of the Masons, Elks. City Club, Commercial Club. and vice-president of the Tewish Con sumptive Relief Association. Past Chairman Bureau of Jewish Educa tion.

Married Nadia Kahn in 1903. Thev had three children: Peter, Jr., who is a graduate of Berkley University, Lillian, and Regina.

DR. NATHAN SALTZMAN 

1889 - 1956 

Born in Teapol, Volin, Russia, in 1889, received a thorough Jewish education until the age of fifteen, when he left for Zhitomir, where he continued his studies. He later entered the University of Kiev. 

At the age of twenty he went abroad, continuing his studies in Vienna, Austria up to 1911.

WALTER S. HILBORN 

Born in Boston, Mass., September 

11, 1879. Attended public schools in 

Boston. Attended Harvard University where he obtained an undergraduate degree and a degree from the Harvard 

Law School. 

He entered the law offices of Warren & Brandeis of which Louis D. 

Brandeis was a partner. In 1905, he moved to New York and became a partner in the firm of Gallert, Hilborn and Raphael. 

Mr. Hilborn has had an extensive career as a public servant. He was at 

Since his arrival here he participated in all Zion activities, as the National Fund, Keren Hayesod, Histadruth, etc. 

For the past 25 years he was looked up to as the official representative leader and spokesman of the Poalei- Zion party also helping organize the 

League for Labor Palestine. 

It was under his influence that men like Charles Brown and Julius Fligelman were brought into the fold of Labor Zionism and are since then a great asset to our community. 

At his 50th anniversary he was honored by the Farband, naming a branch Dr Saltzman Labor Zionist 

Order. 

Chairman of the Bureau of Jewish 

Education, his untimely passing away was mourned by thousands who came to know and love him. He is survived by his widow, Ann Saltzman who shared all his activities and is continuing his good work in all functions that were dear to him. one time Deputy Assistant District 

Attorney in New York County; he served for a time as Special Deputy

Attorney General of the State of New 

York, and for a brief time was a Special Assistant to the Attorney General of the United States. He was Acting

In July, 1929, he moved to Los Angeles to become trustee for King C. 

Gillette and, upon his resignation as trustee, he became associated with the firm of Loeb and Loeb of which he is presently a partner. 

He is a member of the New York 

Bar Association, the American Bar 

Association and the Los Angeles Bar 

Association, and of the American 

Friends of the Hebrew University and of B'nai B'rith. 

He married Aimee Gallert Hilborn on January 1, 1908, who died on June 

21, 1948.

SOUTHWEST JEWRY THE LOS ANGELES JEWISH COMMUNITY LIBRARY 

The Los Angeles Jewish Community Library officially established in 1947. 

But attempts of organizing a central Jewish library in Los Angeles were made as early as in the beginning of the 1930's. At that time, a group of cultural-minded 

Jews, consisting of Rabbis, teachers and lay leaders, osganized the Hebrew Library Association of Los Angeles. For different reasons, this attempt did not succeed and soon the Association was dissolved. Later some other attempts were made, but none of them with great success. The main reason for all these failures may lay in the fact that all these attempts were made before a real organized Jewish community existed here and the attempted libraries did not enjoy the support of the all Jewish community. It was different with the Jewish 

Community Library. The Jewish Community Library was created by the Los 

Angeles Jewish Community Council, and was, therefore, sponsored and supported by the organized Jewish community as a whole. 

The first chairman of the library and its moving spirit was the late pioneer of Jewish life in Los Angeles, the man of culture and lover of books and learning, Peter M. Kahn.

At the beginning, the library was housed in the back of the old Jewish Community Building. Later, after the new Jewish Community Building was erected, the library was moved to one of the rooms in the new building. The constant expansion of the library in book and in services, made this temporary quarters of the library inadequate, so that plans for a special building for the library started to materialize.

In spring 1952, after the passing of Peter M. Kahn. the Jewish Community 

Council Board approved to house the library in an edifiie, to be known as the Peter M. Kahn Memorial Building. A special committee was appointed with the aims of raising the sufficient funds and preparing the plans for such a building. 

In November 1952. the first meeting of this group. under the chairmanship of the late Ben Solnit, was held and a program of activities was outlined. Soon after that a fund raising campaign started and by winter 1953-54 the actual erection of the building started.

On Sunday, August 15, 1954. the new building was finished and a special dedication ceremony was held, in which hundreds of people participated. 

The library is now in possession of over 40,000 books.

SOUTHWEST JEWRY UNIVERSITY OF JUDAISM 

by Justin G. Turner

The University of Judaism, the West Coast branch of the Jewish Theological Seminary of America, was established in the fall of 1947. It was, however, conceived in the fertile mind of Professor Mordecai M. Kaplan who suggested the development of an institution of this type in a brilliant address delivered on February 4, 1945, at a gathering celebrating the 35th anniversary of the Teachers 

Institute of the Jewish Theological Seminary. 

The University at the present day with physical facilities still inadequate to its expanding program, serves its student population through the following departments: 

I - GRADUATE SCHOOL: 

The School requires for matriculation the usual bachelor degree from some recognized college as well as special familiarity with biblical literature and history. Since the University is fully and formally accredited by the 

Middle States Association of Universities and Colleges, it is prepared to offer the degree of Doctor of Philosophy, Doctor of Hebrew Literature, Master and Doctor of Religious Education, and Doctor of Jewish Theology. 

One matriculate in the School has already achieved his doctorate and several students who have completed the number of credits required for work “in course" are now engaged in study and research looking toward the completion of their theses. Since the majority of the students are rabbis and ministers of established congregations or teachers and educators regularly employed, the progress in specialized studies proceeds slowly. 

II – TEACHES INSTITUTE 

This offers a four-year course. It prepares students for teaching in regular weekday Jewish schools and enables those who are not inclined to make a profession of teaching to pursue Jewish studies on a collegiate level. The applicant must have completed an education equivalent to that offered by a secular and Jewish high school. The Teachers Certificate is only issued to such as have received the necessary credits and completed at least two years in a regularly accredited secular college or university. The degree of Bachelor of Religious Education goes to such as have to their credit a minimum of 120 units in the Teachers Institute, and, of course, have met the requirements for secular education as indicated above. 

III - INSTITUTE OF COMMUNAL SERVICE 

This department is still largely experimental since it was only organized last year. Its student body is especially selected from among the postconfirmants in congregational and community schools. Studies will be spread over a 4-year period and will require attendance of six hours per week until at least 60 credits have been attained.

These young people will not only have the opportunity of pursuing their own Jewish education on a high level but the school will provide such professional training as will enable them to serve the community as Sunday

SOUTHWEST JEWRY 

37 School teachers, club leaders, camp counsellors and in similar capacities. 

No applicant can qualify for admission who is not at least in the third year of high school.

IV – UNIVERSITY INSTITUTE AND WOMENS INSTITUTE 

Courses in the Institute are given at the University building and at several branches scattered throughout the city. They cover a wide variety of subjects Hebrew, Jewish literature, history, sociology, religion and philosophy. One credit is given for each semester hour (there are two semesters a year) and 36 credits are required for graduation. These credits must be attained in certain areas of study which are mandatory and in certain areas of study which are voluntary or elective. 

V - FINE ARTS 

The University would not measure up to the idea of Dr. Kaplan did it not contain a Department of Fine Arts. This Department, under the direction of Benjamin Zemach, not only trains young people in creative arts such as dance, music and drama, but offers each year some public presentation of artistic value which brings the work of the Department to the attention of the art loving public, both Jewish and Christian. The Department is assisted by a Committee upon which serve not alone devoted laymen but distinguished composers, writers and artists in all fields.

In this connection, mention should be made of the University Library (a research and scholar's library) and the Museum. The core of these consists of material loaned by the Jewish Theological Seminary and the Jewish Museum. The Library, however, has also been enriched by the Moses and 

Hannah Tannenbaum Memorial collection contributed by Jews of the Los Angeles community and which is being constantly increased. 

The expansion of these departments can only be anticipated in the proposed new quarters of the University. The present facilities have grown too cramped for this purpose.

The permanent staff of the University is as follows: 

Louis Finkelstein, Ph.D., Chancellor and President of the Faculties. 

Simon Greenberg, Ph.D., Vice-Chancellor of the Faculties, Director of the

University of Judaism. 

Samuel Dinin, Ph.D., Dean of the University. 

Jacob Kohn, D.H.L., Dean, Graduate School and Associate Professor of

Theology. 

Max Vorspan, M.H.L., Registrar. 

Mrs. Irwin Reiss, M.H.L., Registrar, Women's Institute. 

Louis Shub, M.A., Library Consultant and Instructor in Jewish History. 

The University glories in the fact that it has in no semester been without distinguished visiting professors whose scholarship has been placed at the service of the Graduate School, and who have consented to lecture likewise in some of the other departments of the University. From the Jewish Theological Semi nary Mordecai M. Kaplan, D.H.L., Professor of Philosophies of Religion in the Rabbinical School and Dean Emeritus of the Teachers Institute Seminary College of Jewish Studies; Shalom Spiegel, Ph.D., William Prager, Professor of Medieval

Hebrew Literature, Rabbinical School; Elias J. Bickerman, Ph.D., Research Fellow in Hellinistic Literature, Rabbinical School; Zevi Scharfstein, D.H. Litt., Professor of Jewish Education, Teachers Institute Seminary College of Jewish

SOUTHWEST JEWRY 

Studies; Shlomo Noble, Ph.D., Instructor in Bible and History, Seminary School of Jewish Studies. From the Hebrew University Martin Buber, Ph.D., Pro fessor of Social Philosophy; Simon Halkin, Ph.D., Professor of Hebrew; Alexander

Dushkin, Ph.D., Director, School of Education and Ernest Simon, Ph.D., School of Education. Distinguished rabbis and scholars - Paul Chertoff, M.A., David 

Aronson, D.H.L., and Abraham Menes, Ph.D.

These scholars attracted large numbers of qualified students not regularly on the roll of any department of the University and many of them also consented to appear in the Lecture Series offered by the University on general topics of scholarly interest to the community at large. These series of Public Lectures are another means by which the University of Judaism makes its presence felt and welcomed by the laity at large.

It should be stated that the Commencement Exercises of the University of Judaism, like those of the Jewish Theological Seminary in New York, have aspired to become occasions in the academic life of Los Angeles which would make an impression upon the whole world of scholarship, both Jewish and Christian. The first to receive an honorary degree from the University was 

Earl Warren, the present Chief Justice of the United States Supreme Court. He was so honored on June 27, 1951. Among recipients since that date have been 

Samuel Dinin, Dean of the University; Ernest Bloch, composer-conductor, University of California at Berkeley; Paul Gray Hoffman, Chairman of Board of 

Studebaker-Packard Corporation; Wendell M. Stanley, Chairman of the Department of Biochemistry, University of California at Berkeley; Darius Milhaud, composer-conductor, Professor of Composition, Mills College, Oakland.

The progress which the University has made since its inception is illustrated in the fact that last year the peak attendance was 960 students all regularly enrolled and registered in the various departments according to standards already indicated. There are men and women at the University of the ages from 16 to 

60 and beyond. There are representatives of all races, negro and white. In the 

Graduate School there have always been rabbis of all shades of Jewish opinion, an occasional Christian minister and theological students who desire to enrich their education with certain courses we had to offer, and it is hoped that the University will remain broadly Jewish and profoundly humanistic. 

We are now at a welcomed crisis in our development. The building now occupied by the University was originally given by Mr. Louis M. Rabinowitz of 

New York City who has become celebrated for his discerning gifts to the Seminary and to institutions of learning of all kinds. This building is now literally overflowing into the streets and suburbs of Los Angeles. A new site has recently been purchased by the Board of Overseers and we are moved by the hope that the new building will soon enable the institution to draw nearer to the realization of those goals first outlined by Dr. Kaplan, then sketched in by Dr. Finkelstein, and which are cherished by all those on the staff of the University or those who, not on its staff, have with love and devotion aided in its advance.

OUR SINCERE APOLOGY

We undertook this project early in 1952, gathering the material, but some of the Jewish leaders in our community have failed to submit the necessary material in time, and therefore we felt that rather than to leave them out it is best to overlook the alphabetical order.

AMERICAN ASSOCIATION FOR JEWISH EDUCATION By DR. PHILIP L. SEMAN Chairman-Executive Committee Western States Region 

One of the outstanding institutions for Jewish education on a national scale was organized by a group of lay leaders and professional workers. They felt that their work would be greatly benefited if there was a national body which would coordinate and serve their local efforts in the area of Jewish education, and would also help to promote community programs and higher standards for American Jewish education. Thus, we learn from the records that nearly seventeen years ago the American Association for Jewish Education was organized and elected Mr. Mark Eisner, “one-time” Chairman of the New York City Board of Higher Education, as its President, and Dr. Israel S. Chipkin, educational director of the Jewish Education Association of New York at that time, as Executive Secretary. The group dedicated the new Association to promote 

Jewish education in America and also to serve as a national clearing house for stimulating, coordinating and as a service agency, as well as to aid in the advancement of standards of instruction and of professional service. Another objective of the Association was to encourage research, experimentation and the production of Jewish educational materials; also to stimulate communal responsibility for all local organizations of Jewish educational endeavor. 

An important objective was to encourage cooperation effort among existing Jewish educational agencies throughout the country and to stress the cultural, spiritual and religious aspects of Jewish life and their harmony with the cultural and democratic traditions of America.

We are indebted to Dr. Chipkin, who in an address in May, 1939, at the 

Annual Meeting of the Association, said that the secretary, Dr. Leo Honor, recently deceased, and Ben Rosen volunteer their services. In 1944, Ben Rosen, (who died at the end of one year's service) had been called to serve as the first Executive Director. He was succeeded by Dr. Chipkin in 1945. Under his direction there was established three departments: One for Research and Information; another for Promotional Service, and one for Community Consultation 

Service. In 1948, a Department of Pedagogics and Curricular Activities was innaugurated.

Our Western States Region was established in 1948 and is now functioning under the able leadership of Seymour Fromer and under the chairmanship of an outstanding public-spirited citizen of Los Angeles-Mr. Julius Fligelman. 

The greatest organizational progress was made during the national chairmanship of Dr. Michael Stavitzky and now under the able leadership of Dr.

Philip Lown, and their active colleagues of the National Board, as well as by the 

Western States Region. 

A few years ago the Bar Mitzvah celebration of the American Association was observed and it was indicated that during the thirteen years, the support

SOUTHWEST JEWRY 

for Jewish educational purposes by Federations and Welfare Funds increased their support from $500,000 to $2,000,000. In 1939, twenty-eight Federations and 

Welfare Funds were interested and included support for Jewish education. In 

1956, there were 190 Federations and Welfare Funds interested in the work of the American Association for Jewish Education and are actively supporting it. Allocations received from these Welfare Funds during 1956 should total $70,000.

During the first thirteen years, central agencies for Jewish education multiplied from 12 to 41%; the percentage of children receiving Jewish education from 

28% in 1939, and 43% of children were attending Jewish schools of education. 

The Bar Mitzvah publication under the caption of "A Milestone of Progress" indicates that in the first thirteen years, there existed not a single post-graduate school in Jewish education. Today there are such departments at the following post-graduate schools: Dropsie College-Dr. Abraham A. Neuman; Hebrew Union College-Dr. Abraham N. Franzblau; The Jewish Theological Seminary of America - Dr. Judah Goldin; New York University - Dr. Abraham I. Katsh; 

Yeshivah University - Dr. Bernard Lander, and also the College of Jewish 

Studies and the University of Judaism, both in Los Angeles. 

At the meeting in 1952, in celebration of the Bar Mitzvah, there were representatives of school board and Jewish educational agencies from 53 communities. 

During these meetings, the general topic discussed was: "Whither Jewish Education" and the conclusions reached were that there was an urgent need to intensify Jewish education for all age groups. Also, that encouragement be given to the 

Jewish education of the adolescent and the young adult as well as to the recruiting of promising young men and women who will prepare for the Jewish teaching profession. They felt that the time was ripe to implement the resolution of the first national conference on Jewish education to launch a national study to evaluate the present status of Jewish education in this country. The National 

Study of Jewish Education has completed its field work in approximately 90 communities and the first report should be ready in 1957. Among the large communities surveyed were Los Angeles, Miami, Detroit; among middle-sized: 

Buffalo, Rochester and Cleveland; small: South Bend, Savannah. This study is sponsored and financed by the American Association, with Dr. Oscar I. Janowsky,

Chairman of the Commission in charge of it, and Dr. Uriah Z. Engelman, as director. Thus, the Association has charted the course for the years ahead, and with the community leaders throughout the country, the American Association for Jewish Education will follow that course, toward the goal of full effective education for all American Jews. "It is a fine sign in this direction," Dr. Philip Klutznick, a brilliant young 

Jew, who is the President of B'nai B'rith, says and keeps on saying: "In many ways, one of the step-children of our community has been Jewish education, both formal and informal. We seem to have found extraordinary resources for many causes, but much too little for this purpose."

Much can be looked forward to under the able leadership of Dr. Judah 

Pilch, the present Executive Director of the American Association for Jewish 

Education.
