THE LOS ANGELES HEBREW JUNIOR HIGH SCHOOL 

Our Jewish Community takes due pride in its fine schools, dotting every section of greater Los Angeles. The awareness of the danger of assimilation moved our leaders to foster every conceivable system of Jewish education, from the Sunday School to the intensive all-day Yeshwah from nursery to university in an effort to inculcate our Young Generation with an appreciation of Judaism, and to inspire them with a sense of loyalty to its cultural, national and religious values. 

In that broad field of Jewish education, even those leaders who believed in a maximum thereof and courageously undertook to foster the costly "all day" school for the upbringing of the Los Angeles Jew of tomorrow, found the tremendous expense of staffing and housing so staggering, that they did not dare go beyond the Grade School level. Accordingly, upon graduating from the sixth grade, their Jewish pupils were forced to enrole in a public Jr. High School, by far too young to resist the strong trend toward assimilation. 

Only one group of Los Angeles Jewish parents viewed their responsibility toward the proper upbringing of their children seriously enough to establish, some years ago, the Los Angeles Hebrew Junior High School. However, without a building of their own, they had to wander, hat in hand from one makeshift locality to another to overcome this and other handicaps. Some of the parents had to dedicate themselves to their labor of love, far beyond the call of duty. 

They were inspired by the conviction that only in the wholesome Jewish atmosphere which Yeshivah education can provide, they could mould the personality of their children in the highest concept of a truly good Jew on the 

American scene.

This Los Angeles Hebrew Junior High School is soon to become of age. 

In February, 1957, its graduates will be ready to enter Senior High School. Such facilities as a laboratory, workshop, sewing and cooking accomodations hitherto unavailable, will soon become essential for these boys and girls. This need for an adequate school building of their own has therefore become so pressing that it actually poes the question of "to be, or not to be?"

At this critical point, the Los Angeles Hebrew Junior High School was fortunate in obtaining the services of the famous scholar and educator, Rabbi Elijah 

D. Stampfer. For a number of years, Rabbi Stampfer was instructor of Talmud and Bible at the Hebrew Theological College of Chicago. Later, even while holding outstanding Rabbinical positions, Rabbi Stampfer always looked upon the educational functions of his congregations as the most important phase of his activities. During the last four year, Rabbi Stampfer held the position of Dean of the Los Angeles West Coast Branch of the Hebrew Theological College. 

In his new position as Dean and Executive Director of the Los Angeles 

Hebrew Junior High School, Rabbi Stampfer not only supervises and directs the activities of its fine staff of six scholarly instructors, but also teaches Talmud with Hebrew as the language of instruction. Visiting his classes in action, one may well imagine that he is attending a Yeshiwah in Israel. 

Mr. Abe Barenfeld, the benefactor and President of the Los Angeles Hebrew 

Junior High School, has recently announced the purchase of a spacious lot on 

Pico Boulevard, Corner High Point Street. He had a sign posted thereon, reading: "This is the site of the new home of the Los Angeles Hebrew Junior High School.

SOUTHWEST JEWRY HISTORY OF TEMPLE ISRAEL OF HOLLYWOOD 

Temple Israel was born in the late '20's, at the Fox Studios in the office of Sol M. Wurtzel, our first president. Aiding and abetting him were several other professional celebrities, whose feet rested in the fleshpots of Hollywood, but whose heads reared high, searching for spiritual inspiration. 

They did not have to search long before they found what they were looking for in the person of the late Rabbi Isadore Isaacson. The founders gathered about the Rabbi, and soon a sizeable group, comprising several "minyons" swelled their ranks.

Rabbi Isaacson, soon drilled the rudiments of Judaism into the minds of these religious rookies, and before long, had a congregation that began to pull in the same direction. A house of worship was rented the spacious home of the 

Japanese movie star, Sessue Hayakawa. 

As for the name chosen, how prophetic, in the light of the birth of the 

Republic of Israel!

Rabbi Isaaison will always be remembered, whenever the early struggles of Temple Israel are recounted. He created an enthusiasm, where apathy had existed before. He rekindled pride in a heritage that had been forgotten. His kindliness, sincerity, patience, and good humor, quickly attracted more adherents, and created a zest and enthusiasm that took form in the establishment of the auxiliaries.

The Brotherhood and the Sisterhood performed a great miracle of public relations. The "Observer" was born. Before long, the walls of our temporary 

Temple began to bulge and creak under the strain of a swelling membership. 

Our second, and larger home, was the Methodist Church, situated on the corner of Lexington and Vine Streets. This was one of the first examples of Interfaith cooperation in Los Angeles. On different nights, Vine Street would hear the 

Methodist hymnal and the Hebrew chants. This was probably very confusing to the casual passerby. The choral singing was much better on Sunday, but Cantor 

Silverman had the edge in solo performance on Friday night. 

Temple Israel was now growing into a congregation of respectable numbers, and not to be denied. We were not an experiment any longer. 

Our Sunday School was crowded with children of members who lived not only in Hollywood, but in outlying sections that sprang up in a rapidly growing Los Angeles- crude little settlements like Beverly Hills and Bel Air. 

Other institutions were also growing — notably of then Reverend, but current Bishop Glenn Phillips. They contemplated building a new edifice on the corner of Highland and Franklin Avenues.

The executive Board of Temple Israel looked over the old church building, and decided that it would make an ideal home for our congregation. So again, we were housed in a Methodist Church, but this time we decorated it with Hebrew appurtenances and a substantial mortgage.

SOUTHWEST JEWRY 

45 Then came the fateful year, 1929. The stock market crash had its repercussions in Temple Israel as well as in the Temples of Finance. The wolf howled dismally in the lonely halls of the Temple because he had nothing to feed on. 

The lines of creditors reached around the corner to Vine Street. The only thing that grew fat was the mortgage. But Temple Israel held the line. The wolf died of starvation, and we started from scratch again. 

In 1936, tragedy aagin struck. Our leader, Rabbi Isaacson, passed away mourned not only by this congregation, but by the whole community that had learned to love and respect him.

Casting about for some time in search of a new leader, we finally chose a young man who came to us from the North. Rabbi Morton A. Bauman immediately endeared himself to us by his youthful freshness and great enthusiasm. His friendliness and ready smile enchanted everyone. Under his leadership, the 

Sunday school became a dynamic institution. Literally hundreds clamored for admittance. Miss Ethel Ewens, the School principal during Rabbi Isaacson's life, returned from retirement to become the superintendent. 

In 1941, war took its toll, and left its mark in Temple Israel history. Rabbi 

Bauman was among the first to make a personal sacrifice. He joined the Army as a chaplain. This left another vacuum in the Temple that had to be filled. Rumor had it that there was a young Rabbi in the Middle West, who was creating a sensation by his fervor, learning and eloquence. Investigation proved that the rumor was true and like Joseph, Rabbi Max Nussbaum emerged from the wilderness into the hearts of our Congregation. 

A new consciousness pervaded our Temple a consciousness of service beyond the confines of our community. Rabbi Nussbaum formed classes for adult education. Parents who waited for their children to finish their Sunday sessions, themselves attended these courses and heard lectures by prominent men in many fields of activity and knowledge. 

the at- 

These were the critical days of Palstine's struggle for existence tempted massacre of an entire people in Europe, the modern Diaspora the accumulation of bitterness and desperature engendered by Britain's vicious foreign policy in the Holy Land, which threatened to destroy displaced Jewry's homeland.

In the ofrefront of Jewry's struggle stood Temple Israel. Members of our board became nationally prominent in their gigantic labors to raise incredible sums of money to furnish Israel with the sinews of war. 

When the war ended. Rabbi Bauman returned. By that time, however, the 

Temple had grown to such an extent, and Rabbi Nussbaum had so endeared himself in the hearts of the congregation that the board found it necessary to engage the service of both Rabbis - thus bringing about a co-leadership unprecedented in congregational history. 

By this time, that Frankenstein of all Temples, old man mortgage, had been burned at the stake. For the first time, Temple Israel was "in the black" but so many families clamored for membership that we could not resist the call of our added responsibility to the community. With the progress and advance of commercial enterprises in the neighborhood militating against us, we decided to build a Temple of our own, a Temple to properly and adequately serve the demands of a growing community.

The beautiful edifice, now a reality, is the result of the valiant efforts and

SOUTHWEST JEWRY 

dreams of a self-sacrificing membership, inspiring leadership, and pride of a fighting people who have faithfully borne the Torah and our great tradition through centuries of travail and darkness to the Land of Promise. 

The Temple has undergone many changes and vicissitudes in the last 10 years. Rabbi Bauman saw an unusual opportunity to advance the cause of 

Liberal Reform Judaism and assumed the pulpit of Temple Beth Hillel in the Vallev, which has become a great Jewish Community under his able leadership. 

In quick turn came another overwhelming personality - Steve Broidy, president of Allied Artists Studio and now a national figure in Jewish affairs. Today, now serving his third term as president is Sherrill C. Corwin - a personality cast in an entirely different mold - gentle, kindly. persuasive, but firm and entirely devoted to his congregation, the community and his faith. 

Temple Israel is reborn today. In the vestments of a new dignity and a new strength of achievement, our Temple will take its place at the forefront of battle in the struggles to come not only for the honor of Judaism, but for the glory of America that has given so many of our brethren the peace and happiness they could not find elsewhere. 

JOHN STONE, Past President and Founder of Temple Israel JOHN STONE

"BIKUR CHOLIM" SOCIETY THE MOTHER OF MOUNT SINAI HOSPITAL & CLINIC By JOS. L. MALAMUT 

Just as it is true that the "Hebrew Benevolent" was the mother of the Federation, so it can also be stated that the mother institution of the presen Beauiful Mount Sinai Hospital and Clinic was a very modest humble little society which was known as the "Bikur Cholim" society.

For those who do not know hebrew, we might explain that the two hebrew words mean "visiting the sick."

And just as the Federation of Jewish Welfare Organizations today never forgets to remind us of the contribution the "Hebrew Benevolent" has made towards the progress of Los Angeles Jewish Institutions so it is also well to remember the contribution the "Bikur Cholim" society has made towards the birth and development of Mount Sinai Hospital and Clinic of today. 

Every generation has its pioneers and veterans, and so it was thirty-five years ago.

In February, 1920, Los Angeles Jewry was a village in comparison to what it is today. Hundreds of poor Jewish families came here mainly for their health. 

They were homeless, destitute and sick but there was one public spirited young man who conceived the idea that Los Angeles must find some sort of an organization that should devote itself to the needs of the sick and helpless. That young man was CHARLES GROMAN. He was the founder, the organizer, and it was then that the "Bikur Cholin" Society was called into being, and later incorporated itself in a "Bikur Cholim Hospital." 

From the humble beginning a two-room bungalow was rented, which housed only one patient, and later was turned into what was known for years as the Home for Incurables.

So while we pride ourselves with the Mount Sinai Hospital of today, let us remember its humble beginning, and the mother organization, the "Bikur Cholim" society, whose founder and organizer was the public spirited veteran of those days, Charles Groman. 

May his loving memory be blessed!

As editor in charge of this volume, I owe a dept of gratitude to the public spirited benevolent son of my late beloved friend, Harry Groman, who came to my aid in an hour when I felt that the undertaking of such a volume is in need of an extra sum of money, as otherwise it may never see the light. Like father--like son, Harry understood and saved us a good many embarrahsments.

THE JEWISH NATIONAL FUND COUNCIL OF LOS ANGELES 

In common with the general rise in popularity throughout the country, the Jewish National Fund made rapid strides in Los Angeles. To the Zionist who keeps abreast with the movement, and who is familiar with the achievements of the Keren Hayesod, Keren Kayemeth, the Funds remarkable expansion and progress in the past years, does not come unexpected. 

Locally the Jewish National Fund has many devoted and followers among our pioneers. To be sure to them the Jewish National Fund was a labor of love, of an affection unparalleled in devotion, nevertheless it was a hard and an ardous task.

Among the pioneers it is well to remember Abraham Shapiro, Harry Fram, S. Rosen, M. J. Beck and Abraham Rothstein.

Later the group was joined by a host of friends, such as Meyer Zameres, Mendel Sheff, William Borden, Clarence Rothchild, Max Saron, Mr. and Mrs.

I. M. Silver, H. Rosen, Mr. and Mrs. Sam Jurbin, Sam Kanner, Mr. and Mrs. 

Samuel Eidelson, A. Weitzman, Harry K. Čohen, Ed. Jalloff, A. Levinson, L. 

Feigelman, A. Fisch, L. Wolf, E. H. Bojarsky, Aaron Riche, Rose Pahn, Dr. 

Nathan Saltzman, D. Savetnick, M. Herman, Mrs. Ida Cohn, Mrs. M. Bercut, Chas. J. Rosin, and Mildred Lerner. The Daughters of Zion under the leadership of Mrs. L. Cohen organized as the Ladies Auxiliary of the J.N.F. Council in 1926. 

This was the begining, thirty years ago. Today the Jewish National Fund is the most beloved, the most popular ideal with every Jew and has the participation and support of hundreds of thousands of Jews in Los Angeles. 

THE JEWISH NATIONAL FUND TODAY 

In the year 1956, the Jewish National Fund Council celebrated its 25th Silver anniversary. From the financial point of view, one can evaluate the great progress made in the past quarter of a century. At the first Chanukah banquet held in 1931, the sum total of $742.82 was raised, but for the fiscal year 1955-56, the sum of $256,000 was raised in the city of Los Angeles, and a quota of $400,000 was unanimously adopted at the 25th Annual Jewish National Fund conference held at the Ambassador Hotel in October, 1956. These figures represent in essence the devoted and consecrated effort of thousands of people through these years, who have taken the JNF ideal as part and parcel of their daily life. 

Mr. Theodore Strimling was elected chairman of the Jewish National Fund 

Council back in 1931 and through these 25 years he has displayed great leadership and has helped bring the JNF the prestige and success it now enjoys. 

In 1940 Mr. Hanan Yarden was appointed executive director for this area. 

He did a very fine job in his capacity and was instrumental in bringing about a greater degree of achievement. In 1949 Mr. Yarden left the West Coast and

SOUTHWEST JEWRY 

became the director of Kereth. Hih position was filled by a veteran Zionist and an able administrator, Mr. Isadore Kadis.

In 1952, the assistant to the executive director of the JNF of America, Mr. 

Fred Kahan was appointed as director for the 11 western states. Mr. Kahan served as assistant to Mr. Mendel Fisher for a period of five years and upon his transfer to the West Coast, he made innovations in the program of the Jewish National Fund. One of these was the setting up of a Folk Division with 

Mr. Leon Meltzer as its director.

The important strides made by this group of organizations working with a great degree of sincerity for the J.N.F. has resulted in bringing about much needed funds for the State of Israel and the Jewish National Fund. In fact for the year 1955-56, the sum of approximately $50,000 was raised through the Folk organizations.

Mr. Meltzer, one of the veteran Zionists, who celebrated his 50th year in the Poale Zion is truly bringing to the masses of our people the word of Zion and the Jewish National Fund. 

From the vears 1954-56, the sum of one and a half million dollars was raised through bequests, wills and insurance policies in the city of Los Angeles. 

Many projects have been established in Israel through these bequests and many more are still in the offering. 

THEODORE STRIMLING 

Theodore Strimling, one of the active communal workers in Los Angeles for over a quarter of a century, was born at Sahvil, Lithuania, October 15, 1881, a son of Abraham and Hinde Strimling. It was there that he received his Jewish education, up to the age of twelve when he was sent off to the Yeshivah of Lomz, then to Slobodka. 

Mr. Strimling arrived in the United

States in 1904, continued his studies, taking up pharmacy and graduated from Brooklyn School of Pharmacy in 1907. 

Married Esther Blatt, September 23, 1913. 

Their children are: Hilda S., Joseph 

D. Strimling. 

Theodore Strimling retired in 1923, and it was then that he dedicated himself to communal work which he continues until this very dav. He has been nresident of the Jewish National Fund 

Council since 1933, he is honorary

I.N.F. in the hearts and minds of hundreds of thousands of people working for and contributing to the

WILLIAM FRIEDMAN 

Born at Sosnowitz, Poland, 1904, son of Chaye Yittel and Mendel Friedman, the oldest of eight children, but only four of them survived. Father was a successful merchant. His father as well as the rest of the children were destroyed by the Hitler regime. 

William was from his early youth, imbued with a great love for social work and Yiddish culture. He is still verv interested in the Yiddish press and theater, literature and art. 

Participated as actor in Yiddish plavs in Warsaw, Lodz and Lemberg.

He was drawn into the Poalei-Zion movement while still very young. 

During the first world war his father lost everything and departed for the 

U.S. Settled in Chicago, from there father advised mother to see that

William learns a trade, so that it will come in handy by the time he will also come to the U. S. Mother at that time had eight children. It was then that William left from Sosnowits for 

Warsaw, then Krakau, and from there for Lemberg. At the age of seventeen father advised mother to send William off to Belgium and from there try to make his way to the U. S. Before getting to Belgium he spent one year in Germany in 1921, he settled first at 

Breslaw, then at Berlin and from there moved to Koeln. 

In 1922 he arrived at Brussel, Belgian, with a "capital" of one frank.

He spent his money on two post cards, sending one to mother. the other to father asking for funds, as he was penniless. As an immigrant he has sustained himself by receiving free meals from the Haias and fifteen days. later he received two dollars from father and also obtained a loan from his kinsmen in the sum of four dollars, with that money he purchased shoes and a raincoat.

Belgium is noted for its rains. Finally he obtained employment in a ladies handbag shop and continued to work until he left for Paris, where he was employed at the ladies millinery trade, but did not succeed very much, so he returned to Belgium. 

In 1924, his father returned to 

Poland, and then William had to give up his dream to leave for the United States. 

As time ran, being twenty years of age, he could not forget that he has left his beloved sweetheart (Fanny), in Bendin, Poland, so he managed to send for her in 1925, and they married immediately in Brussels. They were blessed with their first child at the end of a year, but the first baby died being an infant of five weeks old.

After a struggle of four years, William did manage to work up himself, in Belgium and managed to acquire his own building worth a quarter of a million francs being the owners of a millinery shop.

Simultaneously William became interested in Communal activities, being instrumental in founding the Sports

Club, Maccabee, from which he was delegated to the Macabiade in Tel- Aviv, in 1935. In 1938 he was elected chairman of the Jewish press in Belgium to select the Queen of the Press, he also helped to organize the Farband of Polish Jews in Brussels, also the Ezra, and the "Beth-Lechem, and fortunately for William, he sensed the onrushing wave of the Hitler-Terror,

SOUTHWEST JEWRY 

so he left for the U.S. and arrived in 

New York Feb. 2nd, 1939 on the

Normandie, two months before the outbreak of the war. 

Six months later William organized the Belgium Jewish Society in New

York, which later affiliated with the 

Jewish National Workers Alliance, Branch 430, being elected the first treasurer having financed the first meeting at the National Theatre Bldg. 

Since the New York weather did not agree with him, nor was he successful in his new venture in the fur business, he decided to leave for 

Los Angeles, and arrived here Dec. 5, 1941, two days before the Pearl Harbor attack. Times were bad in Los

Angeles. People left the city on account of the treacherous Japs, William became desperate and worried over his wife and their daughter, Carolyn, eight years old, (now Mrs. Sam 

Hodes and is the mother of two girls, Sheri and Cathy.) 

As usual whenever he arrives at a new destination, he starts a new business, he followed the same method here, and he opened a hot-dog stand, continuing several years and in the meantime he went into the parking lot business succeeding and remaining therein until this very day.

Being a very energetic person, he also became interested as a builder and in other investments and notwihtstanding all business activities, he still finds time for communal work, as well as to create a song from time to time, among his songs are well known his poem "Years Come and 

Go." and a "Hymn" for the Belgium

Jewish Society, where he is the president, and "By the Jordan."

He was also the founder of the Belgium Jewish Society and through his initiative and influence the society has undertaken many projects, as for the Jewish National Fund, City of

Clinic, Calif. Home in Receda, the 

Emergency Fund and many other local projects.

He visited Israel several times, his recent trip was in 1955, where he visited with Mrs. Friedman and returned a great enthusiast working for the rehabilitation of Israel. 

Above all William is very proud of his Belgium society and his vice 

President Jos. Moller, and also of a

Ladies Social Club, headed by the dynamic chairlady, Mrs. Polia Kuss man as well as of a club for young couples, their leaders being Harry and 

Jack Silvers. 

His daughter, Caroline, received a thorough Jewish education, being able to write and read Yiddish and follows in the footsteps of her parents, being active in many local philanthropic endeavors.

It is also worth noting that William 

Friedman is a verv good influence on the "1945" Club, the "New American" 

Club and the "1939." These organizations are always ready to support 

William Friedman's activities and follow suit. 

His other affiliations are: Trustee of 

B'nai B'rith. Hollywood Lodge, vice prosdient Folks Organizations. Tewish

National Fund. Member Tacob Gozdin Branch. Farband Labor Zionist Org. 

Member Beth-Israel Synagogue, Bev erly Blvd. Generous contributor to the 

Jewish Welfare Fund and many other local charities.

The surviving members of Williams family are: Jack Friedman. recently arrived here from Brazille. Israel, residing here since 1949. and a sister, Jennie Bloch, residing in Paris, France.

SOUTHWEST JEWRY HISTORY OF ZIONISM IN LOS ANGELES By DR. MAX NUSSBAUM From the documents available 

and there are very few in existence it is evident a Zionist Organization was organized in Los Angeles in 1901, "subchartered with the Federation of American Zionists", and called the "Ahabath Zion Society". The first presidents who headed the organization from 1901 to 

1910 were the following: Victor Harris, L. Levitt, G. Bloom, J. S. Urdang, Rev. 

Cohn, A. Shapiro, and Jacob Tobinsky. Harry Fram, fortunately still among us, was the long time Secretary of this Society and served with distinction from the very beginning, over a period of many years. 

From a membership application blank still preserved we learn that the "Ahabath Zion Society is a purely Zionistic organization, and has during the period of its existence contributed large sums to the National Fuhd, and other institutions. At the present the organization in conjunction with the other local societies is working to raise a fund for the purchase of a tract of land in 

Palestina. 

The "Society" met at Forester's Hall at 107½ North Main Street. This "Society" held meetings every Sunday evening with an attendance varying between five and fifty people. One of their invitations lists "games, newspapers, amusements, literary meetings - sharp debates, lectures, music", as incentives to attend .It also shows a remarkable spirit in that "everybody is invited to speak against as well as for Zionism". Membership dues were 25c per month. There are minutes available of a debate in 1910 on the advisability to lower the dues to $1.50 per year. The motion was defeated.

On February 7, 1909, a much advertised "public debate" took place on "Are we a nation or only a religious sect?" A. Kimelfeld debated in the affirmative, and L. Falk the negative part. Philip Stein presided. It is interesting to note that the advertising took place in English as well as in Yiddish. Although Victor 

Harris is listed as the 1st President (1901), the "Ahabath Zion Society" was probably at that time a very small organization, for we have in our possession a membership list of June 1910 which shows the names of the entire membership, totaling 29 people. Of that group A. Rothstein is the only survivor. It is further interesting to note that Victor Harris, spearheading Zionist activities in Los 

Angeles, was the Editor of the B'NAI B'RITH MESSENGER. 

II 

There was, however, another group in Los Angeles operating at the same time: A Young Zionist Group which was founded in March, 1902. Harry Fram, who was the secretary of the "Ahabat Zion Society" was instrumental in forming this young group together with others. Presidents of this young group were: 

Charles Weisbart, Charles Isenstein, Simon Lewis, L. Horowitz, Harry Fram,

Abe Finkelstein. (The "Young Zionists" held reunions in 1955 and in 1956. Quite a number of them are as yet alive.) Their old membership strength was around fifty. This entire group was active for about 25 years as of 1902. Other presidents,

SOUTHWEST JEWRY 

57 at random, of the "Young Zionists" were Ben Davidson, Benjamin Lewis and 

David Blumberg.

As of 1914, the "Young Zionists" continued as "Young Zionist Association." 

The Jewish National Fund stirred the imagination of the young people. The "Jewish Colonial Trust" also profited from their activities. The "YZA" established an installment plan to purchase $5.00 units of shares in the "Jewish 

Colonial Trust". Furthermore, in 1914 another Zionist Group appeared on the horizon. They called themselves, "Sons and Daughters of Zion." In 1914 the "YZA" proposed a merger which was apparently accepted, as nothing later was heard of the "Sons and Daughters of Zion". Also in 1914, Mr. Nathan Strauss visited Los Angeles and persuaded Marco R. Newmark to become the first president of the "Nathan Strauss Palestine Society".

III 

No actual data are available on the foundation date of the Los Angeles Zionist DISTRICT. There was apparently a District Office since 1917 because the B'NAI B'RITH MESSENGER carried a story on a Zionist parade and meeting in that year. We know that on June 27, 1920, a big celebration of the 

Palestine Mandate took place in Exposition Park. The announcement for this meeting shows "A. Shapiro, President, Zionist District of Los Angeles." The 

Zionist District was functioning and very much in evidence in 1924, with approximately 150 to 200 members. There was only one Zionist District at that time. 

Harry Fram was secretary again; and he was one of the first who started the original Golden Book which showed how much was collected for the JNF, etc. 

The president of the ZOA in 1924 was Dr. Charles Wissotzky. The head quarters were first at the Lissner Building and were later moved to the Lincoln Building. Regular meetings were held either at the B'nai B'rith Temple at 9th and Hope Street or at the L. A. Lodge B'nai B'rith Building at 9th and Union. 

ZOA presidents were: beginning with 1924 - Dr. Charles Wissotzky, Dr. 

George J. Saylin, Ben M. Goldman, Aaron Riche (2 years), Dr. Louis G. 

Reynolds, Herman A. Bachrack, Dr. Jacob Kohn, Gustave L. Goldstein (2 years), Jacob J. Lieberman, I. Irving Lipsitch, Judge B. J. Scheinman, Dr. Jacob Sonderling, Dr. Leon J. Tiber, Harry Bauman, Isidore Lindenbaum, Albert Silverman, Jacob M. Alkow, Cyrus Levinthal, Benjamin M. Bendat, Samuel B. Steinbach.

IV 

In 1947 the first Zionist Region was established under the name of The Southern Pacific Region, and Gustave L. Goldstein was its first president. It was comprised, among others, of Pasadena, Long Beach, San Diego, Huntington 

Park, West Adams, City Terrace, Hollywood and Beverly Hills.

In 1951 The Western States Region was formed under the presidency of Jacob M. Alkow. It consisted of the former Southern Pacific Region and the 

Northern Pacific Area and is still in existence. The states of the Region are: 

Arizona, California, Colorado, Idaho, Montana, Nevada, New Mexico, Oregon, Utah, Washington, and Wyoming.

ZOA Regional Presidents from 1947 to 1957 (in order): 1947-48-Gustave 

Goldstein, 1948-49-Samuel Deutsch, 1949-50-Cyrus Levinthal, 1950-51-Samuel *Judge Scheinman was the first president of the so-called consolidated districts Hollywood and Los Angeles. Hollywood District then broke up and they again became one District with Los Angeles.

SOUTHWEST JEWRY Lackman, 1951-52-Jacob M. Alkow, 1952-53-Dr. Max Nussbaum, 1953-54-Dr. 

Herz N. Mantchik, 1954-55-56-57-Rabbi Jacob M. Ott. 

The first director of the ZOA was Harry May who was succeeded by Louis Stein, Jess Nathan, and Dr. Walter Platz. The latter is still the Executive Director of the Western States Region. 

Active Districts in operation now are: ARIZONA: Phoenix-Brandeis, Tucson; 

CALIFORNIA: (Greater Los Angeles) -Los Angeles, Bay Cities, Beverly Hills, 

Brandeis-Hollywood, Theodor Herzl, Huntington Park, Israel Zionist Society, Roosevelt, Valley, Chaim Weitzman, Ivrim (Young Marrieds), Masada (Young 

Adults), Rishon (Young Adults); Others, Citrus Valley, East Bay, Long Beach, Pasadena, Sacramento, San Diego, San Francisco, Stockton; COLORADO: Den ver; NEVADA: Las Vegas; NEW MEXICO: Albuquerque; OREGON: Portland; 

UTAH: Salt Lake City; WASHINGTON: Seattle. 

The Jewish National Fund has played a major role in the ZOA and has been one of its main activities. Aaron Riche was chairman of the JNF in 1933; 

Dr. Arthur Turbow in 1934. Theodore Strimling was elected in Nov., 1934 and is now going into 23 years of presidency of the JNF. He was also treasurer of 

JNF for 15 years.

In 1933 the ZOA consisted of 150 members; today (1957), there is a total of 13 districts in greater Los Angeles, a total of 29 districts in the over-all Western States Region of the ZOA, with an approximate membership of 4,500. Up until the beginning of the Second World War, the individual Zionist groups in this area had no coordinating body and did not work together on matters of common interest, except in matters of fund raising for the Jewish National Fund and to a certain extent for the United Palestine Appeal. 

When the political situation became a matter of urgency and emergency in dealing with the British White Paper, a Zionist Emergency Council was formed in 1942 under the chairmanship of Theodore Strimling, with the participation of all existing Zionist groups such as ZOA, Hadassah, Labor Zionist Movement, Mizrachi and Revisionists. As a parallel organization operating in the non-Jewish community on political matters regarding the Jewish homeland in Palestine,a group of prominent Christian leaders established the Los Angeles Committee on Palestine, which cooperated very closely with the Zionist Emergency Council in the matter of issuing statements, sponsoring public meetings and other public relations activities required by the situation. 

In 1948, following the establishment of the State of Israel and in line with the national organizational pattern, the Emergency Council was reorganized and in its place was created the American Zionist Council of Los Angeles. Mr. 

Theodore Strimling served as the first chairman of the new council until 1949, when he resigned, and was replaced by Dr. Max Nussbaum, who took over as new Chairman. Dr. Nussbaum has been assisted by Dr. Otto Schirn, who first served as Secretary of the Council for four years, and later on as Vice-Chairman. 

Dr. Schirn is now Regional Director of the American Zionist Council for Southern California, Arizona, New Mexico, Southern Nevada, and El Paso, Texas.

SOUTHWEST JEWRY DR. MAX NUSSBAUM 

Dr. Max Nussbaum is a graduate of the Breslau Jewish Theological Semin ary, where he received his Rabbinical

Degree in January, 1934. He studied 

Philosophy at German Universities and received his Ph.D., summa cum laude. 

At the early age of twenty-five, Dr. Nussbaum was called to Berlin to serve as Rabbi of the Great Jewish 

Congregation of Berlin, where he stayed from 1934 to 1940. He was, at that time, the youngest Rabbi in Germany. 

Dr. Nussbaum lectured extensively throughout Europe, was a regular contributor to the German, Jewish and European Jewish Press. During these years, he published books on "The 

Philosophy of Max Adler" and on "The

Nationalism of Yehuda Ha'Levi." 

Dr. Nussbaum was connected with the Zionist Movement from his early youth and was one of the leading figures of the Zionist Organization in 

Germany, a member of its Executive

Board and its Chairman for the Jewish 

National Fund. 

Through Dr. Stephen S. Wise, Dr. 

Nussbaum was brought to this country. Dr. Wise had met him in London at the occasion of the Jewish-Arab 

Conference in 1939. Dr. Nussbaum arrived in the United States in August, 1940. He served as Rabbi of a Congregation in Oklahoma, and Director of the Jewish Students' Center at the 

State University of Oklahoma. Simultaneously, Dr. Nussbaum became a member of the faculty and lectured on philosophy. 

In September, 1942, Dr. Nussbaum was appointed to the pulpit of Temple 

Israel of Hollywood.

During his many years in the United 

States, Dr. Nussbaum has lectured. from coast to coast to hundreds of Jewish and non-Jewish organizations, civic clubs, universities and colleges. 

He has contributed articles to the 

American Jewish Press.

In the critical years of the Hitler regime, Rabbi Nussbaum, as contact man for Professor Chaim Weitzman, brought reports constantly from Ger- many to London on the situation of 

German Jews. When he arrived in the 

United States, he was asked to give a complete detailed report on European Jewry to the Government in Washington.

In the Spring of 1953, he was selected by the United Jewish Appeal to visit Israel, Berlin, and Paris in order to report to the American Jewish Community on what he had seen. Since his return, he has made several speaking tours throughout the United States on behalf of the U.J.A., the J.N.F., and the Z.O.A.

Among the many important posts that Dr. Nussbaum has held and now holds are: Associate Chairman, National Executive of the Z.O.A.; National Vice-President, Zionist Organization of America; President, Southern California Association of Liberal 

Rabbis; Vice-President, Hebrew Union College, Jewish Institute of Religion, California School; Member, National Executive, American Jewish 

Congress; Member, Commission on

Religous Organization, National Con ference of Christians and Jews; Chairman, Speakers' Bureau, United Jewish

SOUTHWEST JEWRY 

61 

Appeal of Los Angeles; President, dent, Western Assoc. of Reform Rabbis; and Past President of Western

States Region of the Zionist Organiza tion of America. 

His appointment as National Vice 

President of the Zionist Organization of America in 1953 was the first time in the history of American Zionism that such an honor was bestowed on a member from the West Coast. This was in recognition of his great contribution to the Zionist Movement.

At the last National Convention of the Zionist Organization of America in 1956, in Washington, D.C., Dr.

Nussbaum was elected to the post of 

Associate Chairman of the National 

Executive Committee of the Z.O.A. He is holding this position together with 

Abraham Goodman as chairman of the

National Executive Committee. 

sanitarium, now known as the City of Hope, Folk Shulle, which institution he kept up with his own personal funds, first secretary of the

Jewish war relief, and at the time of the Sokolow-Warburg delegation, he actually neglected his practice as well as his health, and dedicated himself to the wonderful Zionist cause. 

DR. LEO BLASS 

1883 - 1937 

Born in 1883 in Rastov, Russia, his father was a well educated man and a talented poet, a contributor to the well known Hebraic newspaper "Hamelitz". 

Received his early education under his father's personal supervision, up to the age of fifteen. A decendent of the Wilno Gaun, third generation. 

Left Russia in his early youth, and has traveled a great deal. 

Went through Switzerland, Italy, France and Germany, where he firstcompleted him medical studies. After his arrival in the U.S. he took a postgraduated course at St. Louis, Mo.

After his arrival in California in 1908, he also did post-graduate work in 

San Francisco. 

Dr. Blass was indeed one of the new builders of California Jewr

There is hardly a Jewish institution here that can not claim him as a founder and supporter from the first vear he settled here. 

One of the organizers of the Duarte

One of the few Jewish learned men who followed everything concerning

Jewish welfare and culture throughout the world. A contributor to the 

Yiddish press and various periodicals 

Dr. Blass in the latter part of his life devoted much of his time to the study of philosophy and to writing both for local and Eastern publications. He volunteered for war service, but was rejected because of failing health. He lectured extensively for local groups and gave two series of 

Jectures on Tewish literature and Jewish problems under the sponsoring of the Los Angeles public library. In 

1948 he fulfilled a lifelong ambition to revisit Europe and to see the new land of Israel. where he was an honored guest of the Histadruth, and wrote his daughters enthusiastic letters about the Israeli accomplishments. While in Israel he was stricken with a heart attack and died on 

August 20. 1948. His body rests in 

Safed among those he loved and worked for.

SOUTHWEST JEWRY HARRY CHAPMAN Residence: 1534 Indian Trail, Palm 

Springs, California. Mr. Chapman was born in Brusilow, near Kiev, Russia, May, 1, 1886. It was there that he received his education in cheder, and later studied for himself and above all he was from his early youth influenced by the Psalms, known as the "Songs of David." 

Arrived in Chicago in 1906, a youth of twenty, remaining there until 1931, engaged as the owner of the Oriental 

Rug Cushion Company, and in 1931 he settled in Los Angeles, opening a branch of the same firm.

Harry Chapman is the author of four little volumes known under the title: "Life, Wisdom, Truth and Happiness."

These volumes are a collection of verses in the Psalms in which life, wisdom, truth and happiness are extolled, and appended to each section are wonderful quotations from the 

Jewish Sages.

The volumes are dedicated to his children, Edward and Selma King, Raymond and Frances Chapman, and their children Stephani Linda-Roger 

Lewis, Paul Arnold King and Robert 

Michael Chapman.

These four little volumes are certainly a rich heritage that not only the Chapman family should be proud of, but they are being cherished by thousands of readers who find solace and comfort in these wonderful quotations and interpretations were made by the Author, Harry Chapman.

We were interested to learn how the author conceived the idea of creating these little wonderful volumes and here is the story: 

About 17 years ago Mr. Chapman was very ill and being in pain he tried to find comfort in the Psalms, the songs of David which always influenced and inspired him. It was during his period of illness that he conceived the idea that the Psalms are a true balsam to the heart and a great comfort to the tormented soul.

He then confided his idea to the late Rabbi Solomon Neches, a very dear personal friend. The Rabbi encouraged Harry Chapman who then started to do the research work and kept busy with the compilations for a period of ten years.

The volumes saw a second edition recently, they keep the author in a healthy physical shape and in the best spirit. 

Harry Chapman is also known for his love of music, is a great supporter of Jewish education, a contributor to all Jewish philanthropic causes and above all is known for his modesty and love for culture, art and literature. 

A. S. WEINER 

A devoted and dedicated Zionist since Bar-Mitzva, Abraham Wiener settled in Texas, 50 years ago, married 

Ell in 1909 in Houston, and when the first son was born in 1910, they named him Raphael Hertzel, after the father of political Zionism.

In 1955 A. S. Wiener was presented with a J.N.F. plaque at a Beverly 

Hills Dinner-Dance for his devotion.

SOUTHWEST JEWRY 

65 They lived and loved in their Zionism and begat children in their Zionism which is tantamount to Living 

Judaism. 

Abraham and Ella moved to San 

Antonio in 1911.

Abraham and Ella visited Israel in 1949.

Today he is not only on the Board of the Beverly Hills Z.O bust also on the Executive Committee of the Fellowship for Jewish Culture, a member of the Santa Monica Temple, etc., etc. 

HARRY FRAM 

Born in Shavil, Kovno, Russia, in 

1880. Received a Jewish education until he was 18 years old at the Yeshibah. Later he became a bookkeeper in a department store. 

In San Antonio, Abraham, of course, became President of the Zionist Organization. Like his namesake he understood the call Lech Lacha Marzecha Umeladetecho el Ha-aretz asher arechah!

And being a Zionist, he was of course interested in Jewish Education.

He was a leader in the San Antonio Hebrew Institute, and chairman of the Sunday School (Religious School) 

Board of Temple El for many years. 

In that capacity he spoke at Jewish Chataquas and wrote papers for conferences on Jewish Education. 

Abraham Weiner is highly respected in the San Antonio Community as an ethical business man and as an upstanding citizen. He worked many years as a leader of the Bexar County Tuberculosis Association and was appointed by the President of the U. S. on the Housing Committee for Slum 

Clearance. During the Depression he helped many people to stay in business -among them people of many races- 

Mexican, Negro and Chinese.

From his early boyhood he was deeply interested in Zionism, and this became his life's task in later days. 

He is responsible for Zionism on the 

Pacific Coast. 

Instrumental in organizing the Young Zionist, he also worked out the idea of the Golden Book in Los 

Angeles, a constructive factor in raising funds here for the Jewish National 

Fund, active in the L. A. Achuzah.

At the occasion of the visit of Mr. and Mrs. Nathan Straus to Los Angeles he arranged for a conference, which resulted in organizing a Nathan 

Straus Palestine Advance Society, with 

Mr. Marco R. Newmark as leader.

SOUTHWEST JEWRY DR. LOUIS GEORGE REYNOLDS 

1870 - 1947 

Born in Lithuania, March 17, 1870, a son of Abraham Jacob and Gute 

Freda (nee Weinstone) Reynolds, who died in this country some years ago.

The early training of Dr. Reynolds was obtained at the Yeshiva. When he was 16 years old he went to the 

Zurich University, Switzerland, where for two years he studied philosophy and philology. At the Sarbonne in 

Paris he studied for two more years. 

In 1890, being twenty years old, he went to Louisville, Kentucky, and taught public school for one year. He next took up residence in New York 

City and commuted to Newark, New 

Jersey, every day to teach Hebrew in the school of Temple Congregation 

Oheb Sholem. He afterwards became rabbi of this institution.

For his work at the Broca Hospital he received special mention. 

Returning to Sacramento, he remained until 1917, when he moved to 

Los Angeles. While yet in Sacramento, in 1901, he was married to 

Belle Zemansky. He had three sons: 

Dr. Fred, writer; Josephus, graduated from the University of California, M.D. and Avrom. 

He was a past president and trustee. of B'nai B'rith Lodge, and for years. he has been president of Temple Sinai. 

Vice-president of Los Angeles Branch of the Keren Hayseod. Past chairman of the Board of Education of the Mod ern Talmud Torah and past director of the Federation of Jewish Welfare Organizations. 

Passed away June 27, 1947.

MRS. L. G. REYNOLDS 

1902 - 1948 

Belle Zemansky Reynolds, born 

June 8, 1880, in Sacramento, California, was educated in the public schools of that city. Married Dr. Louis G. 

Reynolds, January 21, 1902. Three children. 

In 1900 he transferred his activities as rabbi to the Temple of Bedford, Pennsylvania, and later to that of 

Salt Lake City. In 1904 he entered the school of medicine of Northwestern University of Chicago, graduating in 1908. The year following was spent in practicing at Wesley Hospital. 

At this time he went to Sacramento, where he practiced until 1913. From this time he widened his experience by post-graduate work in Paris and London, at the Broca and Necker hospitals of the former and the Queen 

Charlotte's Hospital of the latter city. 

Mrs. Reynolds was an active worker and organizer, her wonderful spirit making her one of the most able leaders of Los Angeles. President of 

Ladies' Auxiliary, I. O. B. B., past president of Sinai Sisterhood, board member of Council of Jewish Women,

Sinai Sisterhood. Hadassah, Social

67 Medical Service, and member of B'nai 

B'rith Sisterhood, Hebrew Sheltering and Home for the Aged, Modern Talmud Torah, Philanthropy and Civics 

Club, Native Daughters of the Golden West and the Order of Eastern Star. 

Passed away Sept. 14, 1948.

AARON SHAPIRO 

1883-1933 

Born of merchant parents, September 6, 1883, in Kremenchug, Russia, he is a direct descendant of the "Tosfas Yomtov," and also attended the Minsk Yeshivah. He attended 

Chedar and a Russian school in 

Kremenchug, and later went to Charkow. He was not admitted to the university entry examinations at Charkow on account of his political views, and after he had organized a selfdefense for Jews at Charkow he was arrested by the czarist government and compelled to leave Russia. He has actively participated in the Russian revolutionary movement, and organized the first Workers' Zionist 

Organization in Charkow when only 

15 years of age. 

In 1904 he arrived in New York.

He supported himself as well as he could by selling papers and attended school until 1908, when he came to

Los Angeles. By working and going to school in the evening he was soon able to attend the University of Southern California, from which he was graduated, and was admitted to the bar. He was a practicing attorney, since 1916. 

Married Jennie Kraus, and they had one son, Israel. 

Mr. Shapiro was the past district chairman of the Workmen's Circle, one of the founders of the Jewish

Consumptive Relief Sanitarium, as well as a member of its board of directors and its financial secretary.

He was also an organizer of the 

Yiddishe Folk Shule. He was ap pointed special authorized organizer for the states of California and Ari zona for the Jewish People's Relief. 

He was a delegate to the Jewish 

National Workers' Conference in New 

York in 1915. He was a delegate from Los Angeles to the Second Jewish Congress in 1920, and was the chairman for the district work for the Congress.

He has been directly responsible for the organization of the Poalei Zion in 

New York, also the Poalei Zion branch in Los Angeles, and helped to organize the Legal Aid Society and the 

Junk Peddlers' Protective Association in Los Angeles. Passed away Dec. 31, 1933, survived by son, Paul Jarico. 

CHAIM SHAPIRO 

Born of merchant parents in Kremenchug and Charkow, Russia, in 1886, and a student of the Minsk Yishivah. 

In 1906 he arrived in New York, where he began to sell newspapers with his brother in order to be able to attend school, until he was graduated from the Eron Preparatory School of 

New York.

After his arrival in Los Angeles in 

1908, he was enrolled in the University of Southern California. He was admitted to the bar in 1912. 

Married Basha Leslie, by whom he has one daughter, Violet Shulasmith, and one son David Israel, now serving in U.S. Army. 

Chaim Shapiro is a member of the

SOUTHWEST JEWRY 

Jewish Community Council of Los Angeles, where he served for many years. He was in the past a Vice President when Judge Holtzer was the 

President of the Community Council. 

He is a member of the Bureau of 

Jewish Education. He is the President of the Histadrut Campaign of Los An geles, and is the Chairman of the

Yiddish Scientific Institute at Los 

Angeles. He was the Chairman of the Ort for many years continuously in the past. He was one of the founders of hte Yiddish Folkshule of Los Angeles and was its principal in the early years of its existence. 

He ran for Lieutenant Governor on the Socialist ticket of the State of 

California in 1932 and ran for Mayor of the City of Los Angeles in 1933. 

He is called upon very frequently to speak on behalf of many Jewish 

Philanthropic causes.

DR. WILLIAM OSTROWSKY 

1887-1947 

Dr. William Ostrowsky was born in 

Pinsk, Russia, in 1887, a son of Jacob and Rachel Ostrowsky, his father being an attorney, held Young William under his influence but William had ideas of his own, and very early in life became interested in the movement of the Teritorialist, which was forbidden in Russia, and in 1911 he was arrested by the Zarist government, and exiled from Pinsk. 

His friends in the United States helped him to escape Russia, and brought him over to the United States in 1912. 

Attended Eron Preparatory school in New York, and in 1917 he married 

Clara Isenberg and they both moved. to Los Angeles. It was here that Clara went to work, helping her husband to complete his studies, graduating from the Östheopathic College. 

Their children are: Herman Ostrow, 34, dentist, and Adeline Leshin, a graduate from U.C.L.A. and from the 

Jewel Music school. 

Dr. Ostrowsky was one of the founders of the Yivo, a past president of the L. A. Yiddish Culture Club, in fact one of the founders of the Culture Club. He was also very much interested in Jewish education, helping along the movement of the Yiddishe Folkshullen in L.A.

Dr. Ostrowsky was the author of a volume: The Sexual Life of the Fe male." He also contributed many articles to various magazines. He received much praise in the Historical 

Pinkus "Thousand Years Pinsk." 

Passed away Nov. 16, 1947, and was mourned by all who came to know and love him.

MILTON SPERLING 

Milton Sperling, new Chairman of the University of Judaism's Board of 

Overseers, is President and founder of United States Pictures, Inc., an independent motion picture company releasing through Warner Bros. 

Now 41, Sperling was born in New 

York City July 6, 1912, and came serves. As an officer in charge of a combat photographic unit, he took part in D-Day at Bougainville, and at 

New Georgia and Treasure Island. He was one of the group who filmed and produced the documentary accounts of U. S. Marine Corps in action "Tarawa" and "To The Shores of Iwo Jima. 

Sperling married Betty Warner, daughter of Harry M. Warner, on 

June 12, 1939. They have three children Susan Ellen, born Dec. 4, 1941; Karen Jo, born April 8, 1945, and Deborah, born March 8, 1948.

In addition to his University of Judaism duties, he is a member of the Board of Directors of the Brandeis 

Camp Institute; on on the Research 

Council for Cedars of Lebanon Hospital, and a member of the Wilshire 

Blvd. Temple and Hillcrest Country 

Club.

Among pictures he has produced for his own company are "Blowing 

Wilk," "Retreat, Hell," "The Enforcer," and "Cloak and Dagger." Earlier, he had produced "Sun Valley Serenade," "To the Shores of Tripoli,' "Hello, Frisco, Hello," "Crash Dive" and other gems for Twentieth-Century Fox.

77 

DORE SCHARY 

Dore Schary, former M-G-M studio. head, was born in Newark, N. J., August 31, 1905. He was the youngest of the four children of Herman Hugo and Belle Schary, who had journeyed to the United States in search of the freedoms and opportunities it promised. 

While Schary attended Newark public schools, his parents were building up a catering business and opening "Schary Manor", distinctive in

Newark's social life, a place of good food, weddings and whatnot. This was a family affair, father, mother, brothers, sisters all pitching in with the management. 

To the youngest son fell the hatcheck concession. He also doubled as entertainer, singing with his sister for parties, his first taste of things theatrical.

For awhile he worked on the Newark Sunday Call and also became a publicity man for Admiral Byrd's first tour. In 1928, he developed an interest in the theatre. He became an actor with the Stuart Walker Stock Company in Cincinnati, later playing on 

Broadway in "Four Walls" and "The 

Last Mile".

He also directed little theatre shows, playing the vacation season "Borscht 

Circuit", and wrote plays that never were produced, although he sold one manuscript four separate times. Eventually, he saw one of his plays produced, "Too Many Heroes", to critical acclaim, but box-office apathy. 

But, if his plays weren't exactly lighting up the Broadway skies, one did kindle a spark in Hollywood. 

Walter Wanger had read "One Every

Minute", thought it packed vitality, had the writer signed to a contract at Columbia Studios for $100.00 a week. When brawny, six-foot Dore 

Schary arrived, Wanger was dumbfounded. He had expected "Dore" to be a girl. 

When he landed in Hollywod,

Schary was possessed of high hopes, $36.00, two clean shirts and a bride. 

For, on March 5, 1932, he had married Miriam Svet, talented young pianist and artist, who lived down the street from him for a number of years before they finally met and fell in love. Today, in their Brentwood home, are three children - Jill, Joy and Jeb.

The welcome mat at the front door reads "Schary's Manor", originally from his late parents' Newark pride, happy fulfillment of the promise this land of America held to immigrants from foreign shores.

In 1936, Schary went to work at 

M-G-M, writing "Big City", in which 

Spencer Tracy and Luise Rainer starred. His next at that studio was "Boys Town", which won him an 

Academy Award, and proved the turning point in his career. With acclaim for the Father Flanagan picture, Schary next wrote "Edison the Boy" and "Edison, the Man". He then was elevated to executive rank, sharing with Harry Rapf one of the studio's production units. Under this regime such films as "Joe Smith, American", "Journey for Margaret", "Lassie Come 

Home", "Lost Angel" and "Bataan" were produced.

Firmly established on his record as one of Hollywood's top-ranking production minds of constantly increasing stature, it was logical that Schary return to M-G-M where his creative talent and executive ability had first budded so promisingly. Schary became vice-president in charge of production at M-G-M in the summer of

SOUTHWEST JEWRY 

1948. In July, 1951, he assumed additional responsibilities with his appointment by Nicholas M. Schenck, president of Loew's M-G-M, as vicepresident in charge of production and studio operations, acting as chairman of the studio executive board. 

As his first activity, Schary literally read hundreds of scripts and story properties. Out of this came a farsighted and long-range production plan that assured and has maintained M-G-M's pre-eminent position in the motion picture industry. 

Schary's approach to production is guided by numerous convictions. He often has stated that there are no substitutes for enthusiasm, imagination and entertainment that dares to be different. He has a high respect for the intelligence of the American audience. He believes, without reservation, that any sacrifice of quality in picture making is economically unsound. 

Real efficiency of production operation can only be obtained, he contends, by the most careful selection of story material, through preparation of scripts and closest possible teamwork between the creative people and craftsmen responsible for the making of pictures.

Among his first activities at M-G-M, Schary personally produced "Battleground", which received Academy 

Award nomination. Despite his many activities and responsibilities, Schary continues to keep his hand in productions with particular appeal for him. 

He also produced personally "Westward the Women", "Plymouth Adventure", epic sea story of the Mavflower's vovage. "Dream Wife". "Go For 

Broke!". "Take the High Ground!" and "Bad Day at Black Rock".

Public-spirited, socially-conscious and civic-minded. Scharv is a leader in many national, religious, humanitarian and industrial organizations. 

For 1951. 1952 and 1953. he was 

Chairman of the Permanent Charities 

Committee. Among his diversified affiliations are the University Religious 

Conference of UCLA. Tewish Theo logical Seminary, Motion Picture In dustry Council, National Democratic

Party, United Jewish Welfare Fund, past vice-president and a member of the Board of Governors of the Aca demy of Motion Picture Arts and 

Sciences, member of the Executive

Committee of the Welfare Federation of Los Angeles and Hollywood Chair man, American Relief for Korea. He is also on the advisory council of the 

Southern California branch of the

American Civil Liberties Union, and is a member of the National Committee on Immigration and Citizenship. 

Schary received the Look Magazine

Award as the screen's outstanding producer of 1948, and in 1949, Look's 

Achievement Award as the executive of the year. He won this Look Industry Award again in 1954. “Battleground" brought him many awards including a special citation from the Department of the Army, the film's selection by the Newspaper Critics of Liege, Belgium, as the best picture of the 1949-50 season, the Photoplay 

Gold Medal as the Most Popular Picture of 1950 and the 101st Airborne

Division Plaque. 

In 1950, he received a Distinguished 

Achievement Award from the Japan ese-American Citizens League, a special citation and gold medallion for producing "Go Fod Broke!", and in 

1951, awards from both the veterans of the 442nd and 100th Regimental 

Combat Teams for the same film.

The College of the Pacific conferred upon Schary in 1951, the Honorary

Degree of Doctor of Humane Letters. 

He also received the University of 

Judaism Citation in "Recognition of his Distinguished Service in the

Founding of the West Coast Branch of the Jewish Theological Seminary of 

America." In 1954 he was accorded the high honor of being invited to a 

Fellowship at Brandeis University, which he accepted.

Outstanding among the honors received by Schary was Redbook's 13th 

Annual Movie Award presented to

Schary and the M-G-M Studio "For Their Production of Fine Films 

Throughout 1951."

79 As a public service, Schary in 1952 produced a documentary film called "The Hoaxters" which traced the history of Communism. The picture, to the surprise of some, was also a widely-circulated film, and enjoyed commercial engagements in theatres all over the country. It, too, received many awards and citations, as well as being nominated for an Academy Award. It is still being run by various groups - military, civic, religious and patriotic organizations. cited the picture as an outstanding contribution to the American way of life.

ENTS. among others. He is sought out as a speaker on far more occasions than he could possibly accommodate. Even so, he will average roughly 15 speeches in a year, and will give as much time to an address before a congregation of 50 at a small Valley church as he will addressing millions of people as he did recently on the NBC Lecture 

Series. 

JERRY LEWIS 

In the course of its filming, Jerry passed his 25th anniversary since the day on Sept. 17, 1931, when-at the age of five-he made his trouper debut by toddling on stage to sing a chorus of "Brother, Can You Spare a Dime".

In one way or another, he has been almost continuously employed as an entertainer ever since. 

Jerry was born to show folks, so it figures that he had an early start in his profession. His father Danny

Lewis was and still is a nightclub and theater headliner whose act, labeled 

Songs Nostaligic, consists largely of singing ballads of the 1920's in what has been aptly described as a vaudeville baritone. He concludes each performance with a parody rendition of "I've Got a Million Dollar Baby," in which he does a creditable imitation of son Jerry. There was a time a few seasons back that Jerry was playing the

Paramount Theater in New York, and his father was on the variety bill at the Palace Theater just across Broadway. The coincidence led one reviewer to handle it like a maternity item: "Both father and son," the piece read, "are doing fine." 

Son Jerry was brought up in Newark and Irvington, N.J. The family name originally was Levitch, and 

Jerry himself started out with the name Joseph Levitch. 

Jerry's mother played the piano at radio station WOR, and did her husband's music arrangements. Parent 

Danny Lewis sang in burlesque houses, and functioned as master of ceremonies at Catskill Mountain Borsch Belt hostelries. It was at one of these, the President Hotel at Swan 

Lake, N.Y., that Jerry made his stage debut in support of his parents. He did not at once set the world a-fire. 

Jerry is still remembered at Irvington High School as one of the zaniest cheer leaders in the history of the institution. His classmates called him 

Id-short for Idiot. Jerry relishes the memory, and the nickname, and even

SOUTHWEST JEWRY 

now refers somewhat obliquely to his screen performances as "that idiot up there." Needless to say, his screen personality to the contrary notwithstanding, he is not an idiot. Far from it. 

Scratch through the surface of his carefully assumed imbecilic front and you will find an extraordinarily intelligent, serious-minded, highly creative individual. So much so that Paramount entrusted him with the overall responsibility of producing his first picture as a single, and the motion picture industry at the same time honored him with the prestigious chairmanship of its Permanent Charities annual funds drive. In addition to which he put renewed effort into his continuing voluntary spearheading of the national funds drive of the Muscular Dystrophy Association, and other important extra-curricular activities which evidence his new maturity.

It was in July of 1946 that lightning struck-lightening that took the form of what soon was to become one of the greatest acts in show business, Martin and Lewis. Jerry was appearing at the 500 Club in Atlantic City when one of the acts on the bill quit. 

Jerry had met Dean Martin when they both worked at the Glass Hat in New York. Things were dull in Atlantic City, so Jerry hit up the club proprietor: "Why don't you hire this Dean 

Martin? He's a friend of mine." "I don't need singers," D'Amato replied. "But this guy is a comic, too," Jerry lied. "We've done a lot of routines together." "If he's a comic I can use him," said D'Amato. 

The lightening didn't strike right at once. Jerry did his usual record act. and Dean simply sang.

After a week of this, D'Amato was out of patience. "This is funny?" he challenged. "If there's no comedy tonight, you're both fired." 

Jerry Lewis and Dean Martin stayed teamed for ten vears, in the course of which they did 16 pictures, innumerable television shows, and nightclub appearances almost beyond count. 

Then they decided to go their separate ways-or at least as separate as certain contracts which call for them as a team may permit.

So he improvised, and the audience loved it. They were stamping their feet and pleading for more when, after more than an hour of giving his greatest performance, Jerry took his bow.

He knew now for sure that they'd like him alone. 

Jerry and Patti, now married 12 years (since Oct. 3, 1944), have three children, Gary, born July 31, 1945; 

Ronnie, born December 29, 1949; and 

Scott Anthony, born February 22, 1956. Ronnie was adopted. 

Jerry is six feet tall, weighs 175 lbs. has black hair and brown eyes. He had a crewcut before such male coiffures became commonplace. His principal hobby is photography, and his "GaRon Productions", consisting of 16-mm. sound movies that he and his friends make spoofing Hollywood's big pictures, are collectors' items among the host of friends who visit the hospitable Lewis menage in Pacific Palisades. 

Jerry's camera camera equipment, completely equipped lab and projection room, have a serious purpose too, he insists. When the time comes that he becomes a film director-and he has already had tempting offers-he will know from practical experience how to direct.

THE CITY OF HOPE STORY 

The City of Hope is more than a house of healing – more than a medical center and research institute: of Jewish origin and heritage, it is an example, for all Americans, of nonsectarian service, tolerance, compassion and understanding.

Day-to-day contributions from men, women and children in every walk of life reaffirm the belief that man is, indeed, his brother's keeper. And in this belief, there is hope.

Hope, because the People's Movement which has helped the City of Hope achieve new stature in the war against catastrophic disease, provides the strength and sinew for the ceaseless struggle. Hope, because the People's 

Movement regards the patient - no matter where his or her home in the nation as a fellow-human in need of every resource. 

The City of Hope story began, really, more than four decades ago, in 1913, when tuberculosis a deadly Reaper with no regard for humans lives - was taking its toll on the streets of congested cities. 

One day in Los Angeles, a small group of people - sickened by the tragic sight of one of their fellows who had collapsed in the streets - took matters into their own hands. They pooled their meager resources, raising enough to buy a bit of land out on the nearby desert. There they pitched two tents and set about, as best as possible, to care for the pitifully ill, without charge and without question of race or creed. 

Even then, in the face of almost impossible odds, they dared dreamthese humanitarian pioneers of a medical facility that would take its place among the great hospitals of the nation, while espousing the same philosophy that had prompted the pitching of the tents. 

Slowly, through the years, as more and more people everywhere took active roles in the dream, it began to lose its wraith-like qualities and assume reality.

In time, a few bungalows appeared where the tents had stood. Then more bungalows. Then stucco cottages which, in turn, grew into long, low buildings. 

And, closely pacing the City of Hope growth, was the growth of the People's Movement. From Los Angeles to Long Island. from north to south, auxiliaries were formed and, as they too grew, became the voice of the City of Hope; a mighty voice which echoed across the nation: 

You suffer. That is enough. You belong to us. 

Can we help?

Today, the City of Hope Medical Center and its Medical Research Institute, standing on 85 beautifully landscaped acres, far surpass the dreams of its founders. 

There are no wards all rooms are private or semi-private. The key word in patient care is personalization as opposed to institutionalization, emphasizing the preservation of the dignity of the individual patient. 

Reknowned as a "pilot" Medical Center. City of Hope physicians and scientists are embarked on a relentless search for better understanding and knowledge of the major diseases which afflict mankind, and directing their energies toward new and ever-better techniques by which to treat the suffering. 

Basic as well as clinical research studies are going forward in the fields of biochemistry, cytology, genetics, medicine, microbiology, pathology, radiology and surgery.

An outstanding example of City of Hope ideology made manifest is its

SOUTHWEST JEWRY 

Parent Participation Program whereby mothers and fathers are encouraged to take part in the care of their own stricken child. Parent participation provides significant psychological support to parents and children. 

Another instance of City of Hope pioneering is the Medical Center's compact, radioactive cobalt "bomb" - powerful enough to combat deep-seated cancer, yet small and economical enough to be within financial research of hospitals everywhere. This cancer-fighting unit is housed in a room which has no door. Thus, the patient is not compelled to undergo isolation during cobalt therapy itself a traumatic experience in a time of stress. 

A State-chartered School of Postgraduate Medicine makes available a medium of ever-increasing importance in continued learning and refresher training for the practicing physicians, in the area of long-term illnesses. 

The 1946 convention resolved the expansion of the City of Hope into its present scope of catastrophic diseases. Its Cancer Hospital was inaugurated in 

1950; the Medical Research Institute was establihed in 1952. Cardiac surgery was initiated in 1953, and the Children's Wing was opened in 1954. 

Ever-expanding, the City of Hope completed in 1957, a completely modern, advanced cardiac hospital and clinic for study and treatment of heart disease. 

Other expansion programs include construction of a special playground for patients of the Children's Wing; a rehabilitation center; a village for patients from other areas; and a pioneering home care program for tuberculosis patients. 

In physical size and scope, the City of Hope has progressed - and will progress far beyond the dreams of its founders. But the basic precepts upon which it was founded, and has flourished, remain the same: It still opens its doors to persons of every race and creed. No patient has ever been charged for services rendered. Its policies and functions are still determined by the people through biennial conventions, composed of delegates from City of Hope auxiliaries throughout the Nation, for which it is deservedly known as our country's only democratic philanthropy. 

More than that, with a clearly defined philosophy, it is a "way of life." 

SAMUEL H. GOLTER 

The development of the City of 

Hope from a small sanitorium for the tubercular into its present status as a national medical center of specialized scope, is the result of the work of many.

Among its outstanding leaders is 

Samuel H. Golter. 

Golter was named executive vicepresident of the City of Hope in 1955, after nearly a quarter-century as executive director of the organization. 

Born in Russia in 1890-the seventh son of a seventh son-he came to the 

United States at age 16. He found work as a stockboy in a Chicago picture frame factory, and went to night school to learn more and more of his adopted country. Employed by a national clothing manufacturer, he rose quickly from a subordinate job to positions of broad responsibility, attaining executive status at the time. 

America entered World War I. Golter donned the uniform of the U. S. Army. 

After the Armistice, he turned his back on the business world, and joined

SOUTHWEST JEWRY 

83 the ranks of a private philanthropic agency supervising the distribution of needed goods and funds throughout eastern Europe. After six years of strenuous labor, he returned to this country and California for a needed rest.

A friend interested him in the work of a tuberculosis sanitorium located in the desert near Los Angeles. On his first visit there, he was so moved by the humanitarianism evidenced in the policy that no patient - no matter how long his or her stay - paid for care and medical treatment, that he agreed to give three months of his time and experience in helping to solve the problems of the struggling little institution. 

That was a hot summer's day in 

1926. Sam Golter has been with the 

City of Hope ever since.

He fought through a depression striving to keep the almost bankrupt sanitorium on reasonably firm financial footing. He travelled the Nation seeking funds. interesting people everywhere and in every walk of life in supporting the City of Hope and all that it represented in ideological terms, convincing the populace that: 

Yes, we are our brother's keepers.

While assuming the brunt of administrative chores, Golter was appalled by the overwhelming need for doing somtehing more than merely offering hope to the afflicted. In 1946 he came to the conclusion that the City of Hope should be a focal point in the fight against disease, that this fight would take the form of unsurpassed medical care and, ultimately, significant scientific research against the major catastrophic maladies. The 

Convention in that vear approved this concept and launched a program for a medical center. 

Through Golter's efforts and under his inspiring leadership the City of

Hope has gained reknown as a shining symbol of the Brotherhood of 

Man. And, in most meaningful terms, the City of Hope today yes, and the City of Hope of tomrorow, too - 

is the reffection of his dreams and determination. 

No wonder Sam Golter is called "Mr. City of Hope!"

VICTOR M. CARTER 

The strength of the people's movement which has built the City of Hope into an outstanding medical and research center is nowhere better demonstratd than in the devotion to it which is handed down from parents to children.

Among the leaders and builders of the City of Hope, the Carter name has long borne historical significance. 

When Victor M. Carter was elected president of the Medical Center, he was carrying on the dedicated work begun and still performed by the 

Mark Carters. 

Victor Carter was born in Russia. 

After the Russian Revolution, he fled with his parents to Turkey where he sold shoelaces in the city streets until the family embarked for the United States, and settled in Los Angeles. 

Mark Carter started a hardware business. Young Victor attended the 

17th Street Grammar School and, later 

St. Thomas Junior High. He worked. in his father's store before and after school until he entered the firm on a full-time basis.

Imbued by his parents' interest and enthusiasm, Carter became active at

SOUTHWEST JEWRY 

an early age in City of Hope affairs.

His steady rise from auxiliary worker to head of the City of Hope family was paralleled by his rise as a civic and business leader.

In 1937, while helping to found the 

Sportsmen's Club - one of the major auxiliaries of the City of Hope - he was also engaged in founding his first business, the Vimcar Steel and Sash

Company. He wa selected to the City of Hope Board of Directors at about. the same time his company received the coveted Army-Navy "E" for Excellence Award for outstanding co operation during World War II. 

Carter wah elected to the presidency of the City of Hope by delegates to the Biennial Convention of 1949. It was a post he cherished above all others. At that same time he also became president of what was to become, under his leadership, one of the largest hardware supermarts in the West. 

Much of the City of Hope's growth in recent years can be attributed to that same kind of inspired leadership: business-like energy with the added qualities of wisdom and humility. 

Carter's wide and varied interests include membership on the board of directors of the California Bank, and vice president of the Los Angeles Fire

Commission. But no matter how many directorial posts he has held, no matter how many diverse activities have claimed his attention, he has never stinted in the time he has given the 

City of Hope. 

Victor Carter is symbolic of the people who are the fibre of the free and nonsectarian City of Hope ideology, and under whose aegis the City of Hope will continue to expand as 

a people's movement for the benefit of all the people.

BEN HOROWITZ 

Ben Horowitz, executive director of the CIT YOF HOPE, was born in New York City, March, 1914. He received his education at Brooklyn 

College, St. Lawrence University, and the New School for Social Research, and holds B. A. and LL. B. degrees.

During his career he has been a teacher, attorney, public relations consultant and author, and has specialized in business administration and community relations. Prior to assum ing a top post with the CITY OF 

HOPE, he served as Director of the Council of Organizations of the New 

York Federation of Jewish Philanthropies.

He has been associated with the 

Medical Center since 1945, as Eastern

Regional Director, National Executive 

Secretary and since 1953 as Executive Director.

SOUTHWEST JEWRY THE HISTORY OF MOUNT SINAI HOSPITAL AND CLINIC In This Volume Is Dedicated To
