89 

The Mount Sinai Hospital Story 

Standing at the crossroads of bustling metropolitan Los Angeles, a new landmark, Mount Sinai Hospital and Clinic, with its impressive star of David, offers a shining tribute to American generosity and humanitarianism. 

Westside Mount Sinai Hospital has indeed become a landmark of no small proportions for miles around on every side, with the east and south and northerly approach offering interestingly varied contours of the structure which is the last word in modern architectural beauty and utility. 

New in construction, yet Mount Sinai is not a "new" hospital in the true sense of the word. Actually, it represents the latest or third unit in a series of expansions of a health organization that had its beginnings in 1920 when a tworoom house was converted to serve as a refuge for the stricken during a raging epidemic.

Step by step, through years of hardships and obstacles, the institution emerged as it stands today, a hospital of 100 beds at 831 North Bonnie Beach Place, the original site; a clinic containing 34 departments at 207 North Breed 

Street, caring for approximately 60,000 out-patient visits a year; and now, the westside hospital at 8720 Beverly Boulevard, with its capacity of 253 beds. 

Growth in scope and service can be recognized by the changes in its official name as years went by. Thus the "Home for Incurables" and its suggestion of doom for patients was discarded for the "Mount Sinai Home for Invalids". In 

1930 the corporate name "Mount Sinai Hospital" was adopted, this was changed later to "Mount Sinai Hospital and Clinic" when the Clinic emerged as an offshoot of the hospital in 1940.

Always a people's hospital, built and supported by men and women for the benefit of everyone, it has been this sympathetic, personal support in manpower and funds that has kept it in the forefront as a public health service for 37 uninterrupted years.

The hospital and clinic has always been sustained entirely by voluntary contributions, bequests, grants and auxiliary aid, with very little assistance from any organized welfare group, Jewish or otherwise. 

Some of the original community leaders are still in active support of the hospital, a fact the community may well be proud of, as the very same men and women have been key figures, too, in the growth of industrial 

Los Angeles.

Peter M. Kahn, educator, philanthropist, civic leader, became the hospital's second president, following the four-year term of Max Kaufman, in the '20's. 

Harry Blank, I. B. Benjamin and Alfred Lushing guided the destinies of the hospital.

It was during the trying times of the thirties, when the country became increasingly aware of the role that institutions were playing in the fields of philanthropy and scientific advancement, that Mount Sinai Hospital was recognized for its part in human welfare. 

A milestone in hospital annals was reached in 1937 during Benjamin Tyre's administration. During that year, a Nurses' Home was dedicated as a contribution of the Los Angeles Men's Club, the forerunner of the present Mount Sinai Men's Club, a dynamic group which has become, over the past fifteen years, a staunch pillar of aid for the hospital, playing a leading role in its many expansions.

SOUTHWEST JEWRY 

In November, 1940, a modern 12,000-square-foot Clinic with 22 medical departments was opened on the site it occupies today. 

The following year, 1941, Eugene H. Marcus, attorney and director of the hospital board for many years, was elected president, succeeded two years later by Robert J. Gans who directed brilliantly and successfully for eight successive years.

During these vital years, the budget at the hospital and clinic increased from $76,000 to $550,000 yearly. Many more beds were added; new X-ray laboratories were established; complete pharmacies installed; modern surgeries and research laboratories built and equipped; a dental department installed; a new optical division added; and last, but by no means the least, the latest in scientific equipment was acquired for the pursuance of cancer research. 

During this time, a new wing was annexed to the hospital, doubling its bed capacity which has continuously been filled to capacity. As a matter of record, hospital officials do not remember the time when there was not a long admission waiting list because of insufficient facilities. 

The year 1950 saw the addition of many services and departments in both the hospital and the clinic, in keeping with advancement in medical science. 

Physical Medicine and Rehabilitation, for example, subsequently integrated with Occupational Therapy, had been introduced with success in the Veterans' 

Hospital and soon followed at Mount Sinai.

A tumor board came into prominence at the clinic with its weekly conferences and discussions. Comprised of eminent physicians and surgeons, they meet to diagnose, offer consultation and recommendation for tumor and cancer patients. In recent years, the hospital received honorable mention from the 

California Medical Association's Cancer Commission when it officially recognized and approved the tumor board's accomplishments and work. 

The Clinic, too, was recording progress during this period. Individual departments grew in number until today there are approximately 34 clinics-withina-clinic. One of the newer divisions deals with psychiatric science as applied to treatment, embracing Psychosomatic Medicine, Psychiatric Group Therapy, Play Therapy and Psychosomatic Testing; as well as a Child Problem Clinic for 

Parents.

Mount Sinai Hospital has the unique distinction of probably being the only hospital of its kind in the nation that carries three case-loads of patients, caring for acute or emergency patients; chronic or long-term; and custodials or as formerly referred to "incurables", the classification that includes the terminal cancer patients those who remain in wheel-chair or bed in the hospital for the remainder of their lives. 

During 1950 the dream of a new westside hospital began to take hold in people's minds and hearts.

The trend of the population was swerving westward and the need was growing for a Jewish-sponsored hospital in the area that was swiftly becoming a Jewish center. Community leaders, many of the same men and women who were at the cradle of the hospital and still shouldered responsibility, decided the time was ripe for further expansion with a new facility on the west side. 

The rest is history. Friendship for Robert J. Gans and Mount Sinai impelled Hyman Levine, philanthropist and welfare leader to offer his now famous contribution that will go down in community history as the one great factor, more than any other, that ignited the spark of enthusiasm for city-wide clamor and recognition of the need for a new hospital to relieve the serious shortage prevailing in Los Angeles.

Through the Hyman and Emma Levine Foundation that includes sons Isadore, Dr. Max A. and Sid, the donation represented a gift of three and a

SOUTHWEST JEWRY 

91 

MOUNT SINAL HOSPITAL AND CLINIC

SOUTHWEST JEWRY 

half acres of land valued at approximately $300,000 on Beverly Boulevard at San Vicente, a strategic and highly desirable location for such construction. 

What the Levine Family actually accomplished in health and welfare for the community and all the generations to come cannot be evaluated in mere dollars and cents. Their gift transcends mundane values. 

In 1951, Samuel Pensick, well-known businessman, civic and welfare leader, keyman of the United Jewish Welfare Fund, was elected to the president's post, following Mr. Gans.

Simultaneously, Max E. Mark, executive director of the hospital and clinic during many of its crucial, formative years, who had resigned in 1944, was invited to return to head the greatly expanded institution with its increased program of health services and responsibility. He now assumes the dual post of 

Director of Fund-Raising and Executive Director. 

Successful campaigns for funds were immediately launched. City-wide conferences were staged. Ground was broken, with thousands of citizens witnessing the significant ceremonies. A poignant high spot of the program came when 

Jacob "Pop" Simon, beloved board member and a founder of the institution, was helped from his car and said: "I am happy to have lived to see this day and to be here, although I cannot really see what is going on". He had already gone blind and not long after, passed away at the age of 97. Through the years, "Pop" as he was lovingly called, could be seen on Brooklyn Avenue on the eastside of town soliciting funds for the hospital. When funds were slow in coming, he would dig into his own pockets to help pay the hospital's food and sundry bills.

Ground-breaking was attended by state, city and government officials, civic and welfare personalities, and hundreds of community workers who had a personal interest in the epochal event, through their cooperation. 

Impressive was the multiple ground-breaking sequence, participated in by several dozen presidents from as many supporting auxiliaries, led by Mrs. Tena 

Corenson, society and club woman, long in support of the hospital, who used the same gold shovel she broke ground with at the time the Mount Sinai Clinic wa dedicated in 1940.

Following a successful reign of two and one-half years that witnessed the beginning of construction of the westside hospital, Mr. Pensick was succeeded to the preidency in January 1954 by dynamic George J. Heltzer, land developerbuilder and for close to a quarter of a century board member of the hospital and a top official of the Men's Club. 

At this writing, Heltzer is completing his third term, to be succeeded by Maurice Holman, outstanding civic-welfare and community leader. 

With Heltzer's acceptance of the presidency, construction activity was accelerated. City Council proclamations, "Mount Sinai Days", colorful projects, conferences of city-wide scope and finally one sunny day in August 1955, Los Angeles saw the opening of the modern hospital that represented a dual achievement in providing urgently-needed medical facilities and the construction of a hospital combining beauty in architecture, appointments and utility. 

Bands played, flowers scented the atmosphere, bunting flew from the rafters. The public, many of whom had given so much of themselves to see this dream turned into a reality, were rightfully swelling with pride. Primarily, this was a Jewish achievement, created and constructed for the health benefit of all. 

The structure eight floors high with capacity for 253 beds contains no wards. 

Rooms accommodate from one to four persons and all patients, regardless of paying, non-paying or part-paying, receive equal treatment. 

Ultimately this first unit on the westside will be built up to 13 stories, limit-high, with facilities for 450 patients.

SOUTHWEST JEWRY 

93 Constructed on a foundation to withstand future expansions, the cost, including the Administration Building, came to $4,500,000. 

Surgeries, X-ray laboratories and recovery rooms are below the ground floor as an extra precaution in times of catastrophe. 

Hospital rooms are decorated in modern, natural woods with pastel wall colorings as a note of cheer for patients, departing from the accepted hospital "white."

Oxygen is piped directly into the rooms; a specially designed unit permit patients to communicate with nurses by remote control; or to select a radio station or use their bedside lights by a flick of a button. 

An entire wing is set aside for pediatrics. Decorations to stir the interest and imagination of a child were uppermost in thought in its planning. Rooms are specially designed to speed a child's recovery. At the head of each bed, as in the adult patients' rooms is an oxygen outlet, piped through from a central control room in the basement.

Radio speakers play through patients' pillows, not to disturb other patients. 

A meal-pack food distribution system keeps foods hot for as long as two hours.

And last in the story of Mount Sinai but by no manner or means the least, the institution takes pride in its family of 65 auxiliaries and affiliate groups with their membership of approximately 12.000 men and women. Representing a cross-section of Los Angeles in the west, north, east and southside areas, these are the men and women who have literally kept the doors open to needy sick at the eastside hospital and clinic and are doing the same thing now at the newer hospital on the westside.

On attending meetings or functions. an observer would find it difficult to coincide their vouth. in many instances, their blithe spirits and capacity for fun, with the serious work to which they have dedicated themselves, to save human lives. Their groups range from the sub-teen age class to the sixtv-five and older categorv. all working toward the same end: to maintain a humanitarian hospital. 

Director of this groun of dynamic fund-raisers is Mrs. Toby Silverman who has been on the staff as Auxiliary Director for more than twelve vears. She orgenizes the grouns, counsels and directs them and appears before their meetings and social events to make personal appeals for funds. 

Because of the number of groups and the growth of the hospital organization, a Council of Presidents and Past Presidents of all auxiliaries was recently founded to coordinate the efforts of supporting groups for more effective service to the hospital, the clinic. the patients, auxiliaries one to the other, and the community at large. Mrs. Pauline Mevers heads the new Council. 

Through the many years that Mount Sinai has been serving the community, men and women have always been in the forefront of asistance. As in every civic or welfare cause. there are leaders and there are workers all important, all adding up to the one result serving a worthy cause. 

---

SOUTHWEST JEWRY 

Therefore, to name a few outstanding leaders and to eliminate others, would not be fair evaluation of their valuable aid. But even if such an attempt were made, there would not be enough space to include the many thousands of names. 

Leaders like Emil Brown who has identified himself with innumerable activities for the bettermen of humanity, from coast to coast and overseas, has also done a tremendous job for Mount Sinai with his generous contribution of funds, needed equipment and plaques as stimulus for the interest of other workers for Mount Sinai; and topping it off with the presentation of the magnificent Emil and Anna Brown Auditorium on the first floor of the new 

Administration Building on the hospital grounds. 

Such personality as Phil Goldstone who contributed a generous gift for the endowment of a forty-bed wing in the new hospital; and Mrs. Goldstone who provided the establishment of the gift shop. 

Aaron Riche who has been executive secretary of the institution for more than a quarter of a century, donating time and effort and always on call for counsel or advice.

Esther Katzev, 76 year old humanitarian, to mention one of the auxiliary leaders, who occupied the president's seat of the Mother Auxiliary for twentysix years, besides providing the eastside hospital and clinic with needed equipment through the years of utility or recreational purposes. 

We could go on and on.

These, therefore, are the men and women whom the community can honor for their great role in building, maintaining and expanding a hospital that will benefit generations for years to come.

HYMAN LEVINE 

Hyman Levine, one of the outstanding philanthropists in Los Angeles, was born in Skidle, Poland, April 25, 1883.

It was there that he received his education and later married there his beloved Emma who was always a great inspiration to him aiding and encouraging him in his philanthropic endeavors by which Hyman and Emma will always be remembered by thousands of friends who admire and love them. 

Hyman and Emma Levine arrived in New York in 1903, and remained there for a period of six years.

Following this they came to Los 

Angeles in 1909, establishing themselves in the cooperge business in which they prospered and simultanously they became actively engaged in the affairs of the Jewish community. 

Hyman Levine has since then held numerous offices in local welfare. 

There is hardly an institution in this city that you do not find the Levines among the veterans and pioneers.

The Levines were among the foun ders of the Hebrew Sheltering Home, now known as the Los Angeles Jewish Home for the Aged, having served the Home as vice president and later as its president. 

The names of Hyman and Emma

Levine are closely woven in the his tory of the Mount Sinai Hospital and 

Clinic, having given of their time and wealth to the extent that their name became a blessing and a shining beacon of light that will be cherished by generations to come having proven themselves as great humanitarians. 

The most beautiful things about 

Hyman and Emma Levine is their modesty and the manner in which they make their gifts for the institutions they support. Theirs is the biblical way of walking humble, doing justice and rendering aid where it is being needed. 

Hyman and Emma Levine have proven themselves to be real philanthropists, they always lead and others follow.

Angeles and a Gold Key Man. Mr.

DAVID FAMILIAN 

One of the veterans of orthodox Jewry in Los Angeles, who is still very much remembered and revered, was 

Reb David Familian. Humble, very modest and always eager to relieve his fellow-men of their sufferings. His passing was mourned by many thousands of the old timers in every walk of life. 

Reb David Familian was born in

Skwera, Russia, in 1883, and settled in Los Angeles in 1916.

As soon as he settled here he was not only interested in getting rich, but he was primarily interested in things Jewish, near to his heart, became very much active in the movement of Kashruth as well as in Jewish education. 

At the same time he became interested in the plumbing business in which thanks to his integrity and diligence, it turned out to be a very successful venture.

Reb David was deeply interested in many religious and welfare organizations, he was the president of the East 

Side branch of the Mizrachi Organiza tion, president of the Passover Relief 

Committee (Moes Chitin,) and when the Los Angeles Jewish Community Council came into being, he was elected to the Board of the L.A. J.C.C. He was also a director of the L.A. Jewish 

Academy, Home for the Aged, 325 S.

Boyle Avenue, The Hebrew Free Loan 

Society, Breed Street Synagouge, and 

The Zionist Council. 

All local charities and philanthropies as well as National Jewish Institutions all over the world received his fullest support to his very last days, and it is gratifying to note that both his sons, Isidore and George, are actually following in their venerable father's footsteps, both being very much known as liberal supporters and champions of every Jewish and humanitarian cause. 

Passed away May 10, 1946.

SOUTHWEST JEWRY The History of Los Angeles Jewish Home For The Aged In This Volume Is Dedicated To 

H. LEW ZUCKERMAN 

For His Untiring Love And Devotion

To A Great Humanitarian Cause

SOUTHWEST JEWRY 

101 Los Angeles Home for the Aged The Los Angeles Jewish Home for the Aged at 325 S. Boyle Ave., as the largest of its kind outside of New York City, has won numerous citations from the Community Chest and state and local welfare agencies for "setting standards for other institutions."

Providing the "loving care" for which the Home stands to its more than 307 residents calls for an annual maintenance budget of almost $600,000. That the 

Home continues to grow and continues to set standards for other similar institutions, despite mounting deficits each year, is a tribute to the inspired leadership which has made it possible. 

The couple of small buildings which comprised the Hebrew Sheltering Home for the Aged (as it was then called) in 1927, when our last Jewish "Who's Who" was issued, have been augmented by many many more fine structures in the past three decades.

Around 1930 found building construction completed on the beautiful synagogue which serves the pious residents, as well as on the four-story, 85-room dormitory, and the administration building. 

The Emil Brown annex was constructed shortly after World War II and the two original buildings were completely renovated thanks to the benefactors who made it possible and after whom they are named- the late Margaret Mayer and the Guardians.

Efforts of the Junior Auxiliary, led by Ida Mayer Cummings, to bring the residents the latest in modern structures brought into being the seven-story Mary Pickord Building, which was opened in 1951, and the latest addition is the Ida Mayer Cummings Medical Center on its ground floor.

Leadership of the Jewish Home has included the top Jewish communal figures in Los Angeles. Since its formation almost a half-century ago, the Jewish 

Home has been fortunate to have at its helm such presidents as (in the order named): 

Simon Lewis, 1912-18; Max Brown, 1919; Simon Lewis, 1920-21; Abraham 

Horwitz, 1922-23; Charles Beaver, 1924-25; Philip Senegram, 1926; Barnet Rosenburg, 1927-28; Abraham Mark, 1929-31; Jacob Farbstein, 1932; Hyman Levine, 1933-34; H. Lew Zuckerman, 1935-37; Theodore Strimling, 1938-39; Samuel Tuch, 1940-41; Nathan Weisman, 1942-46; Sam Goldman, 1947-48; Sol Levine, 1949-50; 

Eugene M. Rosen, 1951-52; Mischa F. Berb, 1953-54; and Harry B. Seelig, 1955 to this writing. 

Current executive director is Nathaniel A. Snyder, while Murray Blumberg is director of the department of medical and social services. Longest service of any staff member is that of Mrs. Ida Rose Spero, now the superintendent, who came to the Home in 1929.

Among its auxiliaries are the famed Junior Auxiliary, headed for the past 18 years by Ida Mayer Cummings; the 45-year-old Senior Auxiliary, now led by 

Mrs. Betrtha Blackman, and the San Fernando Valley Auxiliary, headed by Mrs. 

Jay L. Bennett.

Close to 1000 of the city's leading business and professional men belong

SOUTHWEST JEWRY 

to the Guardians of the Jewish Home for the Aged (see separate story), while the newest of the Home's fund-raising arms is its San Fernando Valley Vanguard, organized in Sept., 1956, with Morris C. Schrager as the first president. 

Because of its immense budget, created by the growing needs of our aged, the Jewish Home for the Aged deserves the support of every segment of the community. It has written many happy last chapters in the lives of otherwise unhappy old folks and with your aid can continue to add new chapters of "loving care" and "added years of happiness."

SIMON LEWIS 

In writing the history of Los Angeles Jewish institutions and their leaders, one can not very well omit the name of SIMON LEWIS, a veteran and pioneer in Jewish welfare and charitable work in the city of Los Angeles. 

For over a half century Simon

Lewis was active in the field of social service, never resting, always planning, always eager to participate in every good Jewish cause to aleviate the burdens of his brethren, and to feel that he was his brother's keeper.

We therefore feel that this volume would not be complete without the history of the life of SIMON LEWIS. 

near 

Born in Russian-Poland, Wolkowisk, in 1880, his father was a merchant, and kept him in "Cheder", later in a Yeshivah until the age of fourteen. He then had to go to work as a decorator, and as a boy of fifteen he arrived in New York, but he soon learned that his mother passed away so that he had to work even harder in order to bring over the younger members of his family.

Arrived in Los Angeles in 1902 and for a while he was influenced by progressive ideas, but because his fellow workers were extreme radical, he dropped out from that movement and became interested in Zionism, he became the secretary of the first Zionist

Organization (Young Zionists) and for years he cherished the book of addresses of the original membership.

It was Simon Lewis who was instrumental in hte founding of the Hebrew Sheltering Home, which later turned into what is now known as the beautiful Los Angeles Jewish Home for tht Aged, at 325 S. Boyle Avenue. 

In 1912 the "Home" was on Rose

Street, and so small was the first Home, that when the directors had to have a meeting, the residents were asked to be so kind and go outside until the meeting was over.

In those days many Jewish destitute old folks were kept at the "County

Home," which of course was not kosher, and once a Jewish man or woman visited the Hebrew Sheltering Home, they would simply refuse to go back to the County Home.

It was then that Mr. Lewis made up his mind not to rest till the Jewish Community would get busy working for a bigger and better Home for unfortunate Jewish old folks. 

Next a house was rented in 955 

Court Circle, active there with Lewis were Mr. Cantor, Simon Shukin, Louis 

Glasband, I. Kaufman, L. Traub, and 

Mrs. Corenson. This group did not rest till they saw their dream come true.

SOUTHWEST JEWRY 

103 

Later Lewis formed a committee consisting of Ben R. Meyer, Simon 

Shukin, B. Forer, L. Eisner and Louis 

Isaacs, this group raised the first ten thousand dollars with which they purchased the grounds at 325 S. Boyle Avenue. 

Simon Lewis was the first president, M. Brown succeeded him for one year, but afterwards Lewis again took over for a period of eight years.

The memory of Simon Lewis will never be forgotten by all who came to know him, and certainly will they also remember Mrs. Simon Lewis who was always by his side, supporting him and carries on his good work. 

H. LEW ZUCKERMAN 

Born in Hungary, Sept. 10, 1885 of orthodox parentage. In U. S. since 

1901, spent the first 11 months in 

New York experiencing all sorts of hardships, then spent 7 months in 

Albany, N. Y. Left for Sioux City, Iowa, where he remained for two years. In 1905 he lived in St. Louis and in Nebraska, 1906 he lived in 

Mitchel, S. Dakota, in Los Angeles since 7th of January, 1907. 

First thing he did was to join the

B'nai B'rith, immediately becoming active in social work, helping others. 

Married Miss Sadie Goldberg, in 

Feb. 1909, daughter of Louis Goldberg, who was the president of Cong. 

Beth-Israel, Olive Street for a period of eighteen years. Goldberg together with Kaspare Cohn were the founders of the Kaspare Cohn Hospital, which in later years changed into the Cedars of Lebanon Hospital. 

Goldberg was also the founder of the Jewish Loan Society, as well as of the Jewish Consumptive Relief Assn., and many other welfare organizations. 

The Zuckermans are blessed with two sons: Ted and Marvin, both very active in the Guardians for the Jewish Home for the Aged, Ted being the 

President of the Guardians. Father and sons are the owners of the Sally 

Shops, a chain of specialty Dress 

Shops all through Southern California. 

Mr. and Mrs. Zuckerman have been instrumental together with the late 

Simon Lewis in founding the Hebrew

Sheltering Home, now known as the Los Angeles Jewish Home for the 

Aged at 325 S. Boyle Avenue, a model institution the finest and largest on the Pacific Coast. 

In 1909 he joined Congregation 

Sinai, and ever since then he is a member of the board, also a past secretary. For the past 44 years a member of Los Angeles and also the 

National Real Estate Board, a Mason, Scottish Rites and a Shriner. 

Zuckerman twice lost everything he had, but then tried again, came up to the top, lost again, but never despaired. He well remembers the time when he had to sleep on factory floors, lived in New York with two dollars a week, but even then he felt that 

THIS I SSTILL THE BEST COUN- TRY IN THE WORLD. 

Zuckerman is also a proud farmer, believing that you can not take the farm away from the boy.

HARRY B. SEELIG 

Born in Chicago, and came to Los

Angeles as a youth and has recided here now for some 25-30 years. 

As a young man he was a fine violinist and the leader of a popular dance orchestra. This, however, was his avocation; his serious occupation concerned itself with the operation of a finance company in which he served as an executive. 

But Mr. Seelig was not satisfied with mere monetary success. The legal profession had long interested him and so he went into the study of law, emerging finally with an LL.B. and an LL. M. He has been engaged in the general practice of law for a period of over twenty-five years.

It was shortly after he came to Los Angeles that he became interested in the Jewish Home for the Aged and it became, to this day his one and only consuming interest, next to his family and practice. He has served the Home for more than twenty-five years in numerous capacities, but principally as volunteer legal counsel and has been responsible for saving the Home many thousands of dollars. He has been a member of the Home's Board since 1945, and vice president for four years under President Eugene M. 

Rosen and Misha Berg, his predecessors. 

His charming wife, Muriel and their 

10-year old son, Philip, reside at 6347 

W. Orange. 

Mrs. Seelig, among other activities, is active in the Junior Auxiliary. 

His mother, too, resides in Los Angeles and every Friday night and holiday you find Mr. Seelig at his mother's saying "Kiddish" for mother. 

Mr. Seelig is the most modest man ever to have held high office in Jewish community welfare circles. 

LOUIS GLASSBAND 

(Deceased) (1884-1933)

One of the veterans and pioneers in the field of Jewish social service in Los Angeles was Louis Glassband in the beginning of this century. 

Born in Berditchev, Podol, Russia, in 1884. It was there where Louis received his thorough Jewish and secular education.

Arrived in Los Angeles a mere youth and immediately interested himself with the faith of the Jewish people. 

As soon as he acquired the English language he served as an interpreter in the city courts, and simultaneously became instrumental in the founding

SOUTHWEST JEWRY 

105 of Jewish institutions. 

As he witnessed the plight of the destitute Jewish old folks he joined the group that started the movement for a hebrew sheltering home, having served as the secretary and also as vice president.

Later he was also among the founders of the "Bikur Cholim" society (visiting the sick) which in later years was transformed into the "Home of 

Incurables", and in time grew out to be what is now known as the "Mount

Sinai Hospital and Clinic." 

Louis Glassband was also the founder of the "Free Burial Society," remaining their Superintendent for a number of years.

Married and was survived by his widow and four children.

He was also among the founders of 

Beth Israel Congregation, Olive

Street, Temple Emanuel, Hollywood 

Temple Beth-El and above all he was deeply interested in Jewish education and contributed of his time and energy, health and wealth for Talmud 

Torahs and Yeshivahs. 

Louis Glassband was known for his generous contributions to Jewish institutions. 

Founder of the Glassband Funeral 

Parlor, now the ownership of Irving and Joseph Glassband, jointly with their mother, Martha, the widow of 

Louis Glassband, may his memory be blessed.

Guardians of L. A. Jewish Home 

In May, 1938, a group of some 100 charitably-minded men, with the late Nahtan Weisman as their moving spirit, organized the Guardians of the Jewish Home for the Aged of Los Angeles. Their purpose was to combine their charitable activities on behalf of the old folks with social functions and good fellowship. 

How well the Guardians have succeeded in their original purpose can be found in two simple statistics: 

1. The mere handful of charter members have grown today into close to 

1,000 business and professional men. 

2. In the past five years alone, the Guardians have raised more than one-half million dollars towards support of the Jewish Home for the Aged. 

The Guardians is a unique fund-raising organization in that it confines its efforts in that direction to just one major campaign each year, and their annual "Adopt A Parent" drives have won them admiration and respect from the community for the dignity and good taste with which they are conducted. 

Outside of the campaign period, the monthly dinner meetings of the Guardians, held in the main in the Beverly Hills Hotel, are confined to programs of fine food, good entertainment and all-around conviviality. 

Some of the city's top business and professional leaders have taken time out from busy schedules to lead the Guardians. 

The initial president when the Guardians were formed in 1938 was Fred Horowitz, and his successors, in the order named, have included Henry Locus, Edward L. Kozberg, Max Firestein, Harry Friedman, Joseph H. Weisman, Albert 

H. Allen, Max Strasburg, Harold Krowech, George Miller, Charles Goldring, Max

SOUTHWEST JEWRY 

P. Horwitz, Maurice Katleman, Joe Herman, Saul Shapiro, Edward Feldman, Judge Burnett Wolfson and in 1957, Ted Zuckerman.

In addition to their monthly dinner meetings, the Guardians hold two events each year to which their ladies are invited, including a "Mr. and Mrs." dinner dance in January and another gay social evening during the summer. 

Growth of the Guardians has kept pace with the growth of the Jewish Home for the Aged and the Guardiains today have become the major fund-raising agency of the Home.

Annual maintenance budget of the Home for its more than 300 residents is now close to $600,000 and the Guardians are relied upon to provide twenty-five percent of that amount each year.

Throughout their proud history, the Guardians have never failed to meet the responsibilities they have delegated to themselves on behalf of our Jewish aged.

CHARLES GROMAN 

1883 - 1932 

Born of Jacob and Sarah (nee 

Weinberg) Groman, March 1, 1883, in the town of Filesht, Bessarabia, from where his parents came later. 

At the age of 10 Charles Groman came to Chicago. 

At the latter city he received a public and high school education. He was able to hold a position far in advance of his years when he left school. being placed right in the position of manager of the large Chicago factory of the Eastern Hat Company, on which job he remained for ten years. 

Next he came to Los Angeles (1907), and established an insurance business. Having been married rather young, at 19, to Miss Goldie Kobrin, of Kief, Russia, his three children at this time were already quite grown up. He took Harry, born in 1900 into his business and gave it a new name: Charles Groman and Son. His other children are Anna, born 1906, Cholim Hospital, now Mt. Sinai hospital. He has also been manager for four years of the first Jewish Theatre in Los Angeles, and was likewise the founder of this enterprise, past treasurer of the Jewish Free 

Burial Association, and was formerly its president for six years. He has been president for six years of the

Golden State Western Star Lodge. 

Charles Groman was one of the founders of the Jewish Free Burial

Society and the founder of the Jewish Free Loan Society. He was especially known for his warm appeals for public funds at the time drives were carried on in the Jewish Community, in fact he was getting to be known as 

THE OFFICIAL appeal maker espe cially for orthodox congregations and institutions. He was also the founder of the Bessarabian Society, and when passed away on December 7, 1932, he was deeply mourned by thousands at his funderal and by all who came to know him and love him. 

Besides his two sons Charles Groman was also survived by a daughter 

Ann Groman.

WILLIAM KNEWBOW 

William Knewbow was born in Russia in 1896. There he received his early education. At the age of 12 he was compelled to earn his livelihood.

He started as an apprentice to learn the clothing business. In 1910 he came to America.

In 1917 he had to discontinue his studies to enter the World War, where he served in the construction division.

Soon after he received his honorable discharge from the army he returned to Chicago. Becoming used to the warm climate in the South, where he served in the U. S. Army, he disliked the Chicago weather. California was the place. In 1918 he came to Oakland, where he married Miss Bessie Rubin, and the same year he came to Los Angeles, bringing along his wife, her mother and her sister, and a bank roll consistsing of $260 a small capital. indeed, for a provider of a family of three. 

He began to hunt for a job-a job

SOUTHWEST JEWRY 

113 of any kind-to earn a livelihood for the family, but jobs were scarce. Nor could he avail himself of his experience in the clothing line, as there was no clothing industry then in Los Angeles; and here Mr. Knewbow conceived the idea of a clothing factory in Los Angeles, there being no such thing on the entire Pacific Coast. 

Finally, one day, while in search of a job, he located a small organization, then known as the Boys' Clothing Co. 

He sold them the idea of expansionthe ideal of a real clothing factory, to manufacture clothing for men and boys, and compete with the Eastern market. He joined the organization as an employee, and soon was offered a partnership in the establishment. 

But, having no money to purchase a share in the partnership, the organization agreed to advance him the necessary amount. Thus he became a partner in the business with the company's funds. One year later the company began to manufacture a complete line of clothing for men and boys. Two years later a larger plant became necessary to meet the constantly growing demand for their products. 

Mr. Knewbow is also the owner of the Sylvador Apartments, a name composite of the names of his two daughters, Sylvia and Dora. 

In 1930 Knewbow was wiped out and was left penniless, but being an optimist by nature, he did not become panicky, nor despared, but began thinking and planning for a new beginning. 

The first thing he did was to take a civil service examination obtaining the highest rating, as he had a world of experience in the manufacturing of clothing, so he went to work for the "Department of Institutions," in the State of California, and later in the Department of Justice as assistant superintendent in the Atlanta Penitentiary clothing factories, and in 1940 he was employed by the war department. It was in these three positions that he held that he received the highest civil service ratings at the war department. He was senior officer, clothing inspector in 5 Eastern 

States, manufacturing army clothing.

In 1943 he started business again, manufacturing clothing in San Antonio, Texas for the army men, and up to 1950 he actually delivered to the government millions of garments to the U. S. Army, employing thousands of women. In those war years Mr. 

Knewbow had priority for transportation by air for army clothing work and so he rose to the top again, was marveled, admired by all who knew of his accomplishments.

In 1952 he sold his business and retired having accumulated a little fortune. 

He then returned to Los Angeles, purchased a home near the seashore and also a ranch at Fallbrook, California. 

Mr. Knewbow is an active member of the "Guardians," member Allakai Temple, Westwood Shrine Club, life member of the Junior Auxiliary and a liberal contributor to every charitable and philanthropic institntion. He is the holder of a large sum of State of Israel Bonds and a great champion of the State of Israel. 

For an Immigrant boy, he cestainly can be considered a credit to the Jewish community.

SOUTHWEST JEWRY Cedars Of Lebanon Hospital 

Kaspare Cohn Hospital, predecessor to Cedars of Lebanon Hospital, was first housed in a two-story frame residence on Carroll Ave. Opened in 1903, it was primarily intended for indigent tubercular patients. There were 30 beds and one doctor, Dr. Philip Newmark. Serving with him on the consulting medical staff were Dr. Adolph Tyroler, Dr. D. W. Edelman, Dr. Henry Herbert and Dr. E. M. 

Lazard. Dr.

Lazard, later became Chief of Staff and for many years served as 

Chief of the Obstetrics Staff in the hospital. 

The original hospital building was purchased as a site by Kaspare Cohn at the request of Jacob Schlesinger, President of the Hebrew Benevolent Society. Mr. 

Schlesinger became the hospital's first president, being succeeded by Max 

Newmark, Lemuel Goldwater, Ben R. Meyer and George M. Thompson, who heads the hospital at present.

It was still under the name of Kaspare Cohn Hospital that its second home was erected on a five-acre site on Whittier Blvd., just outside the city limits in 

1912. Among the largest contributors to the new hospital were such historic names in Los Angeles as Kaspare Cohn, Harris Newmark and David and Moses Hamburger. There were 50 beds in the main building and 12 additional beds for tubercular patients in a separate cottage. 

Dr. Herman Sugarman and Dr. Henry Lissner were among additional medical staff members about this time. Dr. Lissner, who was in charge of medicine, has remained active through the years and still drops in at the hospital almost every day. Staff doctors took care of patients at the hospital and also received cases without pay in their private offices. 

In 1930, the hospital's third and present home was dedicated at its Fountain Ave. site as a 250-bed hospital. In 1940, the Clinic building was erected to replace the Clinic facility opened on Bunker Hill in 1919. Today, Cedars is a 486-bed hospital, one of the nation's foremost in excellence of medical standards, lifesaving research and amount of free care given those unable to pay, regardless of race or religion. Last year (1955) this figure exceeded $1,000,000, covering more than 100,000 outpatient visits and 20,000 hospital bed days. Contributing to support of this program are the Community Chest and the United Jewish Welfare Fund through the Federation of Jewish Welfare Organizations. 

The largest construction program in the 55-year history of Cedars of Lebanon Hospital, and one of the largest on record for any voluntary health facility in the nation, was announced recently for the Cedars Medical Center by George M. 

Thompson, Cedars' President. 

Named to chair the $6,000,000 Building Program and $5,000,000 Endowment Fund Campaign was Walter N. Marks, Los Angeles realtor. The total $11,000,000 goal was described by him as the largest ever set in a planned effort by any single institution on the West Coast. "It is the belief of Mr. Thompson and the Board of Trustees at Cedars that this is a real, an attainable goal. Every one of the specific projects ranks high as a priority in meeting the community's growing health needs and the challenge of medical progress," Marks said.

SOUTHWEST JEWRY 

115 Chariman Marks further explained that the availability of land adjoining the hospital, and never before purchasable, is a compelling factor in accelerating the building program. 

Additional facilities, as illustrated here, will incorporate expansion of the Cardio-Respiratory Department, $150,000, and Radiation Therapy Department, $150,000, in Cedars' Main Building.

New construction already underway is highlighted by the Main Building's Diagnostic X-ray Wing. Funds required for completion are allocated in the new drive as $100,000.

Other new wing of the Main Hospital Building will house the new Administration Facilities, budgeted at $600,000. Structures also fronting on Fountain Ave., but east of Berendo St., will be the new Clinic Wing with 65 beds $1,000,000, and the Research Center, $750,000 for additional facilities connecting to the Clinic 

Wing and existing Blanche May Memorial Institute for Medical Research.

Facing Fountain Ave., on the west-south of the street will be the proposed 

Living Facilities for House Staff and Nurses, $250,000, and a two-level parking structure, costing $250,000 and bringing Cedars' total off-street capacity to 

800 cars.

Vital complements of the building program on Berendo St., just south of 

Sunset Blvd., will be the Rehabilitation Center, $650,000, and the Institute of 

Nuclear Medicine, $500,000.

Completing the proposed structures, also just south of Sunset Blvd. but towards Catalina St., will be the 100-bed Long-Term Care and Convalescent 

Hospital, $1,300,000 and the 20-bed Psychiatric Center, which includes outpatient facilities, $300,000.

Architectural commitments on the first of the entirely new buildings, probably the Rehabilitation Center and Institute of Nuclear Medicine, are now being discussed by the Cedars Board of Trustees. Contracts will be let and construction started in ratio to campaign progress, it was noted by Marks. Astisting him in the campaign effort will be various division chairmen and the hospital's standing Fund Raising Committee, headed by Louis E. Golan.

Endowment fund phase of the $11,000,000 campaign will be in the form of a Cedars Medical Center Foundation, with the $5,000,000 figure covering the training of doctors, fellowships, unrestricted research and operational costs for the projected new facilities and services. 

It is estimated that the additional facilities will enable Cedars to expand its free medical care program, which last year totaled more than $1,000,000 for an approximate 100,000 outpatien visitt and 20,000 hospital bed days. Financial support of this program is received from the Community Chest and the United Jewish Welfare Fund through the Federation of Jewish Welfare Organizations.

Miss Edna Schuster succeeded Mr. Straus on a part-time basis. Later, casework services were added to the Volunteer Big Brother program and Miss Schuster was employed full time to do both services. This was the origin of the agency's casework program.

SOUTHWEST JEWRY 

California Home for the Aged at Reseda A non-sectarian, non-profit organization under Jewish sponsorship, the Home is dedicated to the care of the indigent and homeless aged over sixtyfive, who have been legal residents of the State of California for two years. 

Medical care, twenty-four hour nursing supervision, occupational therapy and recreational services are included in the Home's program. It is the policy of the Home that each resident shall have his own room. Double rooms are provided for married couples. A fully equipped infirmary is maintained for those needing bed care. A weekly allowance for every resident is included in the 

Home's budget.

The eight acre site in the San Fernando Valley on which the Home is located now houses about 75 residents, but will ultimately house 275 persons. 

The California Home for the Aged relies entirely upon direct voluntary public contribution. No funds are received from local or governmental agencies. 

The 14 affiliated auxiliaries and chapters and their presidents are: Aidettes, Mrs. Henry Silver; Bay Cities Chapter, Mrs. William Turk; Benefactors, Hy 

Getzoff; Beverly Hills Chapter, Mrs. William Shurman; Conference of Jewish 

Organizations, Benjamin Surasky; Eastside Chapter, Mrs. Rose Bellis; Helping 

Hands Chapter, Murray Roth; Hollywood Ladies Auxiliary, Mrs. Jack Katsh; 

Mr. and Mrs. Club, Robert Silver; Boris and Lillian Okuneff Chapter, Mrs. 

Murray Greenfield; Professional Women's League, Ann J. Dinnerstein; Sunshine 

Chapter, Celia Green; Valley Chapter, Mrs. Herbert Baun, Friends of the Aged, Norman Friedman.

The Home is located at 19260 Sherman Way in Reseda; Executive Offices are at 590 N. Vermont. Ben L. Garon is Executive Director.

VRENA ROZENKRANTZ 

Mrs. Vrena Rozenkrantz can truthfully be called "the official mother of the California Home at Reseda," as she has been for years the inspiration and admiration not only for all the residents at the California Home at

Reseda, but by all her associates and co-workers as well as by all who come in contact with her. 

Mrs. Rozenkrantz has given of her time, energy, health and wealth to the extent that only a loveable mother would do so much for her own dear children. 

In 1905, Mrs. Vrena A. Rosenkranz founded the now famous National 

Schools which operate internationally. 

She is rightfully known as female industrialist in America. She is a philanthropist of note. Together with her husband, many years of their planning and many thousands of their dollars

SOUTHWEST JEWRY 

119 went into the development of the present City of Hope. This, too, can be said of the California Home for the 

Aged at Reseda. Today, Mrs. Rosenkranz has adopted the Home as one of her major philanthropies. There is the latest building at the Home, considered by many as one of the finest residences of its kind for aged people carries her name. A painting in oil of 

Mrs. Rosenkranz is in the foyer of this new building. The inscription underneath says "Vrena A. Rosenkranz,

Honorary Life President of the California Home for the Aged and Official 

Mother of this Home."

Nathan Straus Israel Society By MARCO R. NEWMARK 

One of the least publicized yet most worthwhile organizations in Los Angeles is the Nathan Straus Israel Society.

It never has interested itself in political and theological considerations. Zionist, non-Zionist, anti-Zionist, Orthodox, Conservative, Reform Jews can and do enroll without sacrifice of the least jot of principle in any respect. 

FOUNDED IN 1914 

The Society was founded thirty-three years ago at a time when Nathan Straus and others had recognized the elemental need of providing refugees from the restrictions and unspeakable persecutions of Eastern Europe with a home in the land of their fathers, where they and their wives and children could live in freedom, own land and cultivate the soil, and revitalize and transmit their ancestral culture.

It was on March 22, 1914, that the Jewish people in Los Angeles tendered a reception to Mr. and Mrs. Straus, who were then visiting the city. 

The affair was conducted in the old B'nai B'rith hall on Seventeenth Street near Figueroa.

DIGNITARIES ASSEMBLE 

The moguls, elders and dignitaries of local Israel occupied the first row of chairs, where they could look at our visitors face to face and in turn be so looked at by them, so that, as the projectors of the reception had wisely planned it would be difficult if not impossible for them to refrain from participation in any previously un-announced movement which might be proposed. 

After the formalities and speeches, a resolution was presented and unanimously adopted providing that a society be formed for the purpose of making contributions to the economic restoration of Palestine. 

75 CHARTER MEMBERS 

After the meeting, seventy-five of the some 300 present enrolled as charter members, many of the others joining subsequently. 

The Nathan Straus Society was on its way. 

In passing, be it said that Harry Fram was instrumental in forming the Society and that from that day to this he is devoting his untiring efforts to its success.

CREATE LOAN FUND 

Originally, the Society distributed its funds to Palestinian institutions, but in 1927, Jacob Farbstein, after his return from a visit to the Holy Land during which he had noticed the lack of agricultural capital there, suggested that the revenues of the Society, which are obtained from membership dues and contributions, be used for making loans to the colonists. 

This plan was adopted and has been continued ever since. 

PURCHASE CATTLE 

The loans are made for the purpose of enabling the borrowers to buy Holstein cows and agricultural implements. Holsteins were selected because they produce more milk than other breeds and being hardier are better able to withstand the climate and the conditions.

To us in this country a cow does not seem to be a great asset, but to the Palestine farmer it is a valuable acquisition, for the income from the milk makes

SOUTHWEST JEWRY 

it possible for him to pay off the loan; and this often spells the difference between success and failure.

REPAY IN FULL 

The most gratifying feature of the annual reports is the account of repayment, the figures revealing a loss of less than one half of one per cent. 

All loans are made through the Artizans Cooperative Society or the Farmers 

Mortgage Company, both Palestine Corporations. 

The low rate of loss has enabled the Society, during almost ten years, in spite of its comparatively small capital, to make loans totalling over $500,000, and it should be added that in accordance with a suggestion made by Mr. Brick, during his administration, some $8500 have been invested in several sound 

Palestinian financial institutions.

Aside from reports, we have eloquent first hand testimony to the high character and substantiability of our clients. 

A number of our members have visited Israel at various times. They met with the farmers and all of them bear witness to their earnestness and capacity as well as the indispensable value to them of the loans. 

VISITORS ENTHUSIASTIC 

Previous to his election to the presidency, Mr. Fleishman visited Palestine. 

In the annual report at the end of his term he said: "I took the office of President with the direct knowledge that the Society's loan fund is doing a constructive work by helping the farmers to help themselves." 

Mr. J. Herman, after a visit, brought back a similar report. Hyman D. Stack, during a stay there was also deeply impressed and returned with a statement confirming the previous testimony.

AID MANUFACTURERS 

During his sojourn, Mr. Stack, seeing the need for industrial capital placed with the Artizans Cooperative Society the sum of $5000 to be used for loans to small manufacturers, to whom it was of the greatest assistance and who, within a few years, repaid practically the entire amount. Since then other members of our 

Society have visited Palestine among whom were Rabbi Magnin, Chas. Brown, S. Tilden Norton, Samuel S. Wolfson, the late Adolph Sieroty and Julian Sieroty. 

Such is the story of the Nathan Straus Palestine Society. 

For thirty-two years, it has unostentatiously but significantly been contributing to the well being and success of hundreds of farmers by "helping them to help themselves."

For thirty-two years, it has been carrying on in the spirit of the great humanitarian who inspired its founding. 

Everyone of its members can well be proud of its accomplishments. 

Officers: Samuel S. Wolfson, President; Julian M. Sieroty, Vice-President; 

Hon. Benj. J. Scheinman, Vice-President; Dr. J. L. Rude, Vice-President; H. D. 

Stack, Treasurer; Edward Stack, Fin. Sec. and Asst. Treas., Samuel Tierman, Recording, and Harry Fram, Corresponding Secretary.

Directors: S. Tilden Norton, Hon. Chairman of the Board; Edward I. Beck, 

Charles Brown, Harry L. Cohn, Leo C. Fond, Lawrence P. Frank, Meyer Frank, Robert J. Gans, Emanuel J. Greenberg, Henry Herzbrun, J. Herman, Charles 

Isenstein, Simon Lewis, Joshua H. Marks, Ben R. Meyer, Julius Salmonson, Jesse 

D. Scheinman, Joseph Shapiro, Theodore Strimling, Jack L. Warner.

The presidents of the Society have been Marco R. Newmark, Louis M. Cole, Alexander Brick, Judge Harry A. Hollzer, Adolph Fleishman, Judge Isaac Pacht,

Adolph Sierotv and S. Tilden Norton and Samuel S. Wolfson. Dr. J. L. Rude, Benjamin Lewis, H. Gregory Brilliant and the recently elected President Jack 

M. Straus.

The Early History of Independent Order B'nai B'rith (Pacific Coast District) 

Brother Jacob Mayer, a California pioneer, though in later years long a resident of the city of Portland, Ore., where he flourished as one of its merchant princes, in the year 1855, suggested to his co-religionists, with whom he was then fraternizing as a Mason, the formation of a B'nai B'rith lodge, of which he had read something in the Jewish journals of the day. The proposition met with favor and a call was issued for a meeting of Israelites to be held in San Francisco on 

June 12, 1855, for the purpose of petitioning District Grand Lodge No. 1 (New 

York) for a charter. Such a charter was granted on August 13, 1855, and was presented to the petitioners at their third meeting on October 24, 1855. The first lodge of the Pacific Coast, Ophir No. 21, was installed on November 18, 1855, with William Steinhart as the first president and Seixas Solomons as first secretary. The lodges began to increase so that in 1863 a convention of past presidents was held for the purpose of taking preliminary steps towards the formation of a 

District Grand Lodge. Brother David Stern of Modin Lodge 42, who died as auditor of the city and county of San Francisco, presided. The petition was sent 

East and after an extended correspondence, the secretary of the Constitution 

Grand Lodge, Brother Baruch Rothschild, arrived in the city of San Francisco in the fall of 1863, bringing with him the charter for District Grand Lodge No. 4. 

The Grand Lodge was instituted by Brother Rothschild on October 7, 1863, amidst great rejoicing and with Brother Jacob Greenebaum as its first president.

Brother Rothschild made his home in San Francisco and became one of the honored members of District No. 4. At the end of 1915, lodges were distributed as follows: California, 20; Washington, 7; Oregon, 2; Montana, 2; British Columbia, 2; Utah, Nevada, Idaho, Arizona, each 1, making a total of 37. All the old lodges that flourished in the 'sixities and 'seventies of the last century had to relinquish their charters by reason of the fact that they were located in mining towns whose glories faded away at the close of the century. 

BENEFACTIONS 

Ever since the organization of the first lodge the members showed their readiness to respond to the call of the unfortunate wherever they may have been. 

Frequently the cry came from the Orient and then again from towns and cities near home. Persecutions, pogroms, inundations, fires and earthquakes caused the cry for help to go forth, and in no instance did the order refuse a hearty response. 

Even in the last world's catastrophe, where no class or race refuses its share to soothe the sufferings of the unfortunates whose fate brought them into the war zone, or rather brought the war to them, the Order of B'nai B'rith was among the first in the United States to issue a call for help. The B'nai B'rith of this city have been beneficiaries of its bounty in 1906 to such an extent that the recollection of its still awakens feelings of gratitude in their hearts. Not only were the immediate wants of many of them supplied, but hundreds of non-members of the order, late immigrants, were kept, so to say, in luxury during the long weeks, even months, of discomfort and deprivation suffered by the average inhabitant. 

On the high holy days, following the great conflagration, the order provided religious services among the refugees in Golden Gate Park and assisted in the continuation of free religious training under the auspices of the Jewish Educational Society.

The Pacific Hebrew Orphan Asvlum of San Francisco and the Jewish 

Orphans' Home of Southern California.

located in Los Angeles, were both born

SOUTHWEST JEWRY 

within the walls of the B'nai B'rith lodge. That both institutions subsequently assumed importance and dimensions which surpassed the strength of the order and caued them to be absorbed by the Jewish communities at large is something for which the order is not to be blamed. The credit for their creation is still its own. 

Throughout the United States and Europe other eleemosynary institutions are supported in toto or in part by the order and they are monuments of human work, such as the National Jewish Hospital for Consumptives at Denver, the Leo N. Levi Memorial Hospital at Little Rock, Ark., the famous orphan asylusm at Cleveland and Atlanta, the Yonkers Home for Aged at New York and others. 

Ever since their inception the lodges established funds for the purpose of "alleviating the wants of the sick and needy," defraying funeral expenses of deceased brethren and assisting their widows and orphans. Their zeal for the latter caused them in the early 'seventies to embark in an ill-digested and crude life insurance scheme known throughout fraternal organizations as a so-called endowment system. That scheme flourished on the Pacific Coast (District No. 4) for a round forty years when, in order to save the good name and integrity of the district, the Grand Lodge turned all its assets in the shape of B'nai B'rith hall stock over to the remaining four hundred and some odd endowment members, they on the other hand acquiescing in the liquidation of the system by voluntarily surrendering their beneficiary certificates in exchange for their allotted hall stock.

History of the Jewish Centers in Los Angeles

Chronologically even in 1957 in early stages of growth, the Jewish Center movement in Los Angeles started in 1910, many years later than in most eastern cities. Providing activities for underprivileged Jewish youth in the Temple Street area, the Educational Alliance, Los Angeles' first Center, had a precarious existence until 1924 when the Jewish population shift to the Boyle Heights area created the need for a Center in the eastern part of Los Angeles. The Modern 

Social Center (to become in 1937 the Soto-Michigan Center and later to be renamed the Eastside Center) was formed in 1924; in 1928 the Menorah Center in the City Terrace area; the West Adams Center in 1937; and the short-lived 

Menorah-Wilshire and Hollywood-Wilshire Centers in 1938. 

Skipping from area to area, housed in inadequate facilities, always lagging far behind Los Angeles' phenomenal Jewish population expansion, the Centers offered a sorry picture in 1942. In that year, the National Jewish Welfare Board completed a study of Los Angeles' Centers, and, as a result of the recommendations of that study, came the organization of the Jewish Centers Association in its present form. A unified agency with each Center serving as an operating

SOUTHWEST JEWRY 

135 unit of the whole, the JCA has grown through the years under the successive presidencies of the late Judge Irwin Stalmaster, Jack Y. Berman, Leslie Cramer, Louis Warschaw, Max W. Bay, Jules Bisno, and today's president, Lawrence E. Irell. 

1957 sees the Jewish Centers Association serving over 16,000 members in areas wherever Jews are concentrated. Still lagging behind the needs of our 

Jewish population, the Centers have, nevertheless, become the common meeting ground for every Jewish group of the community. Here, in an atmosphere of warmth and companionship, Jewish people have found satisfactions in their Jewishness and comfort in belonging. Serving every age group from nursery school to senior citizen the JCA Centers contribute to the health, happiness and well-being of its members through a many-faceted program. Over 800,000 attendances during the year 1955 1956 attest to the popularity of the Center movement in Los Angeles.

The JCA units are: The Westside Jewish Community Center, 5870 West 

Olympic Boulevard, Los Angeles 36; The Eastside Jewish Community Center, 2317 Michigan Avenue, Los Angeles 33; The Valley Cities Jewish Community 

Center, 13162 Burbank Boulevard, Van Nuys, California; The Bay Cities Jewish Community Center, 2601-07 Santa Monica Boulevard, Santa Monica, Cali fornia; Hollywood Los Feliz Jewish Community Center, 1110 Bates Avenue, Los Angeles 29; Camp JCA in Barton Flats, California; Montebello Extension 

Center, 482 North Garfield, Montebello, California; Stephen Wise Extension

Center, 10522 South Normandie Avenue, Los Angeles; The B'nai B'rith JCA 

Youth House, 1065 South La Cienega Boulevard, Los Angeles. 

The JCA facilities, more adequate than they were in 1942, are, because of budget limitations, not yet sufficient to meet the demand of Los Angeles' growing population. It is our confident prediction, however, that in the years ahead proper provision will be made to meet all the group work needs of our expanding community.

History of the Wilshire Blvd. Temple By MARCO R. NEWMARK 

The taking of the first Federal census in Southern California in 1850 disclosed that there were, among the 1610 inhabitants of the city, six Jewish families. It was further disclosed that the first Jews known to have settled in Los 

Angeles arrived the previous year.

In 1854, Joseph Newmark, an ordained though never regularly officiating rabbi, came here and immediately organized the Hebrew Benevolent Society, Jacob Elias being the president. This was the first Jewish organization in Los 

Angeles. Its purposes were the care of the sick, the paying of proper respect, according to the ancient ritual, to the departed, and the supervision of the cemetery. It met Sunday evenings at the home of its founder. 

Under its auspices, also, Mr. Newmark, in a little adobe which was a far cry from the Temples to follow, conducted services on the Holy Days. Strangely enough, the adobe temple was owned by a Mr. John Temple. 

The first Confirmation of the Religious School was held in Lawlor's Institute, a Los Angeles school of that time located at 2nd and Main Streets.

In 1862, Abraham Wolf Edelman came to Los Angeles from San Francisco to serve as Rabbi of the newly-formed congregation. Services under Rabbi 

Edelman were first held in Arcadia or Sterns' Hall, after the first adobe Temple proved inadequate for the ever-increasing congregation, and later, by the courtesy of Judge Ygnacio Sepulveda, services were held in the single-room courthouse that the proud town of Los Angeles then boasted.

Represented in the Memorial Procession that took place after the death of Abraham Lincoln was the entire congregation of this first Temple. After the 

Procession, special services were conducted at the home of Rabbi Edelman. 

One of the most important days in the history of the Congregation occurred on March 10th, 1869 when the women members of the congregation met for the purpose of devising a plan for the creation of a Temple which the Jewish population of Los Angeles could call its own. Although no records exist of the meeting and the discussions, the pioneer spirit was apparent, for in August, 1872, the cornerstone was set down. A trip was made to San Francisco and the Jewish people of that city contributed the sum of $1000.00 to aid in construction of the 

Los Angeles synagogue. The sum undoubtedly represented an important percentage of the total cost. It took just one year to build the synagogue, which was dedicated on August 8, 1873. It was located on the east side of Fort Street, which is now known as Broadway, between 2nd and 3rd Streets, and was in keeping with the general motif with the then-modern houses of worship. 

In 1884, Rabbi Edelman, realizing that a change from orthodox to liberal religious procedure was inevitable, resigned as spiritual leader since his beliefs would not countenance the change.

Dr. Emanuel Schreiber of Denver, Colorado, was invited to take charge by the congregation. It was on Thanksgiving of the year 1888 that, at the invitation of the Rev. Eli P. Fay, the Unitarians and the Jews held joint services in the 

Fort Street Synagogue. 

The ever-expanding congregation overflowed the Fort Street Temple to the point where it had to worship in the Unitarian Church at 3rd and Hill Streets until a new synagogue could be built. At about the time of the completion of the new Temple at the northeast corner of 9th and Hope, Rev. M. G. Solomon was elected to the occupancy of the pulpit. 

Tradition played an important part in the constructoin of this new temple,

SOUTHWEST JEWRY 

and the honor of designing it was bestowed on Abraham M. Edelman, who was the son of the temple's first rabbi. The new temple was dedicated on September 6, 1896, at a special cornerstone ceremony with the president of the congregation, H. W. Hellman, placing the cornerstone.

In November, 1899, Rabbi Sigmund Hecht, until then spiritual guide of the Congregation Emanuel of Milwaukee, became Rabbi of the Temple. 

Until 1854, the Jews of Los Angeles had no burial place of their own; but in that year they acquired one. It was picturesquely situated in a nook of ground among the hills in the northwest part of the city, which the reservoir of Andros Jose Sepulveda had once occupied. By 1891, it became apparent that this home of the departed required more constant and concentrated attention. Under the leadership of Mrs. Maurice Kremer, in January of that year, the Home of 

Peace Society was organized to fill that need. 

In January 1902, the purchase of a 30 acre burial plot on Whittier Road opposite the Catholic Cemetery, was authorized; and on Sunday, May 18, 1902, the dedication took place. The remains of those who had been interred in the old burial grounds were later transferred to the new cemetery, which is still in use. 

On October 25, 1903, the congregation became a unit in the Reform Organization of the United States when it joined the Union of American Hebrew Congregations. 

On October 27, 1915, Edgar F. Magnin was elected Associate Rabbi, his installation taking place on December 8th.

The present site of the Temple at the corner of Hobart and Wilshire Boulevards, was acquired in 1921. 

Formation of committees and the appointment of architects, which included Abraham Edelman again, who had designed the temple at 9th and Hope Streets, and S. Tilden Norton, hon. president of the Temple, were a part of the many projects necessary for the creation of this new house of worship. On May 20, 1925, the preliminary plans for the new temple were approved, and just one month later Rabbi Hecht passed away. He was succeeded by Rabbi Edgar F. 

Magnin, and on August 26, 1925 Rabbi Maxwell H. Dubin was appointed director of religious education. He has since become Associate Rabbi of the Temple. 

Leading personalities of the motion picture industry are on the Temple roll and are frequently to be soon attending services. On the roster are found the names of Harry and Jack Warner, Milton Sperling, Edward Selzer, Leo Forbstein, all of Warner Brothers; Charles Rogers, Harry Joe Brown, Sol Lesser, Pandro 

Berman, Joseph Schenck, Lester Wm. Roth, David Tannenbaum, Harry Rapf and Larry Weingarten of Metro-Goldwyn-Mayor; Henry Ginsberg, head of

Paramount Studios. For years prior to his death, Irving Thalberg and his wife, Norma Shearer, were regular attendants. Eddie Cantor, Jack Benny and other stars also pride themselves on being connected with this outstanding congregation which is today consideerd the most influential Jewish religious institution in the country.

SOUTHWEST JEWRY Sinai Temple 

Sinai Temple was founded in 1906. In 1956, Sinai celebrated its 50th Anniversary, its Golden Jubilee.

The Fifty Years of Sinai's career as a Congregation have been for the world, the most turbulent, yet the most creative fifty years in the history of mankind. 

The first half of this century has been a motley of wars and disasters and a medley of inventiveness and fruitful creativity. Sinai has not only been a witness to the seething events of the past fifty years, it has been an active participant. 

In all the vicissitudes of these epochal times, Sinai has remained steadfastly a symbol of the eternal verities of life. In a world that was immersed in greed, Sinai remained a symbol of selflessness. In a world hunched by hatred, Sinai remained a symbol of love. In a world dominated by ruthlessness, Sinai preached the eternal doctrine of mercy and compassion. In a word almost torn asunder by war, Sinai thundered forth from its pulpit the ancient Jewish message of peace. 

Sinai has raised three generations of dedicated, loyal Jews true to their Jewish heritage and to our American traditions. 

There is not an area in the communal life of Los Angeles and the West Coast which has not been magnificently served by men and women who have found their spiritual home in Sinai.

For twenty-five of these fifty years the Congregation was guided in its spiritual life by Dr. Jacob Kohn, the most revered and beloved Rabbi on the 

West Coast and one of the most respected men in the Rabbinate. Dr. Kohn became Rabbi Emeritus in 1955 when Rabbi Israel Chodos assumed the spiritual leadership of the Congregation.

Sinai Temple has just completed the first phase of its Building Fund Campaign, for the purpose of erecting a new Temple at Wilshire Boulevard and 

Holmby Avenue in the heart of Westwood.

For over two decades Benjamin Platt served the Congregation as its President. The following have also served as Presidents: Theodore Strimling, Edward 

Hyman and J. D. Sterling. The man who is now at the helm of the Congregation is Eugene M. Rosen.

As soon as the architect's plans are approved the Congregation will initiate the building activities on the new site.

RABBI JACOB KOHN RABBI ISRAEL CHODOS 

Born in 1881, in Newark, New Jersey. In 1902 he was graduated from 

New York University, and in 1902 and 1903 held a teaching fellowship in Philosophy at that University. 

He received his Rabbinical degree at the Jewish Theological Seminary in 1907, and after doing graduate work over a period of years, received the degree of Doctor of Hebrew Letters from the Seminary in 1920. From 1920 

until 1931, when he came to Los 

Angeles, he lectured at the Teachers

College of Columbia University. Dur ing the first World War, he served on the Overseas Commission of the Jewish Welfare Board, conducting services at the front for Jewish soldiers and he is at present a member of the 

Army and Navy Committee of the

Jewish Welfare Board. 

Since his arrival in Los Angeles, he has served as spiritual leader of Sinai 

Congregation, and in addition, has identified himself with major Jewish communal endeavors. He is vicepresident of the United Synagogue of America and a member of the

Council of the Rabbinical Assembly of America. 

Rabbi Chodos came to Sinai Temple in March of 1955, from a fruitful ministry of seven years in Oklahoma

City, Oklahoma, where he built a new Synagogue and carried on a ramified program of educational, social and religious activities. He was also active in the general community, having served as President of the Community Council of Oklahoma City and was a member of the Mayor's Committee on Human Relations. 

Rabbi Chodos was born on November 11, 1905 in Vilna, Poland, and was brought to the United States at the age of three. He received his early education in the New York Public Schools and at the Yeshiva in New 

York. He completed his undergraduate work at Johns Hopkins University and was ordained as Rabbi at the 

Jewish Theological Seminary of Amer ica in New York. He did graduate work at Harvard and had conferred upon him a Doctor's Degree by Oklahoma City University. 

For five years he served on the faculty of the University of Oklahoma. 

He held pulpits in New England

SOUTHWEST JEWRY 

and in the mid-west. He published a critical edition of a medieval Hebrew medical manuscript and is the author of the popular book "Count Your 

Blessings" published by Random 

House. For four years he presented a regular Sunday afternoon television program called "Our Better Shelves,"a program dealing with the classics of the world's literature.

He has addressed audiences across the country, in Canada and also in Mexico. He has been an active worker in Zionist causes, in B'nai B'rith and in Bonds for Israel.

One year of service in Korea earned him a Bronze Star Medal for Meritorious Service.

Rabbi Teitelbaum has been at Congregation Beth Israel, San Francisco for the period of two years where he served as Assistant Rabbi.

EUGENE M. ROSEN President, Sinai Temple 

RABBI HERBERT D. TEITELBAUM 

Since August 1st, 1955 Rabbi Teitelbaum served Sinai as Associate Rabbi.

Rabbi Herbert D. Teitelbaum is a native Californian, born in San Francisco. He received his BA at NYU and his MA at Teachers College of Columbia University. Following attendance at the Seminary College of Jewish 

Studies, he was ordained in 1951 at the Jewish Theological Seminary. 

Shortly thereafter he went into the Army Chaplaincy Corps for two years. 

Sinai is fortunate indeed, to have, at the top echelon of its lay leadership, a gentleman of the caliber, of the experience, and the tireless enthusiasm. of Gene Rosen. 

In the early days of Sinai, Gene was the Executive Secretary of our Congregation. He knew every member of the Congregation and still remembers the needs of the older members of Sinai, the seats that were reserved for them for the High Holy Days, and their special interests. His love for

Sinai Temple spans three decades. 

From his earliest youth Gene was interested in communal affairs. Even during his active years in the business world, first in the insurance field and then in the promotion of supermarkets, Gene found time to do more than his duty as a public spirited citizen. Few men in Los Angeles have to their credit a record of creative service to so long and significant an array of great causes. Gene has served them all with distinction, and has

There is not an hour of the day or night that Gene cannot be reached for 

Sinai Temple. He gives unstintingly of his time and energy and, the ripest possible experience, to the needs of 

Sinai which he loves so well. 

We wish Gene many more years of a happy and fruitful life.

ELY ELIAS 

Born in Chudrovka, Province of

Kiev, Russia, in 1884, of an orthodox family, Mr. Platt arrived here 56 years ago, and had to undergo all the hardships of a Jewish immigrant. For a while he sold sewing machines, and after he had acquired a little salesmanship, he visualized what could be accomplished in the music trade. 

He opened a small store on Seventh

Street and soon found that there were even more music-lovers among the 

Angelenos than he had expected. He continued to prosper and added a new branch almost every year, until today, he is the president of the Platt 

Music Company, a corporation known all through Southern California, with a chain of stores in Los Angeles, which is a credit to Jewish achievement and progress. 

He is a member of the I.O.B.B. and

Honorable Life President of the Sinai 

Congregation. He is a prominent 

Club member and a liberal donor to every good Jewish cause. He is deeplv interested in the welfare of the City of Hope, an institution which he helped to grow from small beginnings
