He has been a resident of Los Angeles since 1920, and prior to his arrival here he was the one who brought 

Zionism to Cincinnati 50 years ago, and in those days the City of Cincinnati was known for its anti-Zionist tendencies, as it was the "fortress" of reform Judaism. 

In Los Angeles, Ely was the founder of the Lee Department Stores, but by now he is retired and devotes all of his time soliciting for the Welfare Fund and for all the Israel projects. 

With him it is a job for every day all the year around.

MOSES TANNENBAUM 

1868 - 1936 

Born in Brisk, Lithuania, in 1868, received a talmudic education at the various Yeshivahs, and left the old country at the age of twenty one, since he did not care to serve in the Russian army. 

Arrived in New York penniless, and after spending three weeks in the sheltering office of the Baron de 

Hirsh removal office, he was shipped to Denver with a dollar in his pocket.

He reached Denver in 1886, was employed as a miner, later as a peddler, but in 1892 he started for himself in the hide and wool business. 

Later he also opened a coal office, in which business he remained until 1915.

He was one of the founders of the 

Mogen David congregation, also one of the founders of the first orthodox 

Jewish Cemeteries, and a Jewish branch of the "Maccabees," Mantefiore Tent." First president of the Sunset Benjamin Lodge, one of the organizers of the Beth Hamedrash 

Hagodol, Denver, Colorado. 

Member of the I.O.B.B., one of the founders of the Denver Jewish Con sumptive Relief Sanitarium, now known as "The Spwaek", a nation al director of the National Consump tive Relief Sanitarium, and a past representative of the Colorado State Legislature.

Immediately upon his arrival in Los 

Angeles, he established the California 

Rubber and Metal Company, of which he was the president. 

Moses tannenbaum was a veteran

Zionist and one of the first share holders of the Jewish Colonial Bank, believing in the future of a Jewish Homeland. 

Was one of the members of Olive 

Street Congregation, Breed Street 

Shull, and Agudath Achim.

A vice president of Sinai Congrega tion, from 1920 to 1927, and chairman of the Board of Education, and had to his credit being instrumental in opening the first Yeshivah in Los 

Angeles, being a great believer in Education. 

Above all he was an ardent Zionist and a past vice president of the Zionist District in 1921 1922. 

Passed away Oct. 1936. May his revered memory be blessed.

SOUTHWEST JEWRY Beth Jacob Congregation 

Beth Jacob Congregation, now in its thirtieth second year, has grown to be one of the largest, most active traditional congregations in Los Angeles. From its humble beginnings in 1925, with Sabbath Services and a Sunday School in a small store and High Holiday Services in a rented hall, the congregation grew rapidly with the influx of Jewish residents to the West Adams area. In 1928, construction of a large edifice at the southeast corner of West Adams Boulevard and Hillcrest Drive was begun to house a sanctuary, school rooms, auditorium, and other facilities. The physical facilitities of Beth Jacob were continually being improved; in 1933 an adjacent lot was acquired for a playground, and in 1949, the school building was enlarged to meet the community needs. 

Through the years, Beth Jacob provided leadership and service to the Jewish community in the West Adams area. Services were held daily, adult classes to study Jewish lore of the ages met each day, the school achieved the highest enrollment and educational standards in the city. For over a decade, Beth Jacob housed the West Adams Jewish Community Center, where youth groups met for social and cultural activities. Beth Jacob was the center for the area's Community 

Chest and Red Cross drives; it was a voting place for the World Zionist Congress and spearheaded the community's activities in the War Bond effort of the last world war. Through the Synagogue, members contributed to a multitude of worthy causes; to Yeshivos and Institutions of higher learnings, the wandering individual in need, to the Passover Relief Campaign of the Jewish Comunity Council to which it has provided leadership and to other recognized needs. 

Above all, Beth Jacob won the loyalty of its members and friends. With the shift of the Jewish community from the West Adams area, the need for moving the Congregation became rapidly apparent. Rather than wait until the organization might disintegrate, far-sighted leaders and members joined together to plan and realize the future of Beth Jacob. In a remarkably brief time, a site was purchased and a building erected in the beautiful City of Beverly Hills. 

The structure which has elicited the admiration of those who have beheld it, houses a sanctuary. classrooms, social auditorium, chapel, library and offices. 

A playground for children is available.

With the dedication of the new edifice in Beverly Hills, a new period of fruitful activity was initiated. Within less than two years, over three hundred families became full members of the Congregation. Activities at the new Beth 

Jacob are many and varied, and the general age level of the membership is young.

Services at Beth Jacob are held daily, with beautiful Sabbath and Festival services presented in the finest spirit of decorum and dignity, with English readings accompanying the Orthodox service. An active Sisterhood and Men's Club provide a full program of social and sultural activities for adults. Likewise does the Beth Jacob Adult Institute, with over 100 registered, present an interesting course of study for adults and College-age youth.

Youth activities at Beth Tacob include, the Minyonaires who hold a Sunday morning religious service. followed by a brunch and sports activities; the Beth 

Iacob Youth Club; and Cub Scout and Girl Scout troops. 

Particularly is Beth Jacob proud of the vast educational project which it houses. The Congregation provides the home for the Hillel Hebrew Academy,

SOUTHWEST JEWRY 

149 as well as it affords financial aid and leadership to the school. The Hillel Hebrew 

Academy is today the largest intensive school on the West Coast, consisting of a Yeshivah Day School for Hebrew and General studies, a daily Talmud Torah for after-public school classes, a licensed Kindergarten-Nursery, a Sunday Religious school and a home-summer camp, known as Camp Aliyah. 

Still young and vigorous, the Congregation envisages greater growth and greater religious and communal service to young and old. Spiritual leadership of 

Beth Jacob has been vested in Rabbi Simon A. Dolgin since 1939. Lay leaders this year are: President, Jacob J. Entin; Chairman, Board of Directors, Morris 

Weisstein; Vice-President, Louis Freeman and Sidney Eisenshtat, Treasurer; 

Noah Landsman; Recording Secretary, Nathan Serot; Financial Secretary, Sol 

Vilensky. Julius Samson is president of the Beth Jacob Men's Club, Mrs. Sam

Osep heads the Sisterhood and Dr. Morris Ira is president of the Hillel Hebrew 

Academy of Beth Jacob.

RABBI SIMON A. DOLGIN 

Rabbi Simon A. Dolgih has been spiritual leader of the Beth Jacob Congregation, Beverly Hills, since September, 1939. Born in Chicago, Illinois in 1915, he studied at the Hebrew Theological College from which he received ordination and at the Illinois Institute of Technology from which he received a B.S. degree. 

Since his entry into the pulpit of the Beth Jacob Congregation, which was then located in the West Adams area of Los Angeles, the Congregation grew to be one of the most prominent Orthodox Synagogues in the 

West. Aside from his activities in building the Congregation and its outstanding Hebrew School, known as the West Adams Community Hebrew 

School, co-sponsored with other neighboring organizations, Rabbi Dolgin took part in other regional and national activities.

He served on the Board of Directors of the Los Angeles Jewish Comunity Council for six years; on the 

Bureau of Jewish Education of which he was a vice chairman, for nine years; as chairman of the Passover Relief Committee and of the Arbitration Committee, and as a member of the 

Allocations Committee of the Council. He has been a member of the 

Family-Adult Service Committee of the Community Chest Welfare Council, and a member of the Board of Directors of the Big Sister League. Rabbi

Dolgin has been active in Zionist activities of Hapoel Hamizrachi, was vice president of the Los Angeles Zionist District and is a member of the advisory committee of the Jewish Na tional Fund. 

Among the National Organizations which have accorded him high offices, are the Rabbinical Council of America, of which he was National Vice 

President and is now an Executive 

Committee Member; the Hapoel Hamizrachi of America on which he is vice chairman of the Administrative 

Committee; the Joint Distribution

Committee on whose western regional committee he served and the National Jewish Welfare Board on whose Army and Navy Committee in the West, he served.

At present, the Beth Jacob Congre-

SOUTHWEST JEWRY 

gation is situated in beautiful Beverly

Hills where it is rapidly growing as an institution of Orthodox Judaism, religiously, educationally and socially. Rabbi Dolgin is acting as director of the Hillel Hebrew Academy at Beth Jacob Congregation. The Acad emy, which is the largest intensive Jewish School in the area, consists of an elementary Yeshivah Day School, an afternoon Hebrew School, a Kindergarten Nursery, Religious School and home summer Camp Aliyah. 

Rabbi Dolgin is married to the former Shirley Gorodeisky of Bronx, New 

York and is the father of four daughters, Saralee, Gaola, Sharonbeth and 

Marcia, and a son, Michael Jacob.

AB. BARENFELD 

Prominent philanthropist, born in 

Derazhney, Podol, Russia, March 15, 1888, son of Chaim and Feige Barenfeld. Father was a prominent merchant and commissioner. Received a

Jewish education till ten, was known as an intellectual student, but did not give much time for the studies.

His father was in the U. S. a short period, returned a changed man, influenced by the American way of life. 

But things did not go very well, so he left again for the U.S. This time he took the whole family with him. Arriving in N. Y. before the high holidays. 

His father then paid $50 to learn the cutting trade, he also had to work 

4 weeks without pay.

Young Ab. was placed in a haberdashery store, but his boss used him for errands only, which Ab. did not like. He started then for himself as a news-boy, and he went to sell his papers in the evenings, visiting the meeting locals, where he would get for his papers 5 cents instead of selling them on the streets for a penny.

He also sold papers under the old 

Brooklyn bridge and since he did not know the English language as yet, he had "trouble" from his non-Jewish competitors, but he remembers well that he was protected and defended. by his friends, chiefly by two he will never forget, for they in time became very famous men in politics. His best friends were Al. Smith, who in later years was a candidate for President of the U. S. and Jim Farley, Postmaster General. 

Up to the age of sixteen Ab. Barenfeld went through all the hardships that so many immigrants had to go through, but at the age of 18 he was well settled in Philadelphia, being the proud owner of a half block of houses, and he was also with his father, the owner of a restaurant on Vine Street. 

Employing 16 waiters and dishwashers, the restaurant was open day and night, father working it during the days and Ab. ran the business night time.

But the crisis of 1907 wiped out their real estate business, and they tried very hard to save at least the restaurant, hoping to weather the crisis.

He at that time became acquainted with a girl who came from his own town in the old country, but his father objected to her, for he wanted to marry him off to a rich girl . . . but 

Ab. was in love, so despite father's objections he married his sweetheart with whom he lives happily until this very day. 

Times were bad then, but eventually he worked himself up again, as a milkman in Pert Amboy, N. J. It was

SOUTHWEST JEWRY 

151 there that his wife gave birth to a girl, Rose, (now Mrs. Sam Glaser).

From there they moved to New York, Ab. did not like N.Y., as he noticed the way the storekeepers were sitting idle, waiting for a customer, he felt that that was not a productive way of living, he then decided to become a farmer which is a more productive way, but farm work was too hard for him. Five times in his life he returned to farming, but finally he decided that farming was not for him. 

They then purchased a market in 

New Brunswick, and in this venture they succeeded, made enough money to deal in real estate in which they were very successful.

All went well, until another crisis hit the country in 1929. Again Barenfeld was wiped out, lost all he possessed. 

His eldest son, Jack Barenfeld, then left for Los Angeles, opened a jobbing house and was doing very well. He advised his father to follow him and try to settle here. 

A younger son, Charles, followed and the Barenfelds began to miss their children, and so they purchased an old Buick and although every one predicted that the Jelopy will never make it, they nevertheless took a chance and made it. 

He started here as a jobber of neckwear, purchasing the wares in New 

York, but in time Barenfeld began to manufacture neckwear here. Starting at first with 4 machines, Mr. and Mrs. 

Barenfeld both worked day and night, but gradually they worked up the business, moving to 719 S. Los Angeles, running 60 machines. 

Then he changed into manufacturing men's shirts, and he was especially fortunate in producing the "Don

Juan" shirt, which became well advertised all through U. S., Canada, and Phillipines and Hawaii. And so up to 1945 the shirt business yielded large profits and Ab. Barenfeld felt that he can already retire, and ever since he did retire he decided that he will dedicate himself to communal work, helping institutions of learning, helping local institutions and above all, helping Israel. 

Ab. Barenfeld is a Board Member of the J.N.F., Central Committee City of Hope, Hon. President, Marmarosh 

Federation of America, President of the Jr. Hebrew High School, Past 

President Child Rescue. He also was instrumental in establishing 2 children's homes in Israel, is a contributor of a laboratory at the Bar Elon 

University, he helped build 64 homes in the Colony B'nai B'rack; established also a free loan fund in Israel with his own capital. 

Life member of Mizrachi Org., B'nai 

B'rith, a Guardian of the Old Folks

Jewish Home a large investor in Israel Bonds, and a liberal supporter of local, national and Israel Institutions.

He is the donor of a room in the cancer hospital at the City of Hope, and he was honored with a life portrait which decorates the dining room. 

He is also known all over as the Jewish "Father Flanagan," being the founder of a "Boy's Town" in Israel.

As far as we know he is also the only one who keeps up a special second house for Sabbath. Come Friday afternoon, he and Mrs. Barenfeld move out of their week-days home, stay over the Sabbath in a special home close to their House of Worship Shaarei-Tefilah. 

Mrs. Barenfeld, being very pious, shares all his philanthropic endeavors, supporting him in all his undertakings.

RABBI OSHER ZILBERSTEIN 

Born in Mezhiritch, in the province of Wolyn, Russia (now Poland). The tenth generation of Rabbis, son-after son. At the age of seventeen, in 1908, he became rabbi of the town, and in 

1918 went to Kiev, capital of the 

Ukraine, to continue in the rabbinate. 

In 1925 he arrived in Winnipeg, Canada, where he served as a rabbi until 1935, when he came to Los Angeles and assumed the spiritual leadership of the Breed Street Synagogue, one of the largest orthodox congregations west of Chicago.

He was one of the founders and is, at present, dean of the Los Angeles

Jewish Academy. It is largely through his efforts that a parochial school in connection with the Academy has recently been instituted, and has received the approval of the Los An geles Board of Education. 

He is president of the Orthodox 

Rabbis Misrad (organization in Los 

Angeles), a member of the local presidium of the Mizrachi Organization, He was one of eleven delegates to attend the American Jewish Conference as a representative of Los Angeles Jewry. 

He is married and has five children.

In Transition By HERB BRIN 

Publisher of "Heritage"

We are a community in transition. 

There is a new face to Jewish life in galuth being reflected in the Southland that is both unique and dramatic.

And Jewish people of this region are blessed with an historic opportunity of achieving one of the pinacles of Jewish culture. 

One would express the hope that the civilization being molded in our midst contain seeds of vitality and eternity for other Jewish communities have flourished upon the land in other days, and like that of Alexandria, have vanished. 

Our's is the first Jewish community to be created out of a mass migration of Jewish people, who, for the most part, are native to America. 

This is not a community of the ghetto transplanted, nor of Germans of 

Judaic persuasion.

This is an endemic Jewish community with roots in the rights of man and in the electrifying concepts of individual freedom as encased in the Declaration of Independence.

To this community have come both opportunity and challenge. 

Within a generation there has been completed a mass movement within the larger migration of Jewish people to the West. 

For even as a vital Jewish community took root and flourished proudly in the Boyle Heights section with all its reflection of Eastern Jewish structure a concomittant movement took place away from Bovle Heights bringing Jewish families into the totality of Southland living; to the Westside and to the suburbs. 

Jewish people bought homes and moved to modern apartments in the 

Wilshire District, Westwood, Rancho Park and within the past 10 years an estimated 100,000 Jewish people flocked to San Fernando Valley. 

Scholars, business men, artists, writers took leading roles in the cultural and industrial development of the West even as other Jewish figures became part of the historical development of the city a hundred years ago. 

A breathtaking new art form the motion picture 

Jewish genius and enterprise. 

was pioneered by

SOUTHWEST JEWRY 

Jewish scientists are in the forefront of research at Caltech, UCLA, and in institutions such as City of Hope, Cedars of Lebanon Hospital, Mount Sinai Hospital.

In the migration, a top-heavy structure of organizational life developed. 

There seems to be an organization of some sort for every minyon, each with its special appeal and urgency.

More temples and synagogues are being built here now than had ever before been under construction at one time anywhere else on earth. 

An estimated $10,000,000 in temple building is taking place in the Los 

Angeles area alone.

These structures are creating new ideas in beauty and design, and Jewish artistry is in its finest hour. 

Two universities flourish here under Jewish auspices:

the University of 

Judaism and Hebrew Union College College of Jewish Studies. Orthodox circles are urging most seriously the establishment of a West Coast branch of 

Yeshivah University.

In many quarters, a medical school under Jewish auspices is considered an ultimate goal of the community.

A most serious need that must be met is that of expansion of services to Jewish aged for present facilities are terribly inadequate to serve the rapidly growing community and its parallel situation of an increased aged population. 

Taking an important position in the development of Jewish life in the area are the various cultural centers being established by Jewish Centers Association. 

Nerve-center for this development is Westside Jewish Community Center, a magnificent structure devoted to cultural development of Jewish people in the West.

Community direction is the responsibility of the Los Angeles Jewish Community Council, which each year mounts the United Jewish Welfare Fund a fund campaign seeking to unify contributions to Jewish welfare needs. 

The fund in great measure supports agencies of the Federation of Jewish Welfare Organization, the welfare arm of the community. 

Many movements are under way for an ultimate merger of LAJCC and Federation, but a most difficult problem of organic structure of the resultant merger has made unity still a matter for tomorrow. 

And while the ideal of a single annual fund campaign agency exists in UJWF, numerous other drives for special projects go on throughout the year, making fund-raising an apparent end to community direction. 

No articulate Jewish literature is able to burst forth upon the Southland scene.

This may be due to the fact that publishing in the Jewish field is captive of the East. 

The years ahead may prove challenging in this regard, too.

Ours is a dynamic community in transition. 

It is vibrant, articulate, a proud community in motion. 

One must be ever upon the alert that it may not contain the seeds of its own destruction.

SOUTHWEST JEWRY 

159 

The Los Angeles Yiddish High School By DR. DAVID BRIDGER 

The history of the Los Angeles Jewish community, especially that concerning its cultural aspects, would not be complete without calling attention to the role of the L. A. Yiddish High School, known as the Yiddish Mittelshul. Over ten years ago the leaders of every active Yiddish group in our community were fired with the ambition of establishing a secondary school for the graduates of the Yiddish elementary schols maintained by the Workmen's Circle, the Labor Zionist Organization, and the Jewish People's Fraternal Order. Prominent among the enthusiasts of this project were Max Holtzman, Alex Robin, and Harry Rottblatt, who were inspired and assisted by the late Peter M. Kahn, then the chairman of the Bureau of Jewish Education. The then organized Yiddish High School, was not only the first secondary educational institution in this community, but also the first successful attempt in uniting the divergent ideological Yiddishist groups in our community, in a common effort to establish a secondary Jewish school that would meet the educational need of all those in need of a higher and more intensive Jewish education. The curriculum of the Yiddish High School, including Yiddish and Hebrew language and literature, Bible, Jewish History, Jewish Institutional life, Jewish Holidays, Jewish music, etc., was so designed as to represent a common denominator of the educational objectives of all the sponsoring groups.

The success of the Yiddish High School was phenominal. It became one of the most popular educational institutions in the community, directed by enthusiastic workers and assisted by hundreds of sympathizers. Six years after the establishment of the first unit in the Boyle Height area, it became necessary to establish a second unit in the West Side, in order to accomodate the graduates of the elementary Yiddish schools in that area. A new stage in the development of the Yiddish High School began after the Jewish Community Council has decided to suspend its support of the J.P.F.O. schoolls. In order to preserve the integrity of the Yiddish High School, and to obviate the need of its being conducted by individuals representing the different ideological groups, the Bureau of Jewish Education became its actual sponsor by appointing a special committee to act as the board of the school. 

To date the Yiddish High School has graduated over one hundred students. 

Many of them have continued and still continue their Jewish studies in the special classes at the University of Judaism. The graduating Exercises, and other educational public affairs of the Yiddish High School have always enjoyed great popularity, showing real evidence of scholastic accomplishments and of its well-balanced meaningful and positive educational program. 

One of the outstanding extra-curricular activities of the Yiddish High School was the establishment and operation of the Yiddish Kinder Theater. From 

1950 to 1955 students of the Yiddish High School and the Yiddish elementary schools, under the directorship of professionals, have appeared in several artistic and stimulating performances, and were enthusiastically applauded by thousands of Yiddish speaking people, and friends of Jewish education and culture. The popularity of the Kinder Theater grew with each performance and it won national acclaim and admiration.

Unfortunately, the decline of the Jewish population in the Boyle Heights area and the subsequent liquidation of some of the Yiddish schools, has contributed to a drastic decline in the enrollment of the Yiddish High School. The East Side branch had to be given up, and both the staff and the activities of the Yiddish High School had to be curtailed. Despite the difficult struggle for its existence,

SOUTHWEST JEWRY 

the popularity of the Yiddish High School has not diminished, and its affairs are conducted by a sizeable group of enthusiastic and devoted workers, who consider the present numerical decline in the school population only transitory stage.

The continued existence of this remarkable and most needed institution depends a great deal on the future strength and development of the local Yiddish elementary schools. It is imperative that new elementary Yiddish-Hebrew schools be organized in new areas with a growing Jewish population. The fate of the Yiddish High School, and of the Yiddish elementary schools depends on the forsight, initiative, and devotion of all those who have for many years so enthusiastically and unselfishly supported secular Yiddish-Hebrew education. 

The community support for such schools, will be assured only when those who desire this type of an educational program, will support it wholeheartedly and continuously.

HARRY ROTBLATT

In May 1919, he became affiliated with the Jewish Daily Forward, which position he held to his very last day.

As manager of an inffuencial newsnaner he gained recognition and the highest respect and love for his passionate way of promoting welfare, education and social service in numerous fields.

MARK CARTER 

Mark Carter was born in Rostov, Russia, December 12, 1884, educated in Russia. Left Europe in 1921, arrived in New York on September 1st, same year.

Came to Los Angeles and settled here on September 23rd. 1921. One of the Los Angeles communal leaders he has been asociated with the Los 

Angeles Sanitarium, known now as the City of Hope, serving it for thirty vears. he still is a member of the 

Board of Directors, and chairman of the Orthodox Relief Committee for the City of Hope. 

Past president of the Central Jewish

Comittee, he has built that group from a membership of only 33 constituent organizations to over one hundred in a period of only four years. Under his administration, the Annual Carnival and Town Fair, held as a benefit, was inaugurated in 1933, an event that has since become an integral part of community life.

In 1936 he accepted the presidency of the City of Hope, serving the maximum of three years in this capacity, and presiding over the organization during a period which saw the construction of many new buildings. In 

1939, he was elected treasurer, holding this post until 1944.

As devoted as he is to the City of 

Hope, he always finds time to identify himself with other vital projects undertaken in the community. He was elected chairman in 1930 of a movement started by B. C. Vladek to provide tools for de-classed Jews in Russia, remaining active until 1933, and in 1940 became California State chairman of the ORT which has as its propram the re-training economic rehabilitation of the Jews in Europe. In 1952 

Elected the Vice President of American ORT Federation.

In 1934 he became a member of the Trustees of the Federation of Jewish Welfare Organizations, serving for ten years until a change in the by-laws limited members to no more than two terms. 

Marired, has seven children, one was a Lieutenant in the United States 

Marine Corps.

Long a believer in the necessity for organization to further the best interests of the community at large, he was among the first to recognize that one of the greatest needs of the rapidly expanding Jewish population of Los Angeles was some sort of coordinating group which could unify the many and carried segments of Jewish thought. He envisioned a democratically conceived body which could both represent and serve its constituent organizations, and consequently assisted in the formation of the United Jewish

Community, predecessor of the Los 

Angeles Jewish Community Council.

He has served as a member of the Community Council Board since its

SOUTHWEST JEWRY 

163 inception and still today is a member of the Board of the Jewish Community Council. 

He was one of the founders of the

United Jewish Welfare Fund in 1935 

and at its first meeting was elected to the third vice presidency, the following year assuming the second vice presidency. He recognized from the outset the great possibilities for unity and cohesive community action inherent in the Welfare Fund set-up to meet the challenge of relief and rescue needs of European Jewry, and for the upbuilding of Palestine as a haven. 

In 1937, Carter's particular talents in working with people and their organizations resulted in his election as chairman of the Welfare Fund's Divis ion of Organizations, which up to that time had only been able to raise about $1,700 a year. Under his direction the first year, the Division hit the $16,000 mark, and in 1943 totalled $58,000. In 

1944, with the Welfare Fund organizational structure changed to meet new conditions, he mobilized the Organizational Division to help provide the greatest army of volunteer workers ever enrolled in the drive, assisting immeasurably in Los Angeles' reaching its all-time high record of $2,050,000 in the "Dawn of Liberation" campaign. After serving the chairmanship for 15 years he resigned when he became ill last year. 

Ever by his side, through most of his years in Los Angeles, has been his charming wife, Reva, herself one of the most active leaders in the Sanatorium movement and always a guiding inspiration in Mark's greatest projects.

She has served as gracious hostess at literally hundreds of important committee sessions held in their home, and at major social functions during the J.C.R.A.'s historic conventions. In her own right, Mrs. Carter directs as president, and has for the last 15 years of the Mahnia Silverberg Auxiliary, the "mother" auxiliary of the Sanatorium. 

Mrs. Carter was president for two terms of Chapter 11, B'nai B'rith.

LOS ANGELES YIDDISH CULTURE CLUB 

Thirty years ago a group of men and women representing various organizations, social and cultural, gathered to formulate plans for the establishment of a club, the object of which would be the promotion of Jewish culture - literature, music and art. Since then the Los 

Angeles Yiddish Culture Club has become one of the most popular organizations in town, and has attracted large groups to its weekly gatherings at their headquarters at 4213 Monroe St. 

One of the organizers of the Club, was E. Tennenholtz, well known Jewish artist, who is still active and interested in Jewish art. Jos. L. Malamut was first secretary. 

Most of the Jewish writers of note who reside in Los Angeles are members of the Culture Club. 

Writers, musicians, and artists of prominence, arriving in Los Angeles, always receive a hearty welcome at the Club. 

Under the leadership of I. Friedland, assisted by M. L. Kopiloff, and other active members, the club has succeeded in publishing a Journal "Cheshbon", once a year, thus making a valuable contribution to Jewish literature. In the course of its existence the club has sponsored hundreds of concerts, lectures and other cultural affairs which have enriched the spiritual life of our community. 

The Yiddish Culture Club main tains a fine library of Yiddish and

Hebrew literature, and it is to the credit of the Librarian, Nathan Azolin that this library is being kept in the best of condition. Chaver Azolin devotes all of his time freely, even a fully paid librarian could not do a better job. 

Following are the officers and directors of the Los Angeles Yiddish Culture Club:

H. Rosenblatt. Hon. Chairman; I. 

Friedland. President: Ben Zukerman, Vice-President; Samuel Breinin, Vice 

Chairman; Jacob Newman, Program 

Director; S. Levin, Corresponding 

Secretary; M. L. Kopeloff, Finance 

Secretary; A. Schwartz, Treasurer; 

Shlomo Zukerman, M. Winarsky.

Finance Committee: 

Minnie Schwartz, Mailing; Sophie 

Cutler, House Manager; Nathan Azolin, Librarian; Aaron Ayeroff, delegate to L. A. Jewish Community Council.

Members of Action Committee: 

A. Babitz, S. Zukerman, Celia 

Zukerman, Ch. Cutler, Liuba Breinin,

A. A. Kaminker and Aniuta Kopeloff. 

JOSEPH PANITZ 

Residence: 3606 Laurel Canyon 

Blvd., Studio City, Calif. Retired. 

Born May 28, 1892 at Plotzk, Poland.

Parents were assimilationist, were not interested in things Jewish, but somehow, young Panitz nevertheless was nationalistically inclined from his youth and arriving in London, England, began to interest himself with things cultural, and later settling in 

New York in 1910, he followed the ideology of cultural nationalism. 

Married Feigele Maron, in New

York, and they moved to Los Angeles in 1921, remaining here since then. 

It is to Joseph Panitz's credit that 

Feigele Panitz, being a fine singer and interpreter of Folk Songs, cultivated a fine taste for the better song,

SOUTHWEST JEWRY 

165 always fitting in her program to suit the intellectual audience. 

Since 1939, Joseph Panitz activated himself with the League for Labor

Palestine, and simultaneously became interested in Jewish education, devoting his time and energy to the Folk- Shullen Movement. He is one of the founders of the Institute of Jewish

Education, a Past Director of the Jew ish Community Council, Past Chairman of the Labor Zionist Council, the "Ofboi," and until this very day he is 

President of the Institute, devoting most of his time to the cultural and educational activities. Panitz is known to be a militant Zionist, fighting for principles, and recently using all his influence with the P-TA, feeling that since they are an intellectual element, they should take over the work around the Folk-Shul with the children as well as with the adults.

Joseph Panitz is indeed to be considered among the leading Labor Zionists in Los Angeles, never tiring, always ready to promote culture and education. Panitz generally is leading, others follow.

FEIGELE PANITZ 

Residence, 3606 Laurel Canyon 

Blvd., Studio City, Calif. Born in 

Dvinsk, 62 years ago, orphaned at the age of five, the youngest of seven children and was raised with an aunt, her mother's sister at Riga, receiving her education up to the age of fifteen.

Afterwards she was brought over to 

London, England, by an older sister.

It was there that she joined a dramatic group, the same group that Samuel 

Goldenburg, famous actor sprang up from. 

Feigele had her first debut at the "Arbeiter Friend" Club, in London, England, at a reception in honor of the late celebrated poet, Abraham 

Reisin, who discovered her and insisted that she join him to the United

States. In fact they both came on the same steamer.

Arriving in the U. S. in 1911, she married Joseph Panitz, she was then only 17. They have spent the next ten years in N. Y. Afterwards they moved to Los Angeles. 

It was in Los Angeles that Feigele was really discovered as soon as she began to sing in intimate circles. It did not take long and Feigele warmed herself into thousands of Yiddish lovers, music lovers and indeed she was crowned as the "Isa Kramer" of the

Pacific Coast. Since then Feigele has made a name for herself, as she has made a great contribution to culture, interpreting not only the folk songs, but also the songs of Rosenblatt, Yehohosh, Manie Leib, Leivick and of many other great poets. Feigele appeared on the W. E. V. D. Radio station in New York. She has toured

Mexico, Cuba, Israel and was received everywhere with great enthusiasm.

In Los Angeles, Feigele Panitz is indeed an institution, a cultural institution, loved, appreciated and admired by all. Daughter Sylvia, a pianist, married to Erno Neufeld, violinist. There are 2 grandchildren.

BARNEY (BARUCH) BACHELIS 

1886-1953 

Born Sept. 28, 1886, in Koshwata, Kiev, Russia, of a Chasidic family, and has received a traditional orthodox up-bringing. 

At the age of fifteen he was brought by his parents to this country. They first settled in New York, and later moved to San Francisco. 

Made his debute as a poet in Dr. 

Wortsman's California Jewish Voice, San Francisco. In 1916 we find him in 

Portland, Oregon, where he published a periodical "Yiddisher Geist," the publication folded up after printing eight numbers.

In 1917, he settled in Los Angeles and was a steady contributor to the 

Jewish daily paper "Die Zeit." 

Simultaneously Bachelis contributed poems to the following Publications: 

"Kibetzer," "Kundess," "Die Feder," "Friei Arbeiter Stimme," "Zukunft," "Forward," "Warheit," "The Day," and in 1921 he helped Dr. Wortzman to revive the "Calif. Jewish Voice," in 

Los Angeles, where he remained a regular contributor on the staff. 

In book form: "Shmeltziche," (short monolegues) in 1926, and "Menshen

Fun Mein Dor," (People of my generation) 278 p.p., published post humously by his widow, Elizabeth 

Bachelis, and their son, Dr. Robert Bachelis.

Bibliography: Z. Raizin, Lexicon, 

Rose Shomer-Bachelis, Californier 

Yom-Tov Bletter, Sept. 1955.

ROSE SHOMER BACHELIS 

The daughter of the noted Yiddish novelist and playwrite, N. M. Schaikewitz, Shomer, was born in Odessa on 

February 12, 1883. Came to the U. S. with her parents in 1891, and settled in 

New York City. Graduated Public 

School, High School and the "Eastman 

Business College." In 1906 entered the field of social work, and for fourteen years, as social worker, worked for the "United Hebrew Charities."

In 1914, during the first World War, was one of the organizers of the "Red Mogen David", which gave material aid to the families of the legionaires who volunteered to serve in the army of Gen. Allenby in Palestine.

In 1915, organized "The Women's 

Jewish Congress Organization", and was elected as a delegate to the first 

American Jewish Congress which convened in Philadelphia in 1918.

SOUTHWEST JEWRY 

Together with her late sister Miriam 

Shomer Zunser, wrote four plays: 

"Eine Fun Folk", "Circus Maidel", 

"In the Hent Fun Gezetz" and the operetta "Der Zingendiker Ganev"

The plays were produced in the leading Jewish theatres in New York, with such eminent artists as Mme. Bertha 

Kalish, Molly Picon, Ludwig Zats, Samuel Goldenberg and Celia Adler, in the leading parts. 

Settled in Los Angeles in 1930. In 

1934 organized the "Los Angeles 

Chapter of Mailam", an organization which sponsored the development of Jewish music in Palestine and in the 

Diaspora, and served as its president for six years. 

With afore-mentioned sister Miriam wrote the biography of their late father, N. M. Schaikewitz, Shomer. 

The biography, "Undzer Foter Shomer" was published in New York in 1950, and in 1953, a Hebrew translation of it was published in Israel. In 

1955 her book "Portraits of Renowned 

Jewish Personalities" written in the 

Yiddish language, was published in

Los Angeles. She is also the author of "Moods and Moments", a collection of verse, written in the English language, and which was published in 

Los Angeles in 1956. 

Was married to Mr. William Silverman, who died in 1942, and is now the wife of Mr. Samuel Bachelis.

EUGENE J. FRIEDMAN 

He brought with him to New York his training in architectural woodwork design gained at Leipsig, Germany. 

In 1911 he established at Pico and 

San Pedro Street, Los Angeles, a cabinet shop with a total capital of $175.00. 

Eugene J. Friedman, born in Hungary on February 21, 1885, lost both parents when he was 11. After intermittent and brief schooling and working he went to Vienna for 2 and years then to Leipsig for 6 years before coming to America.

Cautiously and steadily he built his company, Standard Cabinet Works. 

He moved his shop to 3700 Central

Avenue and finally to its present twoacre location at 1800 East Washington Blvd.

The company, chief executives now include Eugene's son, Ernest, married to the former Ruth Lyons, and George 

Polinger, husband of Eugene's daughter Margaret. His son-in-law, Milton 

S. Tyre, husband of his other daughter 

June, is the company's lawyer.

Standard Cabinet Works, Inc. to day is one of the outstanding manufacturers of woodwork for public buildings, fine stores, banks, hotels and offices in the United States. 

Like her husband, Charlotte Weiss was born in Budapest, Hungary. There she attended high school and came to 

Los Angeles where she married Eugene. She raised their 3 children and helped him during the early days of building the business. She passed away November 22, 1953. There are 8 grandchildren surviving her.

Despite his early business struggle 

Eugene Friedman soon upon his arrival in Los Angeles recognized his responsibilities as a member of the community. He joined the B'nai B'rith 

(Wilshire Boulevard) Temple and later also became a member and helped to build the present Sinai Temple.

He is a member of B'nai B'rith 

Lodge 487, Westgate Masonic Lodge, Elks and Al Malaikah Shrine. He has been a member of the Downtown Optmists Club for 35 years and is a 

Hillcrest Country Club member.

He is actively engaged on the 

Boards of Guardians and Home for the Aged. He is a member of the building committee for the Cedars of 

Lebanon Hospital.

He was one of the pioneer members of the Los Angeles Chamber of Commerce and Merchants and Manufacturers Association where his company still retains membership. 

Mr. Friedman's name is always found among the generous contributors not only to worthy Jewish causes but to other community charities such as Community Chest and Red Cross.

SOUTHWEST JEWRY MORRIS RUDIN 

Today he is the head of the company bearing his name, his son, Arnold is a partner in the firm.

His many activities in Los Angeles have characterized Mr. Rudin as a progressive and entirely forward looking personality, being well known in charitable circles as a liberal patron of Jewish institutions and worthy causes in general. 

ARNOLD RUDIN 

Personifying the growth of Los Angeles in many business activities, Morris Rudin, of the Rudin Furniture 

Company, one of the finest and largest concerns of its kind in the city, has progressed hand in hand with the city's growth. 

Morris Rudin was born in Brusilow, province of Kiev, Russia, in 1897.

His father was a forester and kept young Morris in a Jewish institution of learning until he was 13 years old and became Bar-Mitzvah. 

He showed unusual abilities for study at an early age, but was compelled by circumstances to go to work. 

He migrated from Russia to Canada and his first stop was Toronto, engaging there in furniture manufacturing as a cabinet maker and upholsterer.

Being anxious for travel, he led through all Canadian Western provinces and then to Alaska, where he tried fishing and mining. 

Later he came to Seattle, Washington, but he did not tarry there. He then moved to San Francisco, arriving there on Armistice Day, Nov. 11, 1918.

His career really began in Los Angeles, 18 months later and since then he has been a pioneer in the industry of manufacturing overstuffed furniture direct from factory to consumer. 

Residence: 9133 Sawyer Street, Los 

Angeles. Business address: 1369 W. 

Washington Blvd.

Arnold Rudin was born in Los Angeles in 1921 and made school here, having graduated from University of 

California with the degree of Bachelor of Arts, also a graduate of school of business from Stanford University. 

Married Beverly Klein, their children are: Michael, 7, Ralph, 6, and 

Jay, 3 years old.

Arnold served in the U. S. Army from 1942 to 1946, rank of Captain, served with the U. S. Military government in Germany, and when he returned in 1946, he joined his father's firm, the Rudin Furniture Company as a partner in which business they both continue successfully. 

Arnold is active in the United Jew ish Welfare Fund campaigns furni ture division, he is also a director of the Denver Jewish National Hospital.

NATHAN Z. PESSIN 

1894-1947 

Nathan Z. Pessin,, one of the primemovers in Labor Zionist Circles in Los 

Angeles, passed away in June, 1947, about ten years ago, while flying on a mission from Los Angeles to 

New York to an annual conference of the Histadruth Labor Zionist conclave.

Although a period of ten years has elapsed since then, he nevertheless has not been forgotten, the loss is still being felt here for he rendered services to the community here and his place is still irreplaceable and will not be filled for a long time. 

Nathan Z. Pessin was one of those personalities who when dedicated to social service he puts all his personal interests aside. 

Born in Homel, Russia, in 1894 and it was there that he received a traditional Jewish education and early in his life he began to realize the tragedy of being a Jew in exile and was filled with the ideology of Zion, and striving to help create a Homeland for the 

Jewish people in Israel. 

As a youth of nineteen he was brought over by his sister, Faye, to 

Sioux City, Iowa, in 1913, but he did not stay there very long, as the first world war broke out, and the Jewish 

Legion was organized, Nathan Pessin answered the call and joined the Jewish Legion, and remained in Palestine for a period of two years.

The two years with the Legion and then in Palestine convinced him even more that the only hope for the Jewish people is the aim and the strive to rehabilitate Israel in the land of Israel. 

In 1925 he settled in Los Angeles, established himself in business, raised a family and activated himself even more in social work, in cultural activities and above all, dedicating himself to the cause of Labor Palestine. 

To his credit goes the forming of the Labor Palestine Group of the 

"OIFBOY" a Yiddish cultural branch of the Poalei-Zion in Los Angeles, and alongside with it he was also instrumental in helping to establish the "Institute of Jewish Education" at 

8339 W. 3rd St., was elected president of the Farband Labor Council, as well as the representative of the Poalei- Zion groups in the Los Angeles Jewish 

Community Council.

Despite of being a very busy man he never shirked work as long as he felt that it would help the Jewish cause and would influence others to go along and support the movements that were dear to him.

During the last year of his life he was also the Chairman of the Histadruth Campaign in Los Angeles and in service for this mission he lost his life flving to the annual conference, never reaching New York.

For the love of his people, for his devotion to Labor Zionism and supnort for cutlure and education, he will long be remembered and his memory will be always cherished by all who came to know and love him. 

Nathan Z. Pessin is being survived by two sons: Dr. Harold Pessin, a dentist. and Archie Pessin an associate in the Pessin Grocers Corp., established by his late father. 

May his memory be blessed.

174

SOUTHWEST JEWRY ZUDUK ENISMAN 

1870-1956 

Born Minkowitz, Podol, Russia, of a very poor family, and was compelled to begin struggling for a living at the age of fourteen. 

His schooling ended at 14, and afterwards he was under the influence of his older brother, Leib, who was known as a great scholar. Poverty compelled the family to send off little

Zuduk to an aunt in Brailow, Roumania, and it was there that he learned the bookbinding trade. For a period of two years Zuduk worked just for his food. He then left for Bucharest and there he was beginning to get paid for his work and when he reached seventeen he returned to Russia, choosing the big city, Odessa. 

Being a bookbinder did not only offer him a livelihood but also opened for him a new world, the world of books, the kind of books he never saw before in his little home-town. 

For awhile he was under the influence of revolutionary ideas, but eventually led him to the ideology of Labor Zionism, known as Poalei-Zionism at which idealogy he stuck for a period of sixty years to the very end of his life. 

Here Zuduk was known as the

OLDEST POALEI-ZION. 

Arrived in the U. S. in 1905, together with his wife, Esther, and his eldest son, Hyman. They settled in 

Minneapolis, Minn., where he became active in the Labor Zionist movement. 

His second son, Paul was born there after their arrival.

Enisman and his family moved to Los Angeles in 1914, and husband and wife were leading in the Poalei-Zion movement, his wife taking an active part in the Pioneer-Women Organiza tion, but Mrs. Enisman took sick and after a prolonged illness she passed away July 11th, 1930.

Enisman being so close to various books, account of being a bookbinder, took to the pen, and soon became a contributor to various local and national publications such as the "Freie 

Arbeiter Stimme," "Warheit," and many others, publishing poems and 

Phelitons under the pen name "The 

Village Bookbinder."

Being an old-timer here, he was a regular contributor to the "Calif. Jewish Voice," during the years that the paper was published in Yiddish, displaying fine talent. 

After Esther's death he visited Israel and returning he published his impressions in the Calif. Jewish Voice.

He also revisited Israel after the establishment of the State of Israel. His last years he prepared a volume of episodes of his fruitful life, this manuscript he entrusted to his life-long friend, Moshe Glaser, himself an author and a poet. Feeling that his days were numbered, he asked Moshe

Glaser to edit and sort out his manuscripts, hoping that his sons may care to publish this material posthumously, but in case they will not publish them, he expressed a wish that Glaser should place the manuscript with the IVO (Jewish Scientific Institute, N. Y.)

Enisman left a classical Will in which he pointed out a way of life for his children. This Will was read at his funeral by same Moshe Glaser, and was afterwards published in 

Malamut's California Yom-Tov Bletter. 

Enisman will be long remembered and revered here. May his memory be blessed.

SOUTHWEST JEWRY 

175 

Farband Labor Zionist Order 

In Los Angeles, organized some 40 years ago as a single branch, the 

Borochow Branch No. 122, with 20 members. For almost 15 years this was the only branch in Los Angeles that represented the main body, carrying out its policies along the lines of social and cultural endeavors and assisting in the establishment of a Jewish Homeland in Israel. 

From this beginning, the Alliance has grown to embrace ten branches, nine of them Yiddish-speaking and one English, which function in various parts of the city and represent a total membership of approximately 2000. 

Efforts of the groups are concentrated on the Labor Zionist movement, support of the Jewish National Fund, the Histadrut Campaign, cultural activities, and the maintenance of Yiddish schools and community centers. At present there is one such center at 8339 W. 3rd Street, known as the Institute of Jewish 

Education. Jos. Panitz is president of the Institute. The institute houses the 

Yiddish Folkschul, and currently is planning to build a large auditorium adjacent to the institute.

The City Council, comprised of representatives of each branch, holds a regular bi-monthly meeting, coordinating and directing the activities of the branches. 

The Alliance, better known as the Farband Labor Zionist Order, has 10. branches in Los Angeles coordinated by a Council. Maurice Deitch is the chairman of the council. Jack Kahn is the vice chairman and A. Babitz is the council secretary. Shlomo Hocjberg is the executive director.

The Farband Council in the past twelve months deserves a lot of credit for its cultural activities, mainly for the invitation of such lecturers as Mordecai Shtrigler, well known poet and capable editor of the "Yiddisher Kemfer," Poalei- Zion weekly organ, and Chaim Grade, noted poet and essayist. Both poets have held their audience spellbound, and will be well remembered here. 

Another triumph for the Farband was the dramatic performance of a group of Yiddish actors at the Wilshire-Ebel Theatre, which was filled to capacity. 

The last successful venture, financially as well as morally goes the credit to their new dinamic execuaive director, Shlomo Hochberg, a man of fine literary taste and exceptional abilities to organize and coordinate fine programs that interest the intellectuals as well as the plain folks.

The Farband should consider itself indeed fortunate for having obtained Hochberg as its director. It is certain that Southern California has much to gain by his services as executive director.

and "ITZCHOK BEN ELCHNON." RABBI BERNARD COHEN 

Born in Sokoly, near Bialystok, Poland. He came to Pittsburgh, Pa., in 1922 from which date until 1928 he taught at Pittsburgh Hebrew Schools.

During this period he was representative of the National Jewish Daily "The 

Day" for the middle Western states, and was the editor of the special Pittsburgh edition of "The Day" from 1925 to 1928.

In 1928 he was graduated from the 

University of Pittsburgh with the degree of Bachelor of Arts, majoring in 

Sociology. Five years later, in 1933, of Southern California and was admitted in 1942 as candidate for Doctorate by the Sociology Department of the University. 

Rabbi Cohen is the author of numerous articles on Sociological Educational, and Literary topics which include, among others: "The Immigrant 

Jew in America," (1927); "Lester 

Ward, the Father of American Sociology" (1914); "The Sociological 

Concepts of Charles H. Cooley" 

(1941); "Democracy, Education and

Religion" (2 articles published in B.

S. Calif. Socialogy Dept.

MARION TRAVIS 

Marion Travis was four years old when his parents emmigrated from Latvia to the United States. He was reared and educated at Marietta, Ohio, and entered the oil business there. He married Esther Diane Aronson, of New York City, and then moved to Tulsa, Oklahoma, where he achieved considerable financial success. 

He and his brothers founded the

Jewish Kahilla there, and built the first Orthodox Synagogue in that city. 

The Travis brothers, with their extensive charities, helped establish the 

Yeshiva College of New York City, of which their brother-in-law, the late 

Dr. Bernard Revel, was its founder and president. Marion Travis has been a lifelong Zionist and for a number of years was on the Board of Directors of the ZOA. During and after the first World War, he was also on the Board of the Joint Distribution 

Committee. He personally underwrote

SOUTHWEST JEWRY 

the entire administration expense of the JDC, as well as the salary of the 

Executive Director, Mr. Jacob Billikoff, for the four most crucial years of its existence so that the JDC could make the claim that 100% of every dollar collected would go for relief. 

Mr. Travis suffered complete financial reverses during the great deprestools he is able to explain many of the so-called hidden or esoteric incidents that have baffled scholars throughout the ages, such as the search for and the burning of the red heifer, the linsy-woolsy prohibition, the logical reason for the first two Commandments, being stated as they are, as well as numerous other original commentaries. An extensive bibliography supports his many conclusions.

His wife, Diane Travis, is a noted artist in her own right, having studied in New York and Paris, and exhibited in those cities as well as in Philadelphia, New Orleans, Tulsa, Los Angeles, and elsewhere. 

She is a granddaughter of the sion and had to start all over again.

He is still engaged in the oil business in Texas, and with associates is engaged in the farming and cattle feeding business in California.

His hobby is biblical literature. He is a student of Egyptology, and from time to time he writes on the subject.

The Vantage Press is publishing his book, "The Birth of a People", which will be out some time this summer. 

This book is a modern reconstruction of the Old Testament history and was made possible through his use of such modern tools as archaeology, geology, Egyptology, Egyptian history, farming, and animal husbandry. In this book, Mr. Travis produces a sound and original theory of the happenings and events that led up to the Children of Israel becoming enslaved. He shows who the Pharaoh was that knew not Joseph, and how Moses could have liberated the Children of Israel and sustained them in the wilderness without the miracles or the supernatural happenings that abound in the Old Testament. Their second daughter, Elaine, married to the artist, Ron Blumberg, has three children.

Moses Montefiore, Baron de Hirsh and his "Very Imperial Greatness" grew out likenesses of Tolstoi, Gorki, the Tzar of all Russia - Nikolas the

Up to 12 years he studied Hebrew, the Bible, Talmud, then he switched to worldly knowledge: European languages, mathematics, history, latin, preparing himself for entrance to college. 

At that time, the call of the wild feels strongest, the drawing board lures, and he enters the Imperial Art 

Academy in Woroniesh, where he spends two years. 

The instructors of the Academy

SOUTHWEST JEWRY 

predict a bright future.

Meanwhile the atmosphere in Russia is tense, uneasy and becomes more and more restless. There is no future for the young, who wants to "achieve", and he emigrates to America.

In New York, the usual struggle for survival. Between classes in art and running to exhibitions, he takes up photography for "a living", in spare time he writes poetry. After a time in strange fields, he returns to his own. 

In California, Kovner Grodsky resides for the last ten years.

He is constantly at his easel, he works in oil, portraits, landscapes, still life. His canvases are exhibited in many galleries, art and culture clubs.

Born in Russia, 1904, brought to U.

DAVID LENEMAN Residence: 802 N. Kingsroad. Of fice: 1901 W. 11th Street. Born in 

Warsaw, Poland, July 14, 1906. Graduated from Krinskies Gymnasium, 1902, then attended a Polish School for Engineering, up to 1925. 

Left for Palestine, and attended the Bezalel Art School for two years; later he began to paint for himself. 

Came to Paris, France in 1929, remaining there until 1934, then returning to Palestine for another 2 years.

Exhibited there and painted for the exhibitions on the markets of the Near 

East. Went to South America, exhibited there successfully, later was engaged as teacher of Arts and Crafts for the government, which also arranged an official exhibition of his paintings.

Arrived in U.S. in 1941, exhibited there successfully.

Served with the U.S. Army, 194244, then settled in L. A.

H. ROIZENBLATT 

1878 - 1956 

Born in village Rishoshe, Podol,

Russia. Arrived in U.S. 1891, had a taste of the sweatshop very early, later made Jamaica Normal College for teachers, made literary debut in Jewish Daily Forward, continued to contribute to various publications, made Vilno. At the same time he taught 

Hebrew, Jewish History. Greatly in fluenced by the Zionist ideals, his early writings were in Hebrew. 

In 1911 Hirshbein, whose folk plays had already made him world renown left Czarist Russia and came to the 

United States. "A FARVORFEN VIN- 

KEL" served to launch the Yiddish

Art Theatre under the Direction of Morris Schwartz in 1918. The classic folk plays of Hirschbein such as the 

"POOSTE KRETCHME" and "DEM SHMIDT'S TECHTER" and "THE 

GREENE FELDER" were the making of a new artistic epoch in the Yiddish Theatre.

After many years of traveling he returned to the United States and set- an attempt to edit Yiddish paper in 

Detroit, (1916), in 1921 settled in Los 

Angeles, continuing as a poet, contributing to N. Y. Dailies, weeklies and monthly publications. 

Roizenblatt succeeded to get recognition and acclaim by his critics, and at the end of his life was considered the dean of the living Yiddish poets.

He was loved and admired by the Yiddish readers the world over. 

Was survived by his widow, Malke, and a daughter, Rena.

PERETZ HIRSHBEIN 

(1880-1948) 

Born in the country, 2 Kilometers from the village of Klesczele, Province of Grodno, Lithuania. Childhood spent in the environs of his home, the water mill, which greatly influenced his writing. Cheder and Yeshiva were the education of his early youth. At the age of 20, in 1900 he studied in tled in New York City. In 1934 a son was born to Esther and Peretz Hirshbein and was named Omus. 

In 1941 he made his permanent home in Los Angeles, California. 

The 50 years between 1888 and 1933, and the development of Jewish life in America during those years were the subject matter for his crowning achievement, in the form of a novel, the trilogy "BOVEL".

While in Los Angeles he lectured and participated in the development of Jewish Community life. He died in 

Los Angeles in 1948 after a prolonged illness.

SOUTHWEST JEWRY LAMED SHAPIRO 

1878-1948 

One of the greatest Novelist Yiddish literature ever produced. Born at 

Rezhishtchev, Kiev, 1878. Deceased 

Aug. 25, 1948. 

First debut in 1904. Was not very prolific, but created a furor with his "The Cross," depicting stories of massacres, was immediately acclaimed as a great talent. Had a very dramatic but colorful history, having lost his spouse Freidel, who actually sacrificed her life for him, to comfort him and make him happy. 

Ever since Freidel passed away the genious Lamed Shapiro took to drink, and no matter how much his personal friends tried to help him, he did not recover.

Bibliography: Bal-Machshoves; Sh. 

Niger; Dr. Chaim Zhitlovsky; and many, many others.

SHEEN MILLER 

Born in Philipowitch, Volin, Feb. 

1895. Raised in the village Dibrievke, then continued at the Yeshivah. Left 

Russia at the age of 18, published short stories in various publications. 

Was a contributor to the Cleveland 

Jewish World for a number of years. 

Settled in Los Angeles in 1924, remaining here since. 

By now Sheen Miller is being recognized as the finest novelist in Yiddish. 

Married to Olia Wall. 

Miller is surrounded by hosts of friends and admirers, lovers of Yiddish literature who have formed a standing Committee publishing his volumes year after year. He is very productive despite of the tragic fact that his health has been failing him already for a number of years.

ABRAHAM BARMACK 

1893-1948 

Abraham Barmack, age 55, died suddenly in L. A., May 29, 1948, and was mourned by all who came to know him and love him, for he was a very gentle person, and a leader in 

Labor Zionist circles. Barmack was the Executive Director of the Poalei- 

Zion Party in Southern California. 

Born at Zwihil, Volin, of an orthoall of his life. 

Made a great contribution to that movement and for this he will be long remembered. 

Survived by his widow, Rose, who still continues in this movement and by a son, Haim and a daughter, Libby, who is an artist in her own rights, contributing to various publications. 

May his memory be blessed.

Also in many collections in France, Italy, England, Saul Rabino was born in Odessa, known as "Petit Paris". His father was a Rabbi, as also were many of his forebearers. He received a traditional Jewish education followed by "Yeshiva". From an early age Mr.

Rabino was interested in art, but it was not until his young manhood that

SOUTHWEST JEWRY 

he was able to pursue that expression. 

After studying at the Imperial Art School, as a pupil of Professor Constandi he journeyed to Paris, where in 1913, he entered the Ecole Des Arts. In 1915 he was in Geneva and returned to Odessa the next year, staying until 1921 when he left for 

Constantinople. A year later he came to the United States.

During his childhood he was tremendously impressed by the pogroms in Russia. In years following the revolution in 1919, he witnessed the Civil War with its periods of starvation and disregard for human life. In his work, whether in oils, lithographs, watercolors, black-and-white or murals, Rabino reflects his struggles in childhood and adolescence; his fight against poverty; his impression of the cruelty of mankind toward each other. He is known for his portraits of the Prophets and in his vision and hopes for his people as well as for all mankind. 

Mr. Rabino has exhibited his works in Paris, New York, Montreal, Israel, in the Los Angeles Museum of Arts,

San Diego Art Gallery and in the 

Palace of Legion of Honor in San

Francisco. Many of his works are to be found in the permanent collection at the Los Angeles Public Library and in the Library of Congress, in Washington, D. C. The artist's work is also in many museum and private collections.

CHARLES KROWN 

Residence, 400 Hauser, L.A. Born, Odessa, Dec. 25, 1898, a son of Kalman and Sarah. Was moved to this country at the age of 4, with his parents, who settled in Chicago. Attended 

Cheder until Bar-Mitzva, also public and high school, he then took up pharmacy and graduated in 1918. 

Volunteered for the first world war.

In Los Angeles since 1920, worked as a pharmacist for the Owl Drug Co. for several years, in 1920 he went back to Chicago, then traveled for a drug concern, also for photography. Later he started in same lines for himself and was very successful. In 1948 he moved to Los Angeles, where he settled permanently. 

Marired Charlotte (Horween) of 

Chicago in 1931. One daughter, Elaine 

Adelle Klein.

Member Temple Israel of Holly wood, a follower of Zionism since he was 14. But in 1946 he became very active in Histadruth, Jewish National Fund, State of Israel Bonds, one of the largest investors. Active in Hebrew

University, University of Judaism and a generous supporter of everything educational and cultural. 

Charles Krown is considered an outstanding personality in the Jewish Community of Los Angeles being known for his generosity, always ready to serve the community giving of his time and wealth, also using his good influence on his fellowmen to follow him in humanitarian causes.

The Los Angeles Jewish Community Its Growth and Development By AARON RICHE 

Paradoxical as it may seem to the reader, the fact is that the Editor of this compendium of useful community information actually assigned to the present writer the task of condensing into some few hundred words the fantastic and fascinating story of the growth and development of the Los Angeles Jewish community. What should rightly and properly be a full, complete and detailed account of the creation and development of this modern miracle in community growth is now to be reduced to a "once-over-lightly" treatment of an amazingly complicated subject.

Three decades have witnessed the metamorphosis of a once rather quiet and peaceful Jewish community of less than fifty thousand souls into a conglomeration of more than four hundred thousand men, women and children, sprawled over the vast area of metropolitan Los Angeles. Drawn from every part of the continental United States as well as from the countries of Europe, with infusions even from Asia and Africa, this Jewish community, particularly in the last fifteen years, has undergone a growth and transformation unparalleled in recorded history.

From its early pueblo days, when a handful of hardy pioneers laid the foundations of a Jewish microcosm in the dusty village that was Los Angeles, up to the early nineteen-forties, the growth of the straggling city and its contained Jewish community was steady and substantial. Climate was the lodestone or magnet that drew to the reclaimed desert lands on the Pacific hundreds of families each year, building a modest superstructure upon the firm foundations of the early settlers. And it was in these quiet and peaceful years that the Jewish community as such began to take on recognizable form and shape as a vital part of the American Jewish community. 

Traditional Jewish institutions and organizations came into being to serve the people of the community - a home for aged, hospital, orphan's home, home for chronic invalids, sanatarium for tuberculars, freeloan society, home for wayfarers, home for working girls, the beginnings of Jewish centers, community Hebrew schools and a Welfare Fund. Antedating or concurrent with these were the local branches of ntaional organizations B'nai B'rith, the various Zionist bodies, Workmen's Circle, Council of Jewish Women, Hadassah, Congress and the usual complement of cultural, social and educational societies. 

Federation came into being to bring order out of a degree of chaos in the field of philanthropy and finally in 1933, the Jewish Community Council was formed to coordinate organized Jewish life in Los Angeles and to build an effective, functioning, dynamic Jewish community.

It was the period that followed World War II that saw the steady flow of migration to Los Angeles turn into a veritable torrent or flood, with as many as four thousand men, women and children added to the local Jewish population in one month. Ill prepared as the existing community, with its institutions and organizations, was to receive and assimilate these onrushing thousands, community leaders struggled manfully to provide at least a minimum of Jewish

SOUTHWEST JEWRY 

facilities for these new arrivals. Spreading over the vast terrain of the greatly enlarged metropolis and bringing the established habits and folkways of the cities from whence they came, they presented a problem and a challenge that no othr like community had ever faced. 

Keeping pace with the unprecedentd growth of the community have been the increasing responsibilities not only of building an integrated community with the facilities required to maintain community health and vigor, but of responding adequately to the needs of displaced and distressed Jews the world over as well as giving substantial financial aid and support to those heroically maintaining the new State of Israel. The process of integration cannot be forced but it can and will grow and develop through the unselfish and unceasing efforts of community leaders and workers alike all those who feel and believe that destiny has marked this bursting-at-the-seams community to write new and glorious pages in the history of the Jewish people. 

It is the hope of this writer that with others more competent he may yet have the opportunity to tell the story of the growth and development of the Los Angeles Jewish community as it needs to be told for the benefit of future generations. But right now a humble apology for a skimpy surface sketch. 

Southern California Jewish

Historical Society By JUSTIN G. TURNER 

The Southern California Jewish Historical Society was organized December 

2, 1952 at the home of Justin G. Turner, 501 Woodruff Avenue, Westwood, by a group of history-minded individuals. We were desirous of preserving for the future such records, data and memorandum pertaining to our early Jewish citizens as was available before time would run out and such data would be lost for all time. We desired to stimulate an interest in local and regional, as well as our national history, and prepare for the Tercentenary celebration which was then being planned on a national level, observing the 300th anniversary of the coming of the first group of Jews to the United Sttaes. In addition, the year 1954 was to be the Centennial celebration of the first organized group of our people in Southern California, the Los Angeles Hebrew Benevolent Society, which was founded in 1854 by 30 of its residents. We were also desirous of having a history of the Jews of California prepared for publication. 

We were desirous of cooperating with, and stimulating interest in the American Jewish Historical Society which was organized in 1892. Our Society was the first local chapter of the American Jewish Historical Society with its headquarters in New York City, and with whom we are directly affiliated. 

The Society is one of the most important repositories of manuscripts, books and periodicals in the field of American-Jewish history and has, since its inception, issued its publications regularly dealing with American Jewish history. 

The members of our Society, in cooperation with the Jewish Community Council, acted as a committee to head the local Tercentenary program. A 

Tercentenary Manual for the use of the heads of the Jewish Institutions in Southern California was prepared. The Manual proved to be of great aid in disseminating information and arranging programs, and also contained a concise history of the Jews of Los Angeles, prepared by Justin G. Turner. An excellent exhibit of American Judaica was displayed in the rotunda of the Jewish

SOUTHWEST JEWRY 

195 Community Council Building at 590 N. Vermont Avenue and a catalogue of the items exhibited was made available. A request for permission to use this exhibit was made by the Tercentenary Committee of San Francisco. Accordingly, this exhibit was transferred to the San Francisco Public Library, and to the State Capitol Building in Sacramento. A conducted tour by the members of the 

Legislature, with a special lecture, featured the exhibit at Sacramento, and a joint resolution was adopted by both houses of the California Legislature, approving and commending our Tercentenary celebration. 

Our program has met with great success, locally, and our annual dinner meetings have been well attended. We have been addressed by speakers of national prominence, such as Dr. Bertram Korn of Philadelphia, author of "American Jewry and the Civil War"; the esteemed Dr. deSola Pool, of the 

Congregation Shearith Israel of New York City, the first synagogue organized in the United States; by Dr. Arnold Witznitzer, the first graduate student of the University of Judaism, and author of "History of the Jews of Brazil"; Dr. 

Abraham Nasatir, of San Diego College, author of numerous volumes and monographs on American hisfory; Mrs. Vina Delmar, author of "Beloved" being a fictionalized version of the life of Judah P. Benjamin; Dr. Howard Sachar of the University of California Hillel Foundation, and others.

At our annual meeting of February 4, 1954, a facsimile of the Constitution and By-Laws of the Hebrew Benevolent Society of Los Angeles, which was organized more than a hundred years ago, in 1854, was presented to our members.

In December 1955, Hellman Way Campus of the University of Southern California was dedicated to the memory of Isiah W. Hellman, one of the three men who donated the land upon which the University was built. Mr. Hellman helped establish and became the President of the Farmers and Merchants Bank, the first one incorporated in Southern California. 

On April 6, 1956 a historical marker was placed at 218 South Broadway, the site of Congregation B'nai B'rith, the first synagogue in Southern California, which was organized in 1854. 

On April 17, 1956 Marco Newmark was presented with a Resolution, passed by the Los Angeles City Council, honoring him as a member of a pioneer family and a worthy author upon the occasion of the publication of his volume "Jottings of Southern California History". Mr. Newmark, with his brother

Maurice, had edited "Sixty Years in Southern California" which was written by his father, and which is a standard basic source book of early Southern California history.

The 300th Anniversary of Asser Levy's appearance in the Burgomaster's Court in New Amsterdam requesting that he be admitted as a Burger was celebrated April 11, 1957 with a special program. Dr. Ernest Simon, Dean of the School of Education of the Hebrew University at Jerusalem, discussed Jewish 

Foundations of the Philosophy of Civil Rights; Justin G. Turner discussed Rabbi 

Menassah ben Israel of Amsterdam and his reltaion with Oliver Cromwell which paved the way for restoring civil rights to the Jews of England and Dr. Monte 

Salvin and Ben Chipkin presented a history of the Haym Saloman statute which was presented to the City of Los Angeles in connection with the sale of bonds during World War II. 

Justin G. Turner served as President of the Society since its inception and 

Marco Newmark was made honorary President. Other officers are Dr. Samuel

Dinin, Aaron Riche and Dr. Philip L. Seman, vice-presidents; Peter Kahn, Jr. treasurer; Julius Bisno, recording secretary; Benjamin Dwoskin, corresponding secretary; William R. Blumenthal, financial secretary; Rabbi Rudolph Lupo, archivist and Jacob Zeitlin, associate archivist.
