The History of Brandeis Camp Institute By SHLOMO BARDIN 

The Brandeis Camp Institute owes its existence to the late Supreme Court Justice Louis D. Brandeis, who was seriously concerned about what he called the flight of the young Jew from Judaism.

Dedicated in name and spirit to the ideals of Justice Brandeis, and with his active interest as an impetus, the Brandeis Camp Institute was initiated in 1941. 

Since that time some 5000 young people have learned at first hand the dignity and worthwhileness of Jewish identity, and have been trained for leadership in the American Jewish Community.

Under the leadership of Dr. Shlomo Bardin, founder and director, the Institutes have been based on the ideas of the Danish Volk High Schools and infused with cooperative spirit which marks the social achievement of Jews in Israel. In the usual American summer camp setting, the students live cooperatively share the work . . . share experiences and learn from each other. The first Brandeis Camp Institute was opened in 1941 as a pilot plant by sharing the facilities of the Young Judaea Camp at Amherst, New Hampshire, under the sponsorship of the American Zionist Youth Commission. The start of this experiment in youth training was made possible largely by the generous gifts of time and resources of Harry and Louis Levine of Leominster, Massachusetts, and 

Dewey Stone* of Brockton, Massachusetts. Since 1948, Brandeis Camp Institute has been an independent organization, not affiliated with any national or local body. 

During 1942 the Institute was visited by Mr. Abraham Goodman** who became deeply impressed with this novel approach to American Jewish youth. He found and made possible the purchase of the beautiful property at Winterdale, Pennsylvania - and the Brandeis Camp Institute of the East was established. 

The immediate impressive results achieved by the Brandeis Camp Institute of the East produced demands by parents, community leaders and youth itself for additional institutes elsewhere in the United States. 

A group of prominent Jewish leaders on the West Coast, under the leader ship of Julius Fligelman***, Yoland D. Markson, Edward Meltzer, Judge and 

Mrs. Joseph J. Rifkind and others, were inspired by contact with graduates of the 

Brandeis Camp Institute of the East, and raised their own funds in 1947 to purchase a 2,200 acre ranch at Santa Susana, California. In 1947 Brandeis Camp 

Institute of the West was opened.

Each of the camps were national in the sense that the campers came from all over the United States and Canada.

The campers are given interesting and stimulating courses on: 

1. Jewish life and problems on the American scene. 

2. Survey of Jewish history. 

*Chairman of the Board of Brandeis Youth Foundation, which sponsors Bran deis Camp Institute. 

**President of Brandeis Youth Foundation. 

**Chairman of the Board of Brandeis Camp Institute.

SOUTHWEST JEWRY 3. Achievements and problems of Israel. 

4. History, theory and practice of Zionism. 

5. Jewish literature, poetry and art throughout the ages. 

6. Meaning of observances, ceremonies and Jewish customs, explanation of 

Sabbath Services. 

7. Principles of group work. 

Of great importance are the special workshops conducted in the following fields: Music, drama, creative modern dance, folk dance, arts and crafts, creative writing, and group discussion.

Learning at Brandeis Camp is an experience in itself. Two hours of each morning are devoted to lectures and discussion. This information is then assimiated in bull sessions, in programs, in music, dance and drama until the subject matter becomes a living reality. Audio-visual equipment is used extensively. 

The importance of the California camp, with its suitable climate which enabled operations on a year around basis, became apparent to the leaders of the movement. In 1952 the headquarters of the Institute were moved from New York to Los Angeles, and later to Santa Susana, where they are at present. Activities now are confined to the Western camp. 

The lessons learned from the close and intimate contact with college youth throughout the years made apparent the need to present festivals, traditions and various phases of the Jewish heritage in a manner which is understandable and enjoyable to the young, native born American Jewish youth. This led to the development of programs embracing the Sabbath, Hanukkah, Purim, Tree Planting Festival, and recently the Passover Seder. With the help of outstanding writers such as Mac Benoff, Michael Blankfort, Norman Corwin, Dore Schary and others, a number of such programs have been created and may be made available to the public.

While the training of college youth for leadership in the American Jewish Community remains to this day the focal point of the Institute's program, it has branched out in two other directions in the last five years. Encouraged by the positive results attained in the work with the college youth, the Institute developed a children's camp. In 1953 Camp Alonim was established for children from eight to sixteen. The children's camp was initiated with the desire to create a model camp, combining a good camping program animated by a Jewish spirit, and provide opportunities for joyous Jewish living. Another aspect of the children's camp is the training of counselors, which program is ever enlarging. Camp 

Alonim, which is situated on the beautiful 2,200 acre estate at Santa Susana, California, ranks among the top in the nation in its facilities. 

Since 1953, Brandeis Camp Institute has been very active in the adult field through its weekend institutes, which are conducted regularly throughout the year, and which have proved very successful. Judging by the reactions of people who have attended the weekend institutes, one may say that each institute weekend is a unique, unforgettable experience.

Stimulating and absorbing discussions and seminars are led by outstanding personalities in various fields of Jewish cultural and educational endeavors. Ideas are shared and exchanged among the friendly guests, who come from varied and interesting walks of life. Beliefs to live by are reaffirmed, and one learns something of the beauty, the strength, and the wisdom of a rich and treasured heritage.

SOUTHWEST JEWRY 

199 

BRANDEIS CAMP LEADERS 

Leadership training sessions, starting June 23 and July 28, standing from left, Dr. Shlomo Bardin, director; Philip Wain, vice-president; Julius Fligelman, board chairman; Steve Broidy, president; Judge Joseph J. Riffkind, vice-president, and Dewey Stone of Brockton, Mass., board chairman of the sponsoring Brandeis Youth Foundation. Below from left are author-screenwriter 

Michael Blankfort, camp lecturer; Edward Meltzer and Yoland D. Markson, vice-presidents and 

Benjamin Swig of San Francisco, a director.

SOUTHWEST JEWRY 

In recent years the United Jewish Appeal has been making use of the Brandeis Camp Institute for its own lay leaders. Perhaps one may be allowed to quote from a letter from Rabbi Herbert A. Friedman, the eminent leader of the 

United Jewish Appeal: 

"I cannot begin to tell you what a tremendous impact the weekend at the Brandeis Camp has meant to the campaign. It is really amazing. Almost every single person who was present was individually affected in terms of his own giving and working. Furthermore, the communities to which the men returned have all benefitted immeasurably by the stimulation. I have rarely seen a leadership institute conducted in which the results were so tangibly measured in such a short period of time."

Since 1955 there has grown up an alumni association, consisting of people who have attended either the college leadership institute or an adult weekend institute. This association, which is called "House of the Book", has regular programs for its members in camp, and also under its auspices are presented the Brandeis programs for the general public. 

The progress of the Brandeis Camp Institute and its impact on the community are due to a very large extent to the ceaseless efforts of a group of devoted community leaders of great ability. These people form its Board of Directors, and a number of them have been serving the Institute for many years. Heading this group is Steve Broidy, President of the Institute, Mr. and Mrs. Louis H. 

Boyar, Philip Wain, and others.

In addition to the Board, there are hundreds of men and women, who have helped make Brandeis Camp Institute what it is today.

SOUTHWEST JEWERY The Veterans of the Labor Zionist 

Movement In Los Angeles

From informative sources, from interviews (though not officially) we learn that the first POALEI-ZION branch in Los Angeles, was organized as early as in the year of 1913. A number of chaverim gathered in a private home of one of the chaverim, and it was there that they decided to have a branch. 

The original group consisted of the following: Fishel Riskin, (still living), Yankel Weiner, Aaron and Chaim Shapiro, I. S. Naumov, Joseph Coodly, and 

Joseph Hirsh.

Later this group was joined by Getzel Weiner, (a brother of Yankel) 

Zecharyo Finkelstein, D. Fisher, I. Gorin, Pearl Lossman, (Mrs. Lossman resides in Israel), Ben Solomon, A. Sessin and Bernard Glickman.

In 1920 this group was joined by Zoduk Enisman, (the oldest Poalei-Zion on the Coast. (See biography of Zoduk Enisman in this volume on page 174.) 

Nathan Reider, who came here from Detroit, Chaim Margolin, (came here from 

Minneapolis) David Gisnet, of San Francisco.

This group was instrumental in founding the "National Radical School," which in later years changed its name to Folk-Shull, as the Farband Labor Zionist Order adopted later the name for all its shullen over the United States and Canada, under the suppervision and with the support of the Farband. 

It was this very group that helped support the "Yiddisher Kemfer,” and when in 1920 the Poalei-Zion Party published the Daily News paper, "Die Zeit," the Los Angeles group though very poor in material means, subscribed the paper's stock for thousands of dollars, hoping that the paper will survive and live to be on a paying basis.

These were the pioneers for the "Geverkshaften Campaign," which in later years adopted the name "Histadruth."

Still later there came Dr. Nathan Saltzman, Abraham Babitz Abraham Barmack and Chas. Brown. These last four certainly put the movement on the map, and ever since then the Labor Zionist movement in Los Angeles has written a beautiful chapter, with its numerous branches of Poalei-Zion, Farband Labor 

Zionist Order, Pioneer Women branches, Habonim, Folk-Shull, Institute of 

Jewish Education, etc.

The first group was already very active in the time of the first world war, as they took an active part in the movement for a Jewish Legion to help liberate 

Israel, then known as Palestine.

In those days there were no paid directors, every member considered himself as a general as well as a plain soldier, never tiring, always ready for any and all jobs needed to be done.

We therefore felt that this volume would not be complete, if at least the names of those veterans were omitted.

We are living in a too busy world. Too soon do we forget those who have laid foundations here for culture, for health sanitariums, for everything dear to us.

SOUTHWEST JEWERY ISRAEL OSMAN 

1887 - 1951 graduating in 1926. When he returned to Lithuania, he then shortly afterwards exhibited paintings and sculpture pieces at Kovna.

While in his native country he was engaged by the National Government to design and erect two Monuments in memory of the country's first ten years of Independence. 

At an Exhibition held in 1952, at the YM-YWHA in Montreal he displayed 75 items including sculptures 

Born in Vishkow, Poland of a chasidic leniage, was sent off to cheder at the age of three. He was later instructed by his father, and until 17 he lived in a strict orthodox atmosphere. In 1906 he made his debut in the Yiddishe Vochenblatt, edited by 

Hillel Zeitlin.

Arrived in U. S. in 1909, published short stories in "Tageblatt," Kundus, and "Yiddishe Folk." In 1911 he went back to Poland, and returned in 1913, was engaged as a teacher for 5 years, then settled in Los Angeles, becoming representative of the "Day" until his very end. 

Author of two volumes, "Sefer 

Hanisyonot," in Hebrew and the same volume was published in Yiddish, "Dos buch foon Nisyonos." 

Visited Israel in 1950, spent 6 months there. Passed away June 8, 1951. Survived by his widow, Pnino

Osman, who is Pacifific Coast representative of the same N. Y. daily paper and by a son, Shlomo Osman.

Leaders of B'nai B'rith Western Jewry 

Southern California B'nai B'rith leaders today include (left to right) Irving Schulman, District 

Israel Chairman; James Rostler, Membership Chairman; Jacob Shemano of San Francisco, 1956-57 Grand President; Philip Klutznick, World President of B'nai B'rith; Aaron Riche, Board of Governors, Supreme Lodge; Past President Jacob J. Lieberman; and Arthur Wolpe who will be District Grand President in 1958. 

B'nai B'rith is the common denominator of western Jewry. Being neither 

Orthodox, Conservative nor Reform, it speaks for all three. It includes in its membership the Jew who joins for athletic pursuits, as well as the soul-searcher who is interested in the recently re-organized Committee for Adult Jewish Education. The do-gooder, the card player, the seeker for comradeship; the intellectual, the artist and the patriot, each finds his special hunger well fed by B'nai B'rith's well rounded programs. 

And it does a job! It's Hillel and BBYO programs serve youth on hundreds of high schools and college campuses. It's anti-defamation league battles hatred and bigotry on every front! And B'nai B'rith daily serves as a perpetual source of manpower for Boy Scouts, U. S. Savings Bonds, Community Chest, Red 

Cross, Blood Bank, and similar worthy projects.

It's big. Over 21,000 members in 110 lodges in the eight western states and 

British Columbia now constitute District Grand Lodge Number 4. Past Grand 

President Edward Zeisler is Executive Secretary with offices at 594 Market Street in San Francisco, with branch offices at 6425 Hollywood Blvd. in Hollywood.

Last year Moe Kudler, Los Angeles, was District Grand President. In July

SOUTHWEST JEWERY 

Jacob Shemano of San Francisco will pass his gavel of office to Samuel Tarshis of Seattle. In the following year Beverly Hills Attorney Arthur Wolpe will become Grand President. And then Burton Lewkowitz of Phoenix, each leader in his own way setting the stage for his fellows to say, "Behold, how good it is for brethren to dwell together in harmony. 

Southern California B'nai B'rith Council

President Alvin Hirsch receives Israel's citation from Past Grand President Moe Kudler. 

Arthur Wolpe, Beverly Hills Attorney, will follow Samuel Tarshis of Seattle as District

Grand President at a western states convention in the summer of 1958. 

Hadassah Women's Zionist Organization 

Hadassah, the Women's Zionist Organization of America, was founded in 1912 by twelve American women under the leadership of Henrietta Szold. 

Today, Hadassah is the largest Jewish women's organization in the world numbering over 300,000 members in 1200 chapters and groups throughout the 

United States (with Puerto Rico and Alaska). 

The purposes of Hadassah are: 

In Israel: 

1-To provide medical services in Israel through a model program of preventive, diagnostic and curative medical work. 

2-To encourage and support medical education in Israel. 

3-To assist in the rescue, immigration, settlement and vocational training of Jewish youth from countries where Jewish life and institutions are threatened. 

4-To support a program of land reclamation and afforestation in Israel. 

In America: 

5-To foster the ethics and ideals of Judaism among its members through an elaborate educational program. 

6-To help strengthen democracy through a program of information and study designed to awaken its members to their civic responsibilities and an awareness of the issues of the day. 

The National president of Hadassah is Dr. Miriam Freund.

Mrs. Jack Karp is Chairman of the West Coast Board which comprises the following three regions:

Central Pacific Coast Region-Mrs. Norman Rogers, President Northern Pacific Coast Region-Mrs. Morrie Gumbert, President Southern Pacific Coast Region-Mrs. Leo Hirsh, President

The Jewish Committee for Personal Service and

The Gateways Story 

Jewish Committee for Personal Service was founded in San Francisco in 1021 by Rabbi Eugene Meyer and Irving Lipsitch, executive director of their 

Federation of Jewish Welfare Organization. Rabbi Rudolph I. Coffee is also considered one of the founders although he joined the others within the first year of their operations. William R. Blumenthal was its first executive director.

The Jewish Committee for Personal Service was established to work with the inmates and patients of state penal and mental institutions and with their families towards the ends of rehabilitation. Friendly visiting, release planning, personal, social, and chaplaincy services were major objectives. The agency was conceived as one of the pattern of community welfare services and staffed with professionally trained social workers. It was organized as a state-wide service working out of San Francisco but having financial and structural ties to every Jewish Community in the state. It served as a channel of communication from the 

Jewish communities to the institutions and their climates. 

A regional office was started in Los Angeles in 1923. Rabbi Edgar F. Magnin was its first president and Rabbi Maxwell Dubin was its first executive secretary. 

Rabbi Magnin remained its president for 17 years. Others to follow were Judge 

Isaac Pacht, Harry Graham Balter, Donald A. Breyer, Rabbi Morton Bauman, Victor Carter, George Olincy and Morton Silverman. Executive Directors following Rabbi Dubin were Ruben Resnick, Nathan Sloate and Louis Ziskind. The latter has been in office for the past 18 years.

By reason of the phenomenal growth of the Jewish population in Southern California the regional office soon outgrew its parent and very early in its development became an automonous structure. It serves through both of its offices in San 

Francisco and Los Angeles approximately 2500 Jewish persons within the institution of California. It serves in every area of the penal process from arrest, trial and sentencing, through discharge from institution and parole. It serves in planning care and treatment programs for the mentally ill. 

In 1940 through the efforts of Mrs. Edgar F. Magnin it established a system of auxiliaries of lay volunteers called Personal Service Associates to take over community education and friendly visiting functions in the institutions. These grew to a variety of groups totalling over 1500 women in communities from San Diego to Bakersfield. One group in particular, the Los Angeles chapter, was a major factor in establishing Gateways, Jewish Committee for Personal Services mental treatment and rehabilitation center. Personal Service Associates changed their name in 1957 to Gateways Associates for Mental Health.

In our visitations to state mental hospitals three things were noticed which gave rise to the birth of the Gateways program. (1) Approximately 3 of the 

Jewish patients going into state hospitals could be treated locally under rapid treatment methods if same were available. They could be returned to their homes

SOUTHWEST JEWERY 

within 30 to 90 days and thereby preclude the need for state hospitalization. (2) Large numbers of patients, past their acute phase of illness, were receiving no benefit from further hospitalization but were unable to leave because they had no one or no place to go to upon release. (3) 2/3 of the Jewish patients were not known to social agencies before hospitalization-had been self-sufficient and were not indigents.

From these facts Jewish Committee for Personal Service became interested in developing a "halfway or midway house", one which would offer rapid treatment to stem the tide towards state hospitalization, one which would offer temporary shelter for patients who are beyond their acute illness and ready for a trial leave in the community and who would require the finding of jobs and housing through community resources and the help of our agency staff. Hence it would serve groups on the way to and on the way back from state hospitals—a midway house. Jewish Committee for Personal Service tried unsuccessfully for over ten years to get the interest of our central planning and fund raising bodies in this project. Finally in 1953 with the financial help of our affiliate groups and a small group of business men, namely Victor Carter, John Factor, Ben Heller, George Olincy, S. Tilden Norton and Leo Sergenberg, sufficient funds were secured to purchase property and erect a ten-bed residence and a clinic treatment building. Medical staff consisted primarily of volunteer psychiatrists headed by 

Dr. Stephen Schwartz and Dr. Joseph Pessin. Dr. Eugene and Esther Sommerfeld 

Ziskind, psychiatrists and chief of psychiatric services at the Cedars of Lebanon

Hospital, were prominent in early planning as well as in setting up of the medical staff, policies and procedures. 

Today Gateways, under the direction of Dr. Fred Ziegler, chief of staff, has a complete psychiatric treatment team consisting of psychiatrists, psychologists, psychiatric nurses, social workers, occupational and recreational therapists. 

It is a healthy, growing structure filling a real lack in the medical facilities of the community. 

To Louis Ziskind, executive director, goes the credit for initiating, planning, executing and developing the Gateways project. At the time of this writing, Gateways has been in existence for 32 years. It still does not have financing from any of the central fund bodies in the Community such as the Community Chest (which does finance Jewish Committee for Personal Service) or the Jewish Welfare Fund. It depends on support from its auxiliaries, community contribution and low cost fees schedules.

SOUTHWEST JEWERY 

217 

Hollywood Temple Beth El Hollywood Temple Beth El, Hollywood's first congregation and the sole Con servative synagogue in that area, was formally incorporated on January 26, 1922 under a charter granted by the State of California. The congregation conducted its first sacred services in a bungalow on Wilton Place just south of Sunset Boulevard. An improvised Holy Ark, two Scrolls of the Torah and a few folding chairs transformed the living room into a House of Worship - thus launching in a humble and simple way an institutional career which has served the spiritual, educational and cultural needs of the community with unagging zeal and hitlahavut to this day.

The Temple's first permanent edifice was raised up at 1508 North Wilton Place in September, 1922. The little 600-seat Sanctuary began to attract men and women in all walks of life, many from the motion picture colony: Michael 

Curtiz, Carmel Myers, Mervyn LeRoy, Jack Pearl, Edward G. Robinson, George 

Sidney, Hal Wallis, Benjamin Warner, Carl Laemmle, Lew Seiler, Harry Miazlish,

Phil Goldstone, and later, Joe Pasternak (Chairman of the Temple's Advisory 

Board) and Harry "Pop" Sherman.

With the face of the neighborhood being changed radically by the construction of the Hollywood Freeway and because the Jewish population in the area was markedly shifting westward, the Temple in 1949 launched a major effort looking forward to the building of the new Hollywood Temple Beth El. 

Spearheaded by Harry Sherman, Hyman Levine, Samuel Tuch, Maurice Katleman, Dave Leviloff, Leon Helman, Barney Katz and a host of others, the drive for the new edifice resutled in the present impressive structure at 1305 North 

Crescent Heights Boulevard. Dedicated in the Fall of 1952, under the dynamic presidency of Isadore Levine and the spiritual leadership of Rabbi Jacob M. Ott, the Temple Family grew in the brief course of two years from a membership of some 60 families to 700 families, one of the most remarkable bursts of growth in the history of the American synagogue. 

In dedicating itself to the highest concepts of the ways and thoughts of the historic, living faith of Judaism, Temple Beth El reflects in its programs every facet of a creative and visionary communal life. 

The schooling program for children fosters a diversified Sunday School department and program and a daily Hebrew School department. Largely under the principalship of Cantor Nathan Katzman and the supporting spirit of the congregational leadership and its Rabbi, the school system has benefitted from steady improvement and continuing increases in pupil enrollment. There is a 

Junior Congregation which meets on Shabbat mornings in the Levine Chapel.

There is a Sunday Morning T'fillin Club for boys of Post- Bar Mitzvah age. There is a Fireside Chat program conducted by the Rabbi for upper teens. The Junior 

Choir, under the tutorship of Irving Riskin, Music Director of the Temple and President of the Guild of Temple Organists of Southern California, is highly regarded in the community and quite frequently is invited to participate in the adult worship services.

Temple Beth El numbers in its large Family several important and praiseworthy affiliated organizations. The Men's Club, inaugurated in 1952 under the dedicated leadership of Arnold Clejan, has been a steady arm of institutional support. The women of the Sisterhood, carrying on the pioneering tradition of the first women of Temple Beth El, has been a positive force in willing the dream

SOUTHWEST JEWERY 

of the new Temple and in helping sustain its program. The Parent-Teacher 

Association, formerly a special committee of the Sisterhood, grew to independent status in 1952, and through the exemplary devotion of its first President, Mrs. 

Harry Cherques, and her equally dedicated successors, Mrs. Sam Lieberman and 

Mrs. Norman Rivkin, has become a full-fledged autonomy in the panoply of 

Temple organizations. A special Group for single men and women of middle years developed through the leadership efforts of Mae Lever and Til Lever. 

The work of this group to provide school scholarships for needy students has been a highlight in its brief career dating from 1952. An organization for young couples, The Adams and Eves, was formed in the autumn of 1956 under the guidance of the Rabbi. Its first President, Mr. Henry Keleman, in fellowship with the charter membership of the group and its list of officers, have given to the congregation a glimpse into the future wherein it has every expectation that from this tyro affiliate will flow a great strength for the Temple and community. 

In 1956, the Temple Arts and Music Commission, inaugurated at the suggestion of the Rabbi, an annual Forum Series. The program brought to the public platform such world figures as Eleanor Roosevelt and Herbert Morison. The panel program, "Hollywood and the Bible", received international coverage and was particularly noted by the Israeli press. Ignaz Hilsberg, a distinguished concert pianist and Temple member, appeared in concert, and the Festival of Jewish Music received acclaim for presenting the west coast premiere of portions of David Tamkin's opera, "The Dybbuk," featuring Cantor Katzman and the Compinsky Trio. The membership of the Commission includes Dimitri Tiomkin, Joe 

Pasternak, Michael Curtiz, Mrs. Harry Goldman, Mrs. Richard Behr, Mrs. Phil 

Goldstone, Mrs. Harry Herzikoff, Judge Louis Kaufman, Mr. Lewis J. Rachmil, Mrs. Norman Rivkin, Mr. Morris Gurewitz, Mr. Irving Riskin, Cantor Nathan 

Katzman, Mr. Joe Youngerman and Rabbi Jacob Ott.

Since the autumn of 1952, the Adult Education Commission has presented a Sunday morning adult program conducted by Rabbi Jacob Ott. The Rabbi's continuing series of lectures in the field of Jewish history have represented the most comprehensive of its type being offered in the community. At this writing the 

Rabbi has passed his 150th lecture in the series. Other courses offered have been "The Ethics of the Fathers," "The Ways and Thoughts of Jewish Life," and studies in the Tanach.

In 1956 the Temple acquired as a gift from Mr. and Mrs. John Factor the building known as Factor House, located at 1327 North Crescent Heights Boulevard. The facility, in being added to the main Temple building and the Havenhurst Youth House, became necessary because of the continuing growth of the congregation. After such a long and historic record of service, Hollywood Temple 

Beth El is looking forward to raising up yet another structure, its organic school building, to be planned on the confident basis of affording educational facilities to the community for the next decade and one-half. 

Isadore Levine, Morris Gurewitz, Leonard Zanville and Irving Schulman have been the presidents at the new Hollywood Temple Beth El, but their distinguished service became possible because of the handful of pioneering men and women who began the work, and made this Temple truly a House of God.

HYMAN LEVINE SAMUEL TUCH IRVING SCHULMAN, President Rabbi of Hollywood Temple Beth 

El since June 1, 1952.

During World War II, served as combat chaplain with the 83rd In fantry Division in the European The atre, and was the recipient of five battle stars and the Bronze Star Medal with Oak Leaf Cluster. In the last days of the war and during the early part of the occupation, he rendered outstanding service in assisting the concentration camp survivors at Pocking, Dachau, Regensberg, Ravensburg and Deggendorf. He was also mainly instrumental in reorganizing the Israelite Consistoire in Luxembourg for which he was honored by the American Jewish Congress in 1946.

In 1946, he was called to the pulpit of the Anshe Emet Synagogue in 

Chicago as Assistant Rabbi and research assistant in Bible Studies to the late Dr. Solomon Goldman, of blessed memory. 

Rabbi Ott is now serving an unprecented third term as President of the Western States Region of the Zionist Organization of America. He is a Vice-President of the American

Zionist Council of Los Angeles and 

Chairman of its Education and Cul ture Committee. 

A member of the American Society of International Law and the Rabbinical Assembly of America, he was in

221 1955-1956 on the faculty of the Uni versity of Judaism. 

He is a past department chaplain of the Jewish War Veterans of America, Department of Illinois, and was the first Rabbi ever to hold the post of Chaplain of the American Legion's First Division, Department of Illinois. 

Rabbi Ott is a member of the Religious Organizations Committee of the National Conference of Christians and Jews.

Succeeding Harry B. Seelig 

Mr. J. D. Sterling served as President of Sinai Temple in the years 1955 and 1956. In the business world, Mr. 

Sterling serves as the President of the 

Sunset International Petroleum Com pany. He is highly regarded by everyone by whom he comes in contact.

During his tenure of office the permanent site for the new Sinai Temple at Wilshire Boulevard and Holmby Avenue in Westwood was acquired. At the Annual Sinai Dinner held at the Cocoanut Grove at the Ambassador Hotel on May 5, 1957, Mr. 

Sterling was given a very beautiful gold placque in gratitude for his wise counsel and stimulating leadership as

President of Sinai Temple. Mr. 

Sterling is now serving the Jewish community as the President of the Jewish Home for the Aged of Los Angeles, thus adding another link in the golden chain of his service to the

Jewish and the general community. 

Residence: 13500 Wilshire Blvd., Beverly Hills, California. Office: Tishman Building, 3450 Wilshire Boulevard, Los Angeles, California. 

J. David Sterling was born in

Canada, and it was there that he received his early education. 

Married Barbara Abramson, they were blessed with two children: Morton A. Sterling, 33, University of 

Southern California and Barry H. 

Sterling, age 28, a graduate of Stanford University. 

Mr. Sterling is a past president of 

Sinai temple and currently was elected to the office of president of the Jewish Home for the Aged, 325 S. 

Boyle Avenue.

His social and civic affiliations are very numerous and among them he is a Shriner, Mason, member of the Hillcrest Country Club, interested very much in the Brandeis Camp Institute, member of the Board of Mount Sinai

Hospital and Clinic, a liberal supporter of all Jewish and humanitarian causes.

President of the Sunset Internation al Petroleum Corporation with 14,000 stockholders.

SOUTHWEST JEWERY 

Los Angeles Council of Pioneer Women 

In these days of strife in Israel, her soldiers stand ready on every front. 

Soldiers, too, are the more than 50,000 members of Pioneers Women's Organization in America. The Los Angeles Council, with its 28 constituent clubs, can rightfully boast of the unceasing efforts its members have put forth during the past thirty-one years, as a partner to the Moetzet Hapoalot, the Working Women's Council of Israel, who are credited by the Israeli Government with doing 70% of the rehabilitation work for women and children through their establishment and maintenance of children's homes, training farms, nurseries and vocational schools.

The Los Angeles Council raises one of the highest quotas for Moezzet Hapoalot and Child Rescue Fund in the nation, through the individual efforts of the clubs and the major Council projects which include an Annual Bazaar held in the Fall and a Gala Purim Ball held in the Spring. The Council also participates most actively in the annual Histadrut Campaign, with its own Histadrut quota, conducts an intensive activity for Israel Bonds; takes part in the year-round traditional Jewish National Fund activities, and makes an outstanding effort for the annual United Jewish Welfare Fund. 

The Los Angeles Council has always played an active role in the local Jewish community. It participates in community cultural events, social and welfare organizational activities, and is active in the support of all liberal actions in the community. It tries to develop and educate the members to become informed and to develop an awareness not only of Israel and its problems, but of the entire Jewish community and its relationship to the community as a whole. 

The Los Angeles Council is not only concerned with fund-raising. Much emphasis is placed upon increasing the membership and raising the level of the cultural work and general meetings of the groups. It makes its chaverot proud of the fact that they are Pioneer Women, which fosters the concept of social and economic equality. It teaches them that belonging to Pioneer Women is their constructive investment in a way of life, not only for Israelis but for Jewry throughout the world.

In Israel today, a quarter of a million members of Moetzet Hapoalot carry the load of enthusiasm, pleasure, disappointments and work. The Los Angeles 

Council is proud to join the Pioneer Women who march beside them. 

MRS. CELIA POULSHOCK, President MRS. EMANUEL STRICKER, Vice-President MRS. DAVID SIMON, Vice-President

SOUTHWEST JEWERY 

223 

An Appreciation From the Editor 

I owe this third volume of “SOUTHWEST JEWRY" mainly to three friends:

AARON RICHE, the Mr. Los Angeles Jewry, and next, to the dynamic secretary of the LOS ANGELES JEWISH COMMUNITY COUNCIL and WELFARÉ 

FUND, my very dear friend, JULIUS BISNO; and last, but not least, to Mr.

MARCO R. NEWMARK, first and oldest Jewish citizen of Los Angeles. 

Without the authorization of these three dear friends this volume could have never seen the light of day.

But even with their full cooperation, I still had to face a tremendous task: 

This volume entailed an expenditure of close to four thousand dollars. We have money for everything, except for history. 

But then came the cooperation of our dear friend, Rabbi Max Nussbaum, of Temple Israel of Hollywood; Rabbi Israel Chodos, of Temple Sinai; and his beloved president, Eugene M. Rosen, a veteran and builder of Los Angeles

Jewish institutions. Their cooperation made it possible for us to continue. 

There is one more thing to be considered about the creation of this volume: 

The Editor of "Southwest Jewry" is often being termed as a “Rip Van 

Winkle" for the following reason: "Here is a fellow who edited "Southwest Jewery" in 1926 and 1927, thirty years ago, and, lo and behold, he stays away and returns thirty years later and is editing a third volume..." 

Well, I have a reason for that also: 

My very dear friend, “Mr. Los Angeles Jewry," Aaron Riche, is still going as strong, and sometimes stronger, as when I left him here thirty years ago, so why should I not continue? I may have aged, but I never faltered, I never refused a job to be done while I thought I was able to do it, and so, with the help of Riche, Newmark and Bisno, we thought, we can do it, we can leave records of Jewish history for posterity and that is the reason why we did it. 

Looking backwards: We have here records of the Federation, the Jewish 

Community Council, the Welfare Fund, the Community Library, the Hebrew Junior High School, the Mittel-Shull, the Cedars of Lebanon Hospital, the City of Hope, the Mount Sinai Hospital and Clinic, the Jewish Home for the Aged, the Guardians, the Cultural Aspects, the Zionist Organization, the National Fund, the California Home at Reseda, the B'nai B'rith Lodges, the temples they are as well covered as their representatives have cooperated with us. 

If there is a temple or an organization that the reader will not find recorded in this volume, we wish him to know that this and that rabbi, this and that leader was negligent in his duties, or hesitant in his job, because he thought that his temple or organization might not back him, so he just did not cooperate with us, and without cooperation, you just have no results. 

However, with all the defects, we still feel that the contributing editors have done a terrific job.

We are leaving records of a gigantic Jewish community, and we sincerely hope that the one who succeeds us will know where to continue – continue where we left off, and may he have better luck, more cooperation and better results. 

However, we say: ", Shhechyonu! It was a privilege and a pleasant, though a very hard, task. 

Thanks to all those who have tried very hard to help us. Without their help and cooperation, even this could not have been possible. 

JOSEPH L. MALAMUT, Technical Editor.
